# Welcome to Center Stage

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Mitra Sorrells, SVP, Content, Phocuswright; Pete Comeau, Managing Director, Phocuswright
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 9:00 AM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 9:15 AM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 1582
**Session ID:** `7cc61471-84aa-47cb-8137-c530f091d0c9`

## Summary

The session opened with event housekeeping and recognitions: organizers reaffirmed the carbon-offset partnership with Sustainable Travel International, reintroduced the GetPika photo tool, and welcomed nearly 500 first-time attendees alongside many veterans. The Philip C. Wolfe Scholarship recipient, Lola C. of Tripsetta, was recognized, and a new feature offering live transcripts and summaries of Center Stage sessions in six languages was announced. The newly published Hot 25 Travel Startups list for 2027, sponsored by Amex GBT, was highlighted, noting that one honoree, Odessia, had already been acquired by OpenAI the day after the list's release. The 25th anniversary of parent company Northstar Travel Group was marked, and three upcoming dates were flagged: the Travel Forward outlook publication on December 9th, the Travel Marketing AI Summit in New York on March 1st, and Phocuswright Europe's move to London in late May.

The program's framing theme—pressures, power, pivots—was introduced by pointing to a new generation of AI agents that have rapidly moved from concept to product and are reshaping the interface between travelers and travel companies. This was placed alongside other forces squeezing the industry: shifting consumer expectations, margin pressure, new competitors, regulatory scrutiny, and the difficulty of modernizing legacy systems while still running them, with Friday's FAA ground stop cited as a vivid example. These pressures were framed as shifting power over who controls discovery, who owns the traveler relationship, who accesses demand, and who holds leverage in the value chain—forcing companies into pivots about what to build, defend, or abandon.

Despite these pressures, Phocuswright's newly published Global Travel Market Report shows resilient demand, projecting global gross travel bookings will exceed $1.7 trillion by year-end and reach $1.9 trillion by 2029, with North America remaining the largest but slowest-growing market (3.5% through 2028) while Europe, Asia Pacific, and the Middle East grow near 5% annually and Latin America leads at nearly 10% from a smaller base. Yet strong demand coexists with rising traveler anxiety: research conducted for Protect Group found half of U.S. international travelers report feeling more anxious booking trips than two to three years ago, driven by worries over cost, disruption, and recourse when things go wrong. Internally, companies face their own pressure, with nearly half of surveyed travel firms citing lack of expertise or time—rather than cost—as the biggest barrier to implementing AI, a point underscored by an informal room poll on whether companies feel they are moving fast enough.

Traveler trust in AI is rising quickly: 41% of U.S. travelers trust AI results on travel sites, roughly matching trust in human reviews, with Gen Z and millennials even higher at about 56%. Among those familiar with AI, 43% say they would book inside a platform like ChatGPT or Google's AI mode, though trust drops sharply at checkout, with only 35% willing to pay inside an AI platform. This pattern echoed a nearly 30-year-old Phocuswright study of 'wired travelers' in the late 1990s, when the vast majority had never bought travel online and distrusted entering credit card details on websites, instead using the internet for research before booking through agents or airlines directly—concerns that faded as experience grew, eventually leading to about two-thirds of travel being booked online today. The parallel suggests AI may be in an early research-before-transaction phase similar to the web's early adoption curve.

A gap was identified between consumer readiness and industry readiness: while 43% of travelers say they'd book inside an AI platform, only 28% of surveyed companies are building or planning bookable inventory there, a divide described as potentially becoming a power gap rather than just a technology gap. Further data showed 45% of companies are scaling agentic AI but only 9% feel fully prepared, and startups are far more likely than established companies to have redesigned core workflows around AI (53% versus 18%). This set up the agenda ahead, including Steve Hafner's shift from building Kayak to building an AI assistant inside Booking Holdings, perspectives from Booking Holdings' Omri Morgenstern and Expedia Group's Alfonso Paredes on the growing power of B2B and behind-the-scenes sellers, and further Phocuswright Research on AI deployment and traveler use of AI alongside human advice. With nearly two-thirds of attendees at C-level or VP rank and representation from 36 countries, the session closed by framing the two days ahead as an opportunity to understand which pressures matter, where power is shifting, and which pivots are worth making.

## Key Takeaways

### 🔌 AI booking demand is outpacing industry readiness

Travelers familiar with AI are more ready to book inside AI platforms than travel companies are ready to make their inventory bookable there, creating a potential competitive and distribution gap.

Quotes:

> "43% say they'd book travel inside an AI platform, but only 28% of companies we surveyed are building or even planning to make their inventory bookable there."

### ⚙️ Agentic AI adoption is ahead of preparedness

Many travel companies are scaling agentic AI, but few consider themselves fully prepared, underscoring the need to build organizational capability alongside new tools.

Quotes:

> "45% of surveyed companies are scaling or have scaled agentic AI, yet just 9% say they are fully prepared."

### 📈 Travel demand remains resilient but regionally uneven

Global travel gross bookings are projected to exceed $1.7 trillion this year and reach $1.9 trillion by 2029, but growth varies substantially by region. Latin America is projected to grow fastest, while North America remains the largest and slowest-growing major market.

Quotes:

> Phocuswright projects global gross bookings will be more than $1.7 trillion by the end of the year and $1.9 trillion by the end of 2029. Latin America is projected to grow at nearly 10% annually, compared with 3.5% in North America.

### 🤝 B2B platforms are becoming trust infrastructure

The growing importance of B2B reflects a power shift in which companies increasingly enable trusted sellers behind the scenes, influencing how travel is distributed without always owning the consumer-facing relationship.

Quotes:

> "The growing importance of B2B and the companies increasingly power trust sellers behind the scenes."

### 🏗️ Modernization must confront legacy-system risk

Travel businesses must modernize while continuing to operate existing systems, creating disruption risks when new technology meets legacy infrastructure.

Quotes:

> "The industry is also dealing with ... the challenge of modernizing businesses while continuing to run them." The FAA ground stop was described as a dramatic example of what can happen when modernization meets legacy infrastructure.

### 🚀 Startups are redesigning around AI faster

Startups are more likely than established travel companies to redesign core workflows around AI, suggesting that organizational structure and legacy processes may affect the pace of adoption.

Quotes:

> "53% of startups strongly agree they've redesigned core workflows around AI, versus 18% of established companies."

### ⏳ AI adoption is constrained more by capacity than cost

For travel companies implementing AI, the leading obstacles are a lack of expertise or time; cost is a concern for a smaller share of respondents.

Quotes:

> When we asked travel companies about the biggest challenges in implementing AI, nearly half of respondents cited a lack of expertise or time. Cost was a concern for fewer than a third.

### 🔀 AI is shifting control across the travel value chain

As AI changes how travelers discover and purchase travel, companies must decide what to build, defend, or stop doing as control of discovery, traveler relationships, demand, and leverage moves.

Quotes:

> "Who controls discovery? Who owns the traveler relationship? Who gets access to demand? And who has leverage in the value chain?"

### 🤖 AI is becoming a trusted but incomplete travel interface

AI agents are moving from concept to product and becoming interfaces between travelers and travel companies. Travelers increasingly trust AI recommendations and are willing to book within AI platforms, but confidence falls when it comes to completing payment.

Quotes:

> "These tools are quickly becoming new interfaces between travelers and travel companies." Among U.S. travelers, 41% trust AI results on travel sites, and 43% of travelers familiar with AI say they would book inside an AI platform, but only 35% trust paying inside one.

### 😟 Travelers want trips but feel more exposed

Travel demand is strong, but travelers are more anxious about booking than they were a few years ago, especially regarding costs, disruptions, and what happens when something goes wrong.

Quotes:

> "Half of U.S. international travelers say they're more anxious when booking trips than they were 2 to 3 years ago."

# Executive Interview: What Comes After Search?

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Mitra Sorrells, SVP, Content, Phocuswright; Steve Hafner, Co-Founder & CEO, Lola (a Booking Holdings company)
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 9:15 AM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 9:35 AM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 3341
**Session ID:** `d866bb39-f799-4850-988c-0a8e235509f9`

## Summary

The session opened with a pitch for Lola, a new AI concierge built for experiences such as restaurants, concerts and sporting events, with travel as a secondary use case aimed at a local audience. It runs on a membership model akin to a credit-card perk program but without requiring a credit card, offering personalized recommendations and booking access, and had been live for only 30 days at the time of the conversation. Clips from past interviews were played showing earlier skepticism about starting a new travel company and about subscription models in travel, which were acknowledged with amusement, with the point made that travel is infrequent while going out locally is a much more frequent habit, making membership economics work better outside pure travel.

On operations, Lola was described as being built by a tiny 14-person team based at the Empire State Building, with a $100 million commitment from Booking Holdings directed not at overhead but at marketing to build trust and at the high inference and compute costs of running the product. Early success metrics being tracked include thumbs up or down product feedback, repeat usage, virality, and ultimately bookings, with an acknowledgment that no AI agent platform, Lola included, has yet cracked converting discovery into transactions, illustrated by an audience show of hands on using Muse or Instinct for travel bookings. Unlike horizontal agents relying on inefficient browser-based computer use, Lola was positioned as partnership-based, with direct API integrations with partners like GetYourGuide, Viator, Hertz, Clear, Blade and Duffel to ensure faster, more accurate and bookable experiences, a structure framed as a defensible differentiator against better-capitalized entrants such as OpenAI following its acquisition of Odesia.

Within Booking Holdings' broader portfolio, Lola was distinguished from the sibling initiative Veyo, with Lola focused on experiences and Veyo focused on owning the itinerary layer by ingesting bookings regardless of source and surfacing things like price-drop alerts; the two were described as friendly, non-competitive sister projects led by counterparts who talk regularly. The stated purpose of Lola within the group is less about rivaling legacy brands like Kayak or Orbitz and more about proving what a small, AI-first team can build quickly, including new features, new channels like WhatsApp or text, and a new tech stack that larger, legacy-constrained brands can later adopt. On why big incumbents struggle to move as fast, the discussion pointed to the complexity and real P&L risk of rebuilding legacy software and operations while still running the business, likened to rebuilding an airport while keeping it operational, versus Lola's low-risk experimentation with a small, pre-monetization user base.

Looking at the wider competitive landscape, it was argued that in an AI-agent-driven future, companies furthest from actual supply and content have the most to lose, while those controlling unique inventory, such as Marriott and Airbnb, are better positioned, with Booking Holdings seen as currently oversold on this perception. Small OTAs, ad networks and under-capitalized AI startups without strong brands were flagged as most at risk of being squeezed out. Among big tech players, Google was called the biggest travel company in the world for two decades despite not reporting it as a distinct vertical, and tipped to keep winning via products like Gemini; Meta's Muse was seen as a fun but likely money-losing product given inference costs vastly exceeding transaction value. A lean team building the biggest possible company was reiterated as the long-standing operating philosophy, extending from Kayak to Lola, with heavy use of automation such as AI-generated blog content and marketing replacing traditional hires.

Closing advice to entrepreneurs emphasized building a strong team, solving a genuinely worthwhile problem, raising ample capital as a hedge against being wrong, and forming durable partnerships given the industry's long memory. On what travel and hospitality brands need to do to stay visible and relevant, the key recommendation was to invest in loyalty strategy rather than continuing to pay for anonymous, one-time customer acquisition through channels like Google, redirecting that spend into direct-booking incentives following the playbook of credit card programs and brands like Marriott, while still allowing AI agents permissioned access and even charging them for it. A rapid-fire round closed the session, calling human travel agents irreplaceable, loyalty programs growing in importance, subscriptions niche but probably profitable, and travel startups too numerous, alongside quick, favorable remarks about industry figures Paul English and Glenn Fogel.

## Key Takeaways

### 📅 Frequent local outings make subscriptions more viable than travel alone

Lola focuses on activities people pursue regularly in their own city because travel happens only a few times per year and is less suitable for a monthly subscription.

Quotes:

> Travel is something most people do 3 or 4 times a year, right? So you can't really ask them to pay for it every month.

### 🏢 Incumbents face a high-risk modernization challenge

Established travel companies must modernize legacy software, teams and operating structures while continuing to run profitable businesses, making transformation slower and riskier than it is for a small startup like Lola.

Quotes:

> Imagine the complexity of rebuilding an airport at the same time you have to operate the airport. And that's what all these big brands have to do. And it's tremendously complex. If they make a mistake, it has real P&L implications.

### 🧪 A lean AI team can serve as an innovation test bed

With only 14 people, Lola is designed to demonstrate what a small, AI-first operation can build quickly, test new business models and features, and automate for the wider Booking Holdings group.

Quotes:

> My role within the group is to prove what's possible. Right? To demonstrate what you can do with a small team, what features you can build very quickly. ... We have a new tech stack, a new AI-first harness.

### ⚠️ AI may squeeze intermediaries without unique supply

As AI becomes a primary interface, small OTAs, ad networks and AI companies may be vulnerable if they lack distinctive inventory, sufficient capital or a trusted brand; companies closer to unique supply may be better positioned.

Quotes:

> I think the companies that have the most to lose are the ones that are furthest away from the content, from the gears. ... That's all these small OTAs, all these small ad networks, it's all these small AI companies who have built a product but still haven't raised enough capital to build a brand.

### 🛒 AI agents must turn discovery into transactions

A central challenge for Lola and other AI agents is changing consumer behavior so users complete bookings through the agent instead of switching to established booking platforms or contacting providers directly.

Quotes:

> Anyone who's in the AI agent space, whether you're a horizontal or vertical, is, how do you convert from being discovery and recommendations into a transaction?

### 🧭 Build for pivots and protect partnerships

Hafner’s startup advice is to build a strong team, solve a problem worth solving, raise enough capital to create room for pivots, and protect industry relationships because partnership networks and reputations endure.

Quotes:

> Build a great team, solve a problem worth solving, and go raise as much capital as you can, because you're probably going to be wrong about both your team and the problem you're going to solve. But with capital, you have the oxygen to pivot if you need to. And then the last part would just be: form partnerships. Don't burn the people you're doing partnerships with.

### 🔌 Direct APIs are Lola’s execution advantage

Lola is using direct, partnership-based API integrations with companies including GetYourGuide, Viator, Hertz, CLEAR and Blade to deliver faster, more accurate content and bookings than computer-use browsing.

Quotes:

> We wanted direct connections on an API basis. So that you have rapid responses. They're accurate. The content's broad. You can actually book through it. And it's really efficient.

### ❤️ Loyalty will matter more after search

Travel and hospitality companies should shift some customer-acquisition spending from anonymous, transaction-by-transaction search toward loyalty benefits that encourage customers to book directly and give the brand permission to serve them.

Quotes:

> This whole idea of acquiring consumers anonymously one transaction at a time through third parties like Google, it's going to be dated in a few years. ... Redirect some of that in terms of benefits to your members, to book directly with you.

### 🤖 Lola applies AI concierge technology to experiences

Lola is positioned as an AI concierge for restaurants, concerts, sporting events and travel, with its initial focus on helping local audiences discover and access things to do.

Quotes:

> It's all about being an AI concierge for experiences. So restaurants, concerts, sporting events, yes, we do travel as well, but it's really built for, like, local audience.

### 💰 AI products require funding beyond product development

For AI agents, scaling requires investment not only in product development but also in marketing that builds consumer trust and in the high inference and compute costs of delivering the experience.

Quotes:

> The $100 million commitment from Booking isn't spent on overhead. It's being spent on marketing awareness to build trust, and it's being spent on inference or compute cost because doing what we do is actually really expensive.

### 🧪 Lola is still testing for product-market fit

After its first 30 days, Lola is monitoring user experience feedback, repeat usage, sharing and bookings to determine whether the product is gaining traction.

Quotes:

> We're only 30 days into it, so we're looking for product-market fit right now. We're seeing when people are using the product, do they give us a thumbs up or thumbs down on the product experience signal? We're watching closely repeat rates. We're looking for virality. Are people sharing the product? And then the last thing that we're looking for is bookings.

### ⚙️ Lola targets the effort involved in going out

The central problem Lola seeks to solve is the friction of deciding what to do and arranging it, by combining personalized suggestions with access to the selected experience.

Quotes:

> We solved the problem of people want to go out and they want to go out with the least amount of effort required.

# Executive Panel: Hospitality at the New Distribution Crossroads

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Chris Wilroy, Executive Vice President & Chief Commercial Officer, Hilton; Chris Hemmeter, Managing Partner, Thayer Investment Partners; Kerry Mack, Chief Revenue Officer, Highgate Hotels; Lou Zameryka, Global Head - Hotel Enterprise and Connectivity Partnerships, Airbnb
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 9:35 AM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 10:00 AM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 4083
**Session ID:** `738871f5-1580-46da-ad69-79ec1bee4872`

## Summary

The panel opened with research framing showing a surge of consumer interest in AI and agents as travel-planning tools, but with booking volumes and trust still lagging behind the hype. This raised the long-running question of whether agentic AI will finally boost direct bookings or simply create a new, more fragmented surface area for intermediaries—echoing how both the internet and mobile eras were expected to favor direct booking but instead strengthened OTAs and increased distribution complexity and cost.

A hotel brand perspective laid out three “no-regret” investment areas meant to support more direct bookings regardless of how consumer behavior evolves: a modern, controllable technology stack that can plug into any emerging AI experience; granular, atomized inventory data (room attributes, exact locations, add-ons) so agents can match customer intent precisely; and active partnerships with AI platforms (Google AI mode, ChatGPT, Claude) plus a proprietary AI stay planner. Alongside this, owner-cost-reduction programs like RISE and the scale of the Honors loyalty program (270 million members) were presented as credible signals that increase an agent’s likelihood of recommending the brand, reinforcing trust and direct booking potential.

A marketplace perspective countered that leisure travelers rarely have strong brand loyalty and that a large-scale aggregator could in theory replicate much of what a major chain does, but across millions of properties, making scale and selection—not brand—the key competitive advantage; the view expressed was that “many middles will go away” while big brands and big aggregators both survive, leaving mid-sized intermediaries most exposed. This was tied to the aggregator’s strategy of onboarding boutique and branded hotels with competitive margins and aligned incentives, converting its large base of loyal, direct-booking travelers who have never stayed in a hotel into incremental demand for hotel partners, including via sub-distribution deals such as with American Express.

An owner/operator perspective, representing a mix of branded and independent properties, framed the marketplace relationship as an opportunity rather than a threat, since it converts a new generation of Airbnb-only travelers into hotel guests and helps surface unique, hard-to-sell inventory (one-off rooms, unusual layouts) that traditional OTA scroll formats bury. This depends on owners doing the backend work—correct attribution and structured content—so agents can discover and book these unique products; both the brand and platform speakers agreed that richer content and attributes let agents match guests to the “perfect” property rather than defaulting to generic price/location/review comparisons, potentially increasing willingness to pay. However, it was noted that better discovery does not guarantee a direct booking, since an agent may still route the booking through whichever channel or price point it finds most attractive, and price remains one of the strongest signals of intent in existing data.

## Key Takeaways

### 💰 Price remains a decisive test for agentic distribution

Even if agents improve matching and surface more distinctive hotel inventory, the benefits may be competed away when travelers prioritize price. The panel questioned whether agentic commerce will create incremental demand or simply redistribute the same stays toward the lowest-priced option, making owner economics and distribution margins critical.

Quotes:

> What we’ve seen in the data time and time again is one of the top elements of intent is price. And so how does that accrete or how does that show up in this new world?

### 🔄 Airbnb can bring incremental hotel demand

Airbnb argues that its hotel offering can deliver genuinely incremental guests: travelers who came to Airbnb intending to book an Airbnb may be converted to a hotel stay, creating value for participating hoteliers.

Quotes:

> Our guests... go direct, overwhelmingly direct, to the app. We're bringing an Airbnb guest through an Airbnb channel that went there originally to book an Airbnb, which has never meant a hotel until now.

### 🧱 Hilton’s first no-regrets priority is its technology foundation

Hilton’s initial investment area is its technology stack: a modern, cloud-based platform that the company controls and can evolve through its own development roadmap.

Quotes:

> Our technology platform... is modern, nimble, cloud-based, wholly under our control. We control the development roadmap. That includes PMS and CRS and everything around that.

### 📉 AI could lower guest-acquisition costs

Panelists expect total leisure guest-acquisition costs to be lower in five years, after the industry completes the current groundwork. The potential savings depend on better direct bookings, more efficient digital matching and distribution that remains profitable for owners.

Quotes:

> I think it’s a little more expensive right now as we’re laying the groundwork, but I think once you get the groundwork correct, it will lead to better direct bookings and lower overall costs.

### 🔎 Structured, granular content is essential for agentic discovery

Hotels need to atomize inventory and structure their content so agents can understand precise attributes, room locations, amenities, rates and distinctive property characteristics, then match them to a traveler’s intent. Availability alone is not enough if the property is not discoverable and bookable by the agent.

Quotes:

> It’s one thing to say this is great. I have a three-bedroom house... But have I done enough back-end work that it is discoverable and it is bookable?... We’re making substantial investments in our content and the structure of that content.

### ⚔️ AI may reward value-adding intermediaries and punish friction

The panel does not expect agentic distribution to eliminate intermediaries or guarantee direct bookings. Instead, AI could favor global brands and intermediaries that improve the customer or owner experience, while disadvantaging players that add friction, obscure choices or operate at uneconomic distribution costs.

Quotes:

> I think the future of AI distribution will be helpful for the big global brands and the intermediaries that are adding value to customers or to owners... What it will be unhelpful to is intermediaries that add friction or are deceptive or just create a problem in our ecosystem. I think AI will be an equalizer in that respect.

### 🔎 The key question is discovery versus integrated booking

The industry has not resolved whether AI agents will primarily provide a new surface for travel discovery or become an end-to-end booking tool integrated into commerce.

Quotes:

> There is some question about whether or not this is just a new surface area for discovery. Or is it really going to become an integrated booking tool?

### 🏅 Loyalty status could act as a trust signal to booking agents

Hilton believes its large Honors membership base can influence future agents: recognizing that a traveler is an Honors member may make the agent more likely to select Hilton.

Quotes:

> That sends a level of trust to an agent. It’s a highly credible signal to an agent. If that agent understands that you're an Honors member, that agent is going to book that brand, book a Hilton.

### 🌐 Hilton is connecting to multiple AI booking environments

Rather than waiting for one dominant AI interface, Hilton is working across major platforms and has launched an agentic stay-planning experience on its own direct site.

Quotes:

> We have a booking experience that allows shopping, booking, comparison in Google AI mode. We have a shop and book comparison tool in ChatGPT and in Claude... And then finally, we've launched an AI stay planner on our direct site.

### 🏗️ Scale may determine who wins in agentic distribution

Large platforms with broad supply can spread technology and merchandising investments across millions of properties and users. The panel suggested this may advantage scaled aggregators and make life harder for undifferentiated players in the middle.

Quotes:

> The ubiquitousness to be able to deliver that in many different places... with technology, could ultimately keep... a Hilton or a Marriott... doing well... But there’s a big world of others out there... Many middles will go away.

### 🤝 Better guest experiences protect owner economics

Regardless of how agentic distribution develops, delivering a strong on-property experience remains a durable competitive advantage. Hilton’s RISE program links lower owner program fees to achieving a specified stay-score threshold, aligning experience quality with owner profitability, loyalty and demand.

Quotes:

> We are... fueling the flywheel of owner profitability, customer experience, loyalty, and demand... RISE... is driving down a meaningful reduction in owner program fee if the owner is achieving a particular stay score threshold.

### 🤖 Agentic AI is attracting strong interest, but booking adoption lags

Travelers are showing substantial interest in AI as a planning tool and in agentic experiences, but that interest has not yet translated into comparable booking volumes. Trust remains a significant barrier.

Quotes:

> We're seeing a great deal of interest from the right kind of travelers. We're [not] seeing the booking volumes yet. And we're still seeing a pretty significant lag in trust.

## Idea Cloud

- Agents Control Discovery, Not Necessarily Booking (score: 366)
- AI Demand Must Improve Owner Economics (score: 364)
- Structured Attributes Make Hotels Agent-Ready (score: 362)
- AI Reopens Direct Distribution Debate (score: 353)
- Marketplace Scale Challenges Brand Control (score: 334)
- Trust And Loyalty Shape Agent Choice (score: 302)
- Owner Economics Fuel The Flywheel (score: 282)
- Investing Despite Fundamental Uncertainty (score: 227)

# Changemaker: Intrepid Travel (Sponsored by Intrepid Travel)

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Charlotte Lamp Davies, Founder, A Bright Approach; James Thornton, CEO, Intrepid Travel
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 10:05 AM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 10:15 AM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 1957
**Session ID:** `065e26cf-b2aa-4060-b02b-17a43d430f8c`

## Summary

The session opened with a framing of travel's biggest opportunity: most travelers can easily look up the best tapas restaurant in Barcelona, but what they really want is to shop at a local market and share a meal with a local family and new friends, an experience few businesses can actually deliver. Over more than 20 years, small-group adventure travel has shifted from a niche pursuit to something travelers increasingly demand, driven by a desire for adventure, human connection, memorable experiences, and confidence that their travel has a positive impact on the world.

Intrepid's growth was traced from carrying 47 people to Thailand in its first year of operation in 1989 to carrying more than 318,000 customers to over 118 countries last year, built on a vertically integrated model with its own offices in more than 35 countries, local tour leaders, and locally owned accommodation and restaurant partners that keep more tourism revenue in destination communities. Vietnam, where Intrepid opened its first country office in 2005 and now even operates its own hotel in Hoi An, was offered as an example of embedded local capability built over decades. The company is on track to surpass $1 billion in revenue next year, has tripled in size since before the pandemic, and posted its biggest booking month ever in October with 26% year-over-year growth in North America, a market seen as key to its 2030 targets.

Partnership was highlighted as central to scaling the Intrepid style of travel, with new collaborations bringing Intrepid trips to REI customers across North America and to Air Canada Vacations, pairing iconic consumer brands with Intrepid's local operational expertise. These partnerships were described as win-win: established brands gain a consistent, global, safety-vetted supply chain they couldn't build themselves, while Intrepid strengthens its ground network and contracting power. Travel advisors remain the majority distribution channel, valued for recommending immersive experiences and for wanting to align with trusted brands.

As a certified B Corp, Intrepid frames purpose and profit as mutually reinforcing rather than a cost: deeper investment in impact engages staff, engaged staff produce engaged customers (reflected in an NPS of 82), and engaged customers book more trips, generating returns that are reinvested into impact outcomes. This purpose focus was credited as a driver of the company's unusual growth rate for a 37-year-old business and as a magnet for attracting talented employees. An anecdote about a guide in Botswana's Chobe National Park thanking Intrepid for its role in Southern Africa's tourism ecosystem illustrated the company's emphasis on consistent presence and local supplier investment over sheer scale.

On technology, the discussion concluded that algorithms meant to connect people can instead drive polarization and loneliness, and that the industry's challenge is to combine its technology, distribution, and customer relationships with Intrepid's local teams and infrastructure to bring authentic experiences to more travelers. AI and automation were positioned as tools to speed up operations and resolve customer friction points and manual processes, while the human element—local guides and face-to-face connection—remains the irreplaceable core of the travel experience itself.

## Key Takeaways

### 💰 Local supply chains keep more tourism value in destinations

Working with locally owned accommodation, restaurants and businesses helps retain more of tourism’s economic benefits in the communities Intrepid visits.

Quotes:

> We work with locally owned accommodation, restaurants and businesses, and that allows us to keep more of the economic benefits of tourism with the places and the people that we visit.

### 🇺🇸 North America is central to Intrepid’s 2030 ambitions

Intrepid identifies North America as a key market for achieving its 2030 targets and reports that October was its strongest booking month in company history, with year-over-year growth of 26%.

Quotes:

> Now North America is key to us achieving our ambitious 2030 targets. And I'm really happy to report that October was the biggest booking month ever in our history. We grew 26% year over year.

### 🌱 Purpose is treated as a long-term commercial advantage

Intrepid says purpose and profit reinforce each other: investing in impact strengthens employee and customer engagement, supports its high NPS and repeat bookings, and ultimately contributes to financial returns.

Quotes:

> We don't see it as a short-term cost. We see it as a long-term strategic advantage. And I think one of the best things for us about having strong purpose and impact credentials is I attract way better people as a result.

### 🤝 Partnerships extend Intrepid’s global operating platform

Partnerships with established brands such as REI and Air Canada Vacations give them access to Intrepid’s globally consistent supply, local operations and health and safety protocols, while helping Intrepid expand its network and reach more customers.

Quotes:

> Established brands like REI as an example can come and contract with a business like Intrepid and we're able to provide a global solution to them that can add a great revenue stream to their business they wouldn't otherwise have the ability to deliver.

### 🏘️ Embedded local operations support scalable delivery

Intrepid’s model combines offices in more than 35 countries, local tour leaders, locally owned suppliers and company-operated trips to deliver destination experiences consistently at global scale—an infrastructure it says competitors cannot quickly replicate.

Quotes:

> It's a vertically integrated company with a global network of local expertise. At Intrepid, we have our own offices in more than 35 countries. We operate all of our own trips. Our tour leaders are locals.

### 🌍 Travelers increasingly seek local, human experiences

Many travelers want more than conventional sightseeing: they are seeking local markets, time in residents’ homes, new friendships, and experiences that create lasting human connections.

Quotes:

> People want adventure. They want human connection. They want experiences that stay with them long after they have returned from their traveling experiences.

### 🇻🇳 Vietnam shows the value of long-term local capability

Vietnam demonstrates how durable local teams and community relationships can strengthen a destination operation: Intrepid opened its first country office there in 2005, and many of the original team members remain.

Quotes:

> Many of the people that helped build that operation are still with us today. Bringing decades of local knowledge and community relationships.

### 🧑‍💼 Travel advisors remain a vital distribution channel

Despite the growth of technology and automation, Intrepid says the vast majority of its distribution still comes through travel advisors, who recommend immersive experiences and provide customers with trusted guidance.

Quotes:

> The key role of travel advisors for Intrepid is that still the vast majority of our distribution comes through travel advisors. They're the ones out there recommending our types of immersive experiential experiences. They want to work with a trusted brand.

### 🤖 AI can remove friction, but not replace human connection

Intrepid is applying technology and AI to manual processes and customer pain points so customers receive faster responses, while preserving in-person human connection as the core of on-the-ground delivery.

Quotes:

> We're implementing it to solve customer pain points and customer problems... And then the human component is incredibly important on the ground with delivery. People want that human connection.

### 🚀 Intrepid has paired purpose with substantial growth

After expanding from 47 travelers in its first year to more than 318,000 customers across over 118 countries last year, Intrepid says it has tripled in size since before the pandemic and is targeting more than $1 billion in revenue next year.

Quotes:

> Last year, Intrepid carried more than 318,000 customers to over 118 countries around the world... We're on track next year to do more than a billion dollars in revenue. We've tripled in size since before the pandemic.

## Idea Cloud

- Purpose And Profit Can Coexist (score: 373)
- Travelers Seek Immersive Connection (score: 372)
- Partnerships Expand Local Experiences (score: 362)
- Local Expertise Is the Advantage (score: 353)
- Locally Embedded Tourism Keeps Benefits Local (score: 347)
- Scaling Through Vertical Integration (score: 335)
- Intrepid’s Growth Ambitions (score: 235)

# Executive Panel: Competing for the Traveler Relationship

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Carey Kyler, Senior Director, Insights & Research, AARP Services; Jamie Nettles, VP Capital One Travel, Capital One; Jane Gasparian Lee, Head of B2B & International Marketing, United Airlines; Lorraine Sileo, Founder, Phocuswright Research and Senior Analyst, Phocuswright
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 10:15 AM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 10:40 AM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 4253
**Session ID:** `2490eb64-40ef-4630-9e85-c29297e245e3`

## Summary

The panel opened by framing a shared challenge: an airline, a credit-card travel business, and a membership organization for the 50+ community are all effectively selling travel to overlapping customers, raising questions about how each defines and targets its most valuable segment without excessive overlap. United described its most valuable customer as the loyal traveler who actively chooses the airline, acknowledging the definition is still evolving internally and may include travelers willing to take a connection to fly United, with segmentation built into cabin design from Polaris to Premium Plus to differentiated economy products like RelaxRow. AARP emphasized that its 50+ audience is not a monolith but a massive, $12.5 trillion economic group that will soon be the largest adult population segment in the US, requiring marketing by life stage rather than age, supported by data showing high digital device usage and rapidly doubling AI adoption among older travelers. Capital One Travel positioned its most valuable customer as the cardholder who uses card benefits and the travel portal together, noting these customers spend more, refer others, and are less likely to close their accounts.

Asked who 'wins' when a 55-year-old leisure traveler begins planning a European trip, United argued it does not need to be the starting point, noting it has no monopoly on inspiration and is happy to be found on TikTok, Expedia, or the Capital One portal as long as customers eventually convert. AARP said its edge lies less in being the starting point than in trust earned through decades of advocacy and vetting of partners, which gives members confidence that the whole trip, not just the booking, will go well. Capital One framed travel as a non-winner-take-all market, arguing that brand-loyal flyers will book direct while less loyal customers are drawn to its simplicity and flexibility across booking, lounges, and hotel collections, and that there is room for competitors to both compete and partner. A deeper dive into Capital One's strategy showed a significant share of Venture cardholders already using the card for travel even before the Discover acquisition, with the company explaining that owning its technology and agency end-to-end, including through the Hopper acquisition, lets it build trust, value, and simplicity across the full travel lifecycle rather than outsourcing to an OTA.

On AI agents booking directly into inventory, United confirmed it currently blocks this, citing concerns over customer data protection during an automated transaction rather than opposition to AI itself; it still welcomes AI-driven research and inspiration but wants a human in the loop for the actual purchase while it works out what it needs from partners to authorize specific agents safely. Capital One declined to comment on other companies' strategies but said account security is table stakes for a financial institution and that it is closely watching the trade-off between the ease AI agents offer and the trust issues they raise. It was noted that recent industry outages have not helped consumer confidence in automated booking, though comparisons were drawn to past hesitancy around entering credit cards online, suggesting trust in AI agents may build over time, while AARP added a cautionary note about AI's tendency to hallucinate even in research and planning contexts.

The conversation then turned to whether AI could help travelers optimize across competing loyalty programs and cards. AARP pushed back on the idea that consumers actively think about optimizing their loyalty portfolios, arguing instead that the real optimization consumers want is simply ease and savings, achieved through trusted relationships rather than point-juggling. United agreed but added a marketer's perspective, arguing that some AI-driven optimization use cases will emerge, but brands still need to cultivate an emotional connection that leads customers to choose them directly. Capital One suggested AI's broader role is to simplify complex decisions on a customer's behalf, predicting growth in customers asking AI agents how to best use their points and benefits.

The session closed on generational differences in loyalty drivers. United shared proprietary cross-generational research finding that reliable, free Wi-Fi was the one universal priority, which it tied to its Starlink investment as a modern, non-traditional loyalty lever. AARP offered a complementary view, noting that older generations respond more to company credibility, Gen X responds to word of mouth, and Gen Z cares about ethical corporate behavior, while stressing that inclusive design choices such as high-contrast, easy-to-read websites can welcome older users without alienating younger ones. Capital One closed by noting that its travel portal is actually seeing the most growth among younger, less brand-loyal Gen Z and millennial customers, who value the flexibility to earn and redeem value across many places rather than committing to a single airline or hotel brand.

## Key Takeaways

### 🛠️ Capital One is building end-to-end travel capabilities

Capital One is investing in its own technology and agency operations, including Hopper capabilities, to control the travel experience and create services tailored to cardholder needs—from booking through the trip.

Quotes:

> Fully owning our own technology end to end and owning our agency allowed us to provide them more value and build unique [experiences] for our cardholders' needs... We’re focused kind of holistically how we can make travel easier for our cardholders.

### 🧭 Consumers want loyalty made simple

Travelers generally are not trying to optimize every card, membership and portal in their wallet. The strongest proposition is to make it easy to save money, book confidently and receive relevant value, whether or not an AI agent eventually handles the optimization.

Quotes:

> I don't think people wake up thinking about all of their loyalty cards and how to optimize them... Any place I think where just make it easy for me to save money, make it easy for me to book. That's the optimization I believe for a consumer.

### 🔄 Generational change is reshaping the 50-plus audience

Travel marketers must prepare for a changing 50-plus customer base: Gen X is entering the segment now, and millennials will begin turning 50 within four years, bringing different planning, loyalty and travel behaviors.

Quotes:

> Gen X is now 46 to 61. And millennials will start turning 50 in four years. So sit on that for a minute, right? Very, very different practices and loyalty and planning and travel.

### 📱 Older travelers are highly digital

The assumption that older consumers are not digitally active is misleading: 90% of people over 50 have a cell phone, they use an average of seven digital devices, and AI usage doubled from 2024 to 2025.

Quotes:

> There's kind of this misnomer that older people aren't digitally active. And we know 90% of people over 50 have a cell phone. The average amount of digital devices... It's 7, right? ... in the last from 24 to 25, AI usage double.

### 👥 The 50-plus market is vast and diverse

The 50-plus audience spans a very wide age range and should not be treated as one homogeneous segment; it represents a $12.5 trillion economy and is on track to become the largest share of the U.S. adult population.

Quotes:

> The 50-plus is not one monolith of a consumer. And 50 to 100 is an absolutely enormous group of people... It's not a niche. It's the market.

### 🤝 Trust keeps human help relevant

AI is increasingly useful for travel planning, inspiration and research, but human assistance remains important when trips are complicated or require confidence and trust, such as cruises or multigenerational travel.

Quotes:

> While AI is gaining usage for planning and ideating, at the end of the day, trust sort of wins that relationship. And when things are complicated, maybe difficult to plan... that's where that in-person piece does come in.

### 🌐 Travel brands need an omnichannel presence

United does not expect to own the entire discovery journey; it aims to reach travelers wherever they seek inspiration or complete a purchase, including its own channels, Expedia and the Capital One portal.

Quotes:

> We don't have a monopoly on research and inspiration... But we want you to find our content wherever it is that you would like to make that purchase... through Expedia and, of course, through the Capital One portal as well.

### 🤝 Trust and vetting are AARP's relationship advantage

AARP uses the trust built through its broader advocacy and member work, together with vetting its commercial partners, to give members confidence that providers will support them throughout the trip—not just at booking.

Quotes:

> People who belong to AARP know that we're looking out for them and we're going to vet things and look at things so that they can have a good trip because it's not just about that first step... It's the whole thing.

### 🔐 AI booking needs safeguards

United is pausing direct AI-agent booking because agents may require access to sensitive customer data. The company is exploring standards and partner requirements that would make automated transactions safe while keeping humans in control for now.

Quotes:

> We absolutely still encourage the research and the inspiration and comparison... but it's just the booking part... We still want the human in the loop for that transaction.

### 📶 Practical benefits can drive loyalty

Loyalty is not limited to traditional brand attachment or points programs. Across generations, travelers may be won by practical benefits that consistently improve the journey—especially free, reliable Wi-Fi.

Quotes:

> The one thing that everyone cares about is good, free, reliable Wi-Fi. There are still levers to drive loyalty that may be in a non-traditional sense, but in the sense of delivering on what the traveler is looking for and what they value.

### 💳 Travel engagement strengthens card value

For Capital One, customers who use travel benefits and Capital One Travel are more valuable across the card relationship: they spend more, refer more friends and are less likely to close their cards.

Quotes:

> Customers that use the benefits of the card and use Capital One Travel, they tend to be much more valuable customers overall. They are higher spending. They're more likely to refer a friend to get a card. They're significantly less likely to close their card as well.

### 🎯 Segment broad markets by customer needs

Serving a broad population requires understanding customers' life stages, travel preferences and problems rather than relying on age alone, then developing the right offering for each segment.

Quotes:

> What we try to do is meet people where they are. And not understand and market to them by their age, but rather what life stage they're in, their chronology, what sorts of problems they're trying to solve for.

## Idea Cloud

- Trust Owns The Travel Relationship (score: 356)
- Winning The Loyal Traveler (score: 352)
- Travel Engagement Strengthens Card Value (score: 348)
- The 50-Plus Market Isn’t Monolithic (score: 346)
- Banks Build Their Own Travel Platforms (score: 329)
- Segmentation Follows Life Stage And Travel Needs (score: 329)
- AI Booking Needs Trust And Data Safeguards (score: 329)
- No Single Starting Point Wins (score: 308)

# Global Startup Pitch: Seedup People's Choice Winner

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Romy Abbrederis, CEO & Co-Founder, Lobby
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 11:10 AM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 11:15 AM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 742
**Session ID:** `f1271149-4c34-4511-ace3-dd967d376e16`

## Summary

The session opened with a pitch for Lobby, a startup built to help hotel sales and reservation teams handle complex, non-standard booking requests such as a wedding for 300 guests on half board with a split invoice and a contracted TUI rate. The pitch introduced Daniela, a fictionalized but real-inspired reservation manager who has worked at the same hotel for 18 years and holds all the institutional knowledge about contracts and exceptions, illustrating how operations break down when such staff are unavailable and how this problem recurs at every hotel Lobby onboards.

The core problem described is that despite the rise of OTAs, channel managers, and booking engines, a significant volume of bookings still arrive manually via email, PDFs, and tour operator portals, covering group requests, events, wholesale static contracts, and direct inquiries. This manual handling leads to low conversion, high operational costs, and error-prone processes, with skilled staff like Daniela spending most of their time on repetitive copy-and-paste work.

Lobby's solution consists of two modules: Automate, which converts incoming requests into actions like bookings, quotes, modifications, and cancellations, and Analyze, which checks existing reservations for errors and tracks team performance. The platform sits between communication channels (extranets, email, voice) and third-party systems of record such as PMS and CRM platforms, with new integrations taking days rather than weeks and onboarding typically completed within a day. Hotels retain control over what gets fully automated versus what requires human review before execution, and Lobby is designed to live directly inside existing inboxes (Outlook, Gmail, Zendesk) to minimize friction and switching costs, driving broader adoption. The stated benefits are faster responses, higher conversion, and improved efficiency, allowing portals to scale volume without added headcount and attracting more business from partners due to faster response times.

The team sees this operational problem as extending beyond hospitality into other travel verticals, and the underlying engine is being adapted to serve them even while the current focus remains on hotels. Commercially, Lobby is already working with multi-property setups, management companies, operators, and franchise networks across the Indian Ocean, Gulf region, Switzerland, and the Caribbean, and has white-labeled its technology for an attractions and experience platform. The company raised $2.2 million in pre-seed funding this year, with GetYourGuide co-founder Pascal Mathis joining the board.

The founding team's background includes four years building core banking software together before Lobby, with one founder as an engineer and another as a product owner, plus a third team member with Silicon Valley AI startup experience; advisors include the former CTO of Accor. The closing message grounded the company's mission in a personal story: Daniela was inspired by the real reservation manager of the presenter's family hotel, framing Lobby's purpose as making complex booking effortless, followed by an invitation for hotels, partners, or interested attendees to connect afterward.

## Key Takeaways

### 🧠 Hotel operations can depend too heavily on individual experts

Experienced employees may hold critical knowledge about contracts, exceptions and processes, creating operational disruption when they are unavailable.

Quotes:

> When she’s away, operations break down. And we meet a version of her at every hotel we onboard.

### ⚡ Fast integrations and onboarding reduce implementation friction

Lobby says new integrations can be delivered in days rather than weeks, with typical onboardings completed within a day, helping hotels adopt the platform quickly.

Quotes:

> With new integrations taking days and not weeks, and generally onboardings being done within a day.

### 📈 Faster responses may help hotels win more partner business

The claimed operational benefits include faster responses, higher conversion and improved efficiency; the speaker says faster replies are also prompting partners to allocate more business to hotels.

Quotes:

> Because they’re so fast in responding, partners are allocating more business to them as of now.

### 🏢 Current customers include multi-property hotel networks

Lobby is working with multi-property setups, management companies, operators and franchise networks across the Indian Ocean, Gulf region, Switzerland and the Caribbean.

Quotes:

> Today we are working with multi-property setups, management companies, operators, and franchise networks across the Indian Ocean, the Gulf region, Switzerland, and now also the Caribbeans.

### 🏨 Manual booking work remains widespread in hospitality

Despite substantial OTA, channel-manager and booking-engine volume, many hotel requests still arrive through email, PDFs, tour-operator portals and other non-automated channels.

Quotes:

> There’s a significant amount of volume that still comes in today through email, PDFs, tour operator portals, and more.

### ⚠️ Manual processes contribute to lost revenue and errors

The speaker links manual booking operations to low conversion, high operating costs and mistakes caused by repetitive copying and pasting.

Quotes:

> The result? Well, it’s low conversion, high operational costs, and mistakes can slip through because it’s manual.

### 🔎 Analyze checks reservations and team performance

The platform’s Analyze module reviews existing reservations for errors and tracks team performance, extending its role beyond initial request handling.

Quotes:

> Analyze where we’re checking existing reservations for errors and tracking teams’ performance.

### 🌍 The hospitality use case is expanding across travel

Although hotels are the current focus, the speaker says the underlying engine is being adapted to additional travel verticals and is already being explored beyond hotels.

Quotes:

> This problem is not only within hospitality, but it’s across the different travel verticals.

### 🤝 Human review can remain part of the automation workflow

Hotels can choose which processes to fully automate and which require a staff member to review before execution, enabling a configurable human-in-the-loop model.

Quotes:

> Hotels can decide what they want to fully automate, or where they would like to have a staff member first review it before anything gets executed.

### 📥 Inbox-native design aims to improve adoption

By operating inside Outlook, Gmail and Zendesk, Lobby seeks to reduce tool switching and embed automation in the places staff already work.

Quotes:

> By embedding Lobby exactly where the user already works, we remove the friction and also the switching costs and increase adoption.

# Executive Panel: Street Talk - Where the Smart Money Goes

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Gilad Berenstein, Founder, Brook Bay Capital; Jake Fuller, Financial Analyst, BTIG, LLC; Mark Mahaney, Senior Managing Director, Evercore
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 11:15 AM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 11:40 AM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 4486
**Session ID:** `8e57ae96-95f9-4f2a-92ec-1c3e87718fe2`

## Summary

The panel opened with each investor naming where their next dollar would go: one favored the distribution space, another named Amazon as a public-markets pick with a derivative read-through from Nvidia and travel IPO activity, while also flagging Airbnb and a 'dislocated high-quality' thesis on Booking.com given easier year-ahead comps and overstated agentic disintermediation risk. A fourth panelist said they wanted to back a platform providing identity for agentic commerce.

Asked what could go wrong, the panel converged on fears around a single dominant horizontal AI agent capturing most of the economic value and squeezing OTA advertising revenue and bookings share, which was framed as bad not just for the industry but for humanity broadly; another risk cited was losing the human element of hospitality as AI scales, with a caution that AI should support rather than replace human connection, while a third flagged consumer adoption patterns as the wildcard that could disrupt projected ripple effects across the industry. On readiness, there was agreement that average consumers aren't yet ready to trust AI with a several-thousand-dollar annual vacation purchase, but that this will change within a year or two as AI takes over discrete 'jobs to be done' like itinerary optimization rather than the entire trip.

On what not to fund, voice AI was singled out as a commodity lacking a defensible moat, while another panelist said they'd avoid any company failing to adopt agentic AI, drawing a parallel to the slow but inevitable shift of travel bookings online decades ago and warning that travel companies must become AI-native the way they once had to become internet companies. On where capital is actually flowing, panelists described near-universal AI-native sourcing, a pivot from loyalty, distribution and search toward labor-enhancing 'operations technology' aimed at absorbing hospitality labor tasks (with hoteliers still questioning near-term ROI), and a split focus on 'picks and shovels' for agentic commerce such as identity and content syndication, alongside non-AI-disruptable product categories like a differentiated travel insurance offering, with operational excellence underscored as the real driver of repeat travel.

On public markets, the view was that a travel IPO could succeed only with a genuinely differentiated, high-growth, AI-enabled value proposition, echoing Airbnb's 2020 debut, since public investors are starved for growth and chasing names like Anthropic. OpenAI's acquisition of Odessia split opinion: one panelist saw it as validating but ultimately a negative signal for independent travel-AI startups, expressing far greater concern about Meta's Muse, which is free, preloaded across billions of accounts, and avoids the cold-start problem; another argued Odessia validates travel's outsized share of LLM queries even without a travel backbone, drawing parallels to Google's earlier struggles integrating hotels versus flights. A show of hands indicated notable existing Muse usage, and one panelist voiced strong conviction that only two or three companies (Google, Meta, possibly OpenAI) have the scale and ad-funded economics to make mass-market AI travel planning work, eventually turning into customer acquisition channels that OTAs—strong in supply aggregation and channel optimization—could monetize. Another panelist pushed back that current tools like Muse and Instinct remain slow and unreliable for actual hotel search, attributing this to both early growing pains and immature plumbing, and a further nuance offered was that the future likely involves AI 'swarms' of specialized vertical agents working with horizontal ones rather than a single super-agent.

On whether incumbent OTAs can survive this shift, the consensus distinguished between the consumer-facing .com businesses, seen as structurally threatened because personal AI agents will know travelers better than any aggregator site, and the underlying B2B supply-aggregation businesses, viewed as far more durable and likely to persist as logos even as front-end power shifts toward whoever owns consumer context. Hotels were seen as having a genuine opening to reclaim distribution by leaning on the on-the-ground delivery of experience and personalization that resellers can only promise, turning context and direct relationships into the new loyalty currency. On the broader growth outlook, online travel was judged to have matured into a GDP-plus growth category, with continued OTA advantage resting on the deep fragmentation of global hotel supply and OTAs' rich historical travel data, which a closing lightning round reinforced: AI trip planners were called a 'nice feature' rather than a standalone business, opinions split on who wins the agentic race between startups, OTAs, LLMs and suppliers, and B2C trip planning was flagged as the most overfunded current travel theme.

## Key Takeaways

### 🧭 AI trip planners may be features, not standalone businesses

The panel sees near-term value in using AI for tedious travel optimization, such as complex itineraries, but the lightning-round consensus was that an AI trip planner alone is more likely to be a feature than a durable standalone business.

Quotes:

> “AI trip planner. Nice feature or real business?... Feature. It breaks my heart, but it's a feature.”

### 🌐 Scaled horizontal AI platforms could control travel’s top funnel

The panel sees Meta, Google and potentially OpenAI as uniquely positioned to make AI agents widely accessible through massive user bases and advertising-funded infrastructure. These agents could weaken OTA consumer brands while becoming important customer-acquisition channels for OTA supply.

Quotes:

> “The top of the funnel will be owned by the people with context... Meta and whatever Google comes out with and OpenAI, they'll eventually become customer acquisition channels.”

### 🏨 Operational excellence is a core travel differentiator

The panel argues that operations are not merely a back-office concern: execution quality determines whether travelers want to repeat an experience. This makes operational improvement an important investment area even when it appears less exciting than consumer-facing technology.

Quotes:

> “Operational excellence is the difference between a trip you want to go on again and a trip you don't want to go on again.”

### 🏗️ OTA consumer brands and B2B platforms face different risks

The panel separates an OTA’s consumer-facing website from its underlying B2B inventory aggregation and booking infrastructure. AI agents may make brands such as Expedia.com and Booking.com less important for recommendations, while still relying on their supply, connectivity and booking capabilities.

Quotes:

> “The OTA has two large businesses within... There is a dot-com consumer aggregator business... And there is a B2B inventory aggregation business below it. I actually think that the dot-com business is in a pretty bad place. I think the aggregation business [is] in a much stronger place.”

### 🏨 Hotels can use agentic distribution to reclaim customer context

The panel sees agentic commerce as a once-in-a-generation opportunity for hotels and other suppliers to build direct relationships around customer context and personalization. Suppliers still have the advantage in delivering the actual on-the-ground experience, even if intermediaries promise it.

Quotes:

> “Context is becoming increasingly more relevant. That's the new way of loyalty... There's an opportunity to gain more information and context.”

### 🛠️ Invest in agentic picks-and-shovels and AI-resilient businesses

Rather than trying to predict the eventual shape of agentic distribution, investors can back enabling infrastructure such as identity and content syndication, alongside businesses whose core value is not itself easily disrupted by AI. These positions may remain relevant regardless of which platforms ultimately win.

Quotes:

> “The two things I'm investing in are picks and shovels for agentic commerce... And then I'm investing in things that AI cannot disrupt, or AI is not the core of it.”

### 📊 Online travel is mature but still has GDP-plus growth potential

The panel views online travel as a mature category rather than an exhausted one, expecting GDP-plus growth over time. Fragmented lodging markets, particularly outside the United States, and OTAs’ accumulated traveler data remain structural sources of opportunity.

Quotes:

> “Online travel is clearly matured. You probably have GDP-plus growth for the category... just how freaking fragmented the hotel market is.”

### 🚀 Travel companies must build agentic functionality

The panel compares agentic adoption with the earlier shift to online booking: companies that fail to incorporate agentic functionality into their apps risk becoming structurally disadvantaged as consumer behavior changes.

Quotes:

> “You have to adopt it. You have to have that functionality on your app as part of your business. If you don't, I wouldn't invest in them.”

### 🎙️ Voice AI needs a moat beyond the commodity layer

The panel’s strongest avoid recommendation was voice AI as a standalone investment theme: basic voice capabilities are widely available, so companies need a differentiated moat beyond the interface itself.

Quotes:

> “Voice AI... is a commodity. Anyone can build with voice AI. Find a different moat.”

### 🐝 AI swarms may outperform a single super-agent

Rather than relying on one agent to handle every travel task, the panel expects groups of specialized agents to work together. A general-purpose agent could coordinate vertical travel agents with deeper domain knowledge and infrastructure.

Quotes:

> “I'm a big believer in AI swarms. Groups of AI agents that come together to accomplish overall things.”

# The Bridge Series: Travel Tech Trends Around the World

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Brigit Zimmerman, CEO, Priceline; Siew Hoon Yeoh, Founder, WIT and Editorial Director, Northstar Travel Group Asia; Filip Filipov, CEO, OAG; Gonzalo García Estebarena, CEO, Despegar; Mohit Kabra, Group Chief Operating Officer, MakeMyTrip; Pete Comeau, Managing Director, Phocuswright
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 11:45 AM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 12:15 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 5281
**Session ID:** `34da7834-3c0c-42bd-8c47-512c1ed14420`

## Summary

The panel brought together three of the world's largest OTAs—Despegar (Gonzalo), MakeMyTrip (Mohit) and Priceline (Brigit)—alongside OAG, which supplies flight data across the industry and acted as a neutral referee for the discussion. The conversation was structured around Phocuswright's 2026 themes of pressure, power and pivot, with audience polling via Slido and smiley/frowny cards used throughout to test agreement.

On pressure, the audience voted that AI assistants and agents represented the greatest threat to travel distribution, echoing a similar result from a prior WIT survey. Panelists, however, described different regional pressures: Priceline pointed to US affordability concerns driven by rising supplier costs and historically low consumer sentiment despite continued demand for travel; Despegar cited the sheer pace of change and acceleration, noting its AI agent already drives 8% of sales; MakeMyTrip highlighted pricing pressure on airlines tied to oil-price shocks from the West Asia crisis, offset by strong non-air growth (now two-thirds of its mix) in buses, cabs and bundled holidays; and OAG framed the overall picture as cyclical economic volatility compounded by overhyped expectations around agentic AI, arguing travel agencies won't simply disappear. OAG's capacity data showed flat or declining growth in markets like Brazil's domestic sector, steady conditions in North America, and strong double-digit international growth in India.

Discussion of OTA market share showed Phocuswright forecasting a dip to 47% of the online market in Latin America by 2028, which Despegar framed as healthy industry democratization and competitive growth rather than a threat, a view the audience largely endorsed via card vote. MakeMyTrip acknowledged air ticketing volumes are under pressure from capacity constraints, airline exits and the oil crisis, but said resilient growth of 20%+ is coming from non-air transport and bundled accommodation offers suited to different budgets.

On power, the audience again pointed to AI assistants and super apps as likely owners of the traveler relationship in five years. Priceline said it is investing both in third-party integrations (ChatGPT, Snapchat) and in its own AI assistant, Penny, insisting OTAs must be wherever customers are, while the audience voted confidently that US OTAs can still win in air. Capital and ownership questions surfaced around Prosus's stakes in both Despegar and MakeMyTrip rival Ixigo; Despegar said shared AI investment across Prosus portfolio companies does not involve sharing customer data, while MakeMyTrip noted its long public-market history has made capital access a non-issue, with competitive advantage resting on execution, supply depth and customer experience. OAG argued that whoever sits in the transaction—not necessarily the AI agent—holds the real power, and that agencies will likely survive AI disruption much as they survived prior shifts to Google and Meta.

## Key Takeaways

### 🌍 More competition could grow travel while reshaping OTA share

Low-cost carriers and more competitive distribution could make travel more affordable and expand the overall market, even if OTAs lose some booking share. The panel framed that as potentially positive for efficient distributors.

Quotes:

> For the industry it means democratizing travel, making it more affordable, and when travel grows actually it's good for us.

### 💳 The transaction intermediary may still own the traveler relationship

Despite predictions that AI assistants will control the customer relationship, the panel argued that whoever sits in the middle of the transaction—especially where the traveler pays—may retain that relationship. In either case, price, supply depth and the ability to combine experiences remain core competitive advantages.

Quotes:

> Anyone who is in the middle of the transaction where you put your money owns the relationship with the customer—and that happens to be the agencies at this stage.

### 📈 Latin America’s growth is accelerating OTA execution

Latin America was described as the fastest-growing region, with Despegar gaining market share and responding by launching products faster as customer and technology expectations change.

Quotes:

> We are gaining market share within that context. We are launching a lot of new things, shipping products faster than ever before.

### 🧠 Cross-category commerce models can power connected trips

Prosus is investing in a large commerce model trained on transactions across its portfolio, not just travel. By predicting a customer’s next action and transferring insights across categories, the model could help OTAs connect flights, hotels and other trip components in a more natural journey.

Quotes:

> It's basically trained on billions of transactions around all of the different portfolio companies, not only travel. And basically what it does is exactly predict the next action that someone will take in the path to eventually buying something.

### 🇮🇳 MakeMyTrip shifts growth beyond air

Air-ticketing growth in India is under pressure from capacity constraints and higher fuel costs, so MakeMyTrip is growing non-air businesses such as buses and outstation cabs, while bundling transport with accommodation for different budgets.

Quotes:

> A lot of the growth is coming in from non-air... bus ticketing with outstation cabs... What we're trying to do is bundling them up with right kind of accommodation options that can work for people.

### 🇮🇳 India leads international capacity growth

Flight-capacity data pointed to India as the standout growth market, with nearly double-digit international capacity growth and increasing low-cost-carrier capacity; Brazil’s domestic capacity was declining while North America remained broadly stable.

Quotes:

> The good story here is India. India is increasing capacity. It's growing... You can see almost double digit growth in terms of international.

### 🤖 AI agents could be either a channel or a travel product

The panel’s central strategic question is whether AI agents will merely distribute existing travel products or become products that combine experiences and reshape how trips are bought. Agencies may remain resilient if agents need deep supply, pricing and itinerary-combination capabilities.

Quotes:

> The bigger question for us to answer... is the AI agent a distribution channel or a product? If it is a product then the agencies will thrive because they've been doing it for many years.

### 💰 Affordability and geopolitical costs pressure travel

The panel described an unaffordability crisis driven by supplier operating costs, inflation, geopolitics and fuel prices. Consumers still want to travel, but discretionary spending is harder to sustain.

Quotes:

> I think the industry is experiencing a little bit of an unaffordability crisis. This is driven by geopolitics and like the cost base of a lot of the suppliers that we actually use in the industry.

### 🤖 AI agents already generate 8% of Despegar sales

Although the panel debated the pace and significance of agentic AI, Despegar reported that an AI agent already represents 8% of its sales, showing measurable commercial impact today.

Quotes:

> Today we have an AI agent that represents already 8% of our sales.

### 💬 Conversational agents must become richer than chat

OTA assistants such as Priceline’s Penny are being built to understand traveler intent and interact with other agents, moving beyond static APIs toward personalized recommendations. The panel also stressed that travel discovery needs visual and inspirational elements, not just a text-chat interface. Adoption remains early, with Penny still scaling.

Quotes:

> Please let's not have it just as a chat. I mean it needs to be a little bit more inspirational. Like we still need to see the hotel, the beach, the country and get inspired.

### 🔗 OTAs must meet travelers—and their agents—where they are

OTAs are investing in their own products while integrating with major LLMs and third-party platforms. They are also preparing to serve AI agents as customers, not just human travelers, so they can remain part of the transaction wherever it begins.

Quotes:

> We want to be wherever our customers are... that means with third parties, but that also means we want to serve whatever customer comes to us. So human customers, agents as customers.

## Idea Cloud

- AI Agents Reshape Distribution (score: 380)
- The Traveler Relationship Is Up for Grabs (score: 365)
- Affordability Shapes Travel Distribution (score: 331)
- Travel Demand Pivots Under Pressure (score: 327)
- OTAs Defend Their Distribution Role (score: 321)
- India Emerges as a Growth Engine (score: 298)
- Travel’s Supply and Capacity Constraints (score: 260)
- Commerce Data Enables Cross-Category Growth (score: 255)
