# Changemaker: Wenrix (Sponsored by Wenrix)

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Tor Leelo, CPO and Co-Founder, Wenrix; Robert Cole, Senior Research Analyst, Lodging and Leisure Travel, Phocuswright Inc.
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 12:15 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 12:25 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 895
**Session ID:** `9fcb834f-fda5-4b75-824b-83caf6c5b552`

## Summary

The session opened by framing how AI assistants are changing travel search and increasingly enabling bookings, pushing boards to ask what companies are doing to be ready. Connecting to these channels via MCPs was described as the right first step, but the deeper, harder work comes after that connection is made: ensuring unit economic strategy, differentiation, and infrastructure are truly ready for AI channels, since flows for search, booking, and servicing will all change even as metasearch and direct channels continue to drive significant volume.

On search, AI assistants let travelers ask much more complex and detailed queries beyond simply the lowest price, including refund and change fees, branded fare options, and loyalty benefits. Delivering this richer information earlier in the search phase increases calls to suppliers, straining unit economics and look-to-book ratios, and agencies that fail to provide this detail risk becoming less attractive to both travelers and AI assistants. Traditional methods relying on live supplier calls or search-time caching struggle to deliver the needed coverage, accuracy, and detail, so the proposed alternative is prediction at search: combining airline policy intelligence with historical data to predict structured fare rules and branded fare pricing in advance, achieving higher coverage, strong accuracy, and lower cost than organic search-time lookups.

On booking, AI assistants threaten to disrupt the traditional OTA funnel model that relies on upsells and cross-sells, since a traveler could book a flight with one OTA via an AI assistant and then be referred elsewhere for hotel, car, or insurance. Until new monetization avenues emerge, the economics of the flight booking itself become far more critical. The suggested response is a deeper layer of profitability intelligence that makes forecasting more granular, helping set prices that convert while remaining profitable, alongside differentiating the offer beyond price—such as enabling a bundled fare with 96-hour cancel-for-any-reason coverage to help partners stand out.

On servicing, as AI makes search and booking faster and easier, servicing may become the main point where travelers actually interact with an agency's brand, making fast, frictionless resolution a key differentiator for loyalty and for being recommended by AI assistants. The core problem is that travel infrastructure remains broken: roughly 60% of servicing tasks still depend on costly manual work, with the hardest cases involving unstructured fare rules, virtual interlining, multiple passengers and forms of payment, and waivers across channels like NDC, LCC, BSP Link, and ACH/ADM disputes. The bottleneck is automated execution rather than conversation, and the described approach—developed over nearly a decade by going deep rather than wide, solving edge case after edge case with a zero-human-in-the-loop mindset and learning from real servicing data—aims to close that execution gap.

The session concluded that while the pace and scale of AI assistants reshaping travel remain uncertain, agencies need to start preparing now, and that the work done to support AI assistants—improving search technology, refining pricing, differentiating beyond price, and automating servicing—will strengthen flight strategy and infrastructure more broadly, making flights a stronger growth driver regardless of where a traveler's journey begins. The closing exchange reiterated that servicing automation must go beyond handling the simple, common cases to address the full range of complex scenarios.

## Key Takeaways

### 🔌 MCP connectivity is only the starting point

Connecting to AI channels through MCPs is described as an important first step, but agencies must also ensure their unit economics, differentiation, and infrastructure are ready for these channels.

Quotes:

> It's not only about connecting MCPs. It goes much deeper.

### 🏗️ Backend automation must resolve servicing edge cases

Wenrix describes a backend-focused approach to closing the servicing execution gap by tackling complex cases one by one and aiming for zero human intervention. Examples include unstructured fare rules, virtual interlining, multiple passengers or payment methods, and waivers across fragmented channels; around 60% of servicing work still depends heavily on costly manual effort.

Quotes:

> The bottleneck isn't conversation, it's automated execution... solving edge case after edge case with the mindset of zero human in the loop.

### ⚖️ Richer search data creates a unit-economic tradeoff

Surfacing detailed fare and policy information earlier in the search journey can require more supplier calls, increasing costs and putting pressure on unit economics and look-to-book ratios. Agencies must balance that burden against the competitive need to provide clearer information.

Quotes:

> Providing this information earlier in the search phase means more calls to the suppliers, creating an extra burden on unit economic and look-to-book ratios.

### ✨ Differentiated offers can compete beyond price

Agencies can stand out in AI-mediated booking by adding meaningful product value rather than competing only on fare price, such as a bundled fare with 96-hour cancel-for-any-reason protection.

Quotes:

> We enable our partners to offer a bundled fare that includes 96 hours cancel for any reason in order for them to really stand out.

### 🧠 Prediction at search can enrich fare intelligence

Wenrix says agencies can combine airline policy intelligence with historical data to predict structured fare rules and higher branded-fare prices during search. This is intended to provide broader coverage and high accuracy at lower cost than relying only on organic supplier searches or caching.

Quotes:

> Combining airline policy intelligence together with historical data, we predict already at search detailed and structured fare rules and prices of higher branded fares.

### 🔀 AI assistants may split the OTA cross-sell journey

A traveler could book a flight through one OTA and then be referred by the AI assistant to different suppliers for hotels, car rental, or insurance. This threatens the cross-sell assumptions behind traditional OTA funnel economics.

Quotes:

> A traveler may book a flight through an AI assistant with one OTA and then be referred to another supplier for the hotel car rental or insurance.

### 🛎️ Servicing may become the key brand interaction

As AI makes search and booking easier and faster, servicing could become the main stage where travelers directly experience an agency's brand. Fast, frictionless resolution can help agencies build loyalty and earn recommendations.

Quotes:

> As search and book become easier and faster with AI, servicing may be the only place your traveler really interacts with your brand.

### 🧭 AI preparation can strengthen the broader flight strategy

The work required for AI assistants can deliver benefits beyond those channels: better search technology, more refined pricing, stronger differentiation beyond price, and automated servicing can make flights a stronger growth driver wherever the traveler journey begins.

Quotes:

> The work that you will do for AI assistants will help you improve search technology, refine your pricing, stand out beyond price alone, and finally automate servicing.

### 🔎 AI search requires richer, clearer travel information

AI-assisted travelers ask broader questions than which itinerary is cheapest. Agencies increasingly need to surface refund and change fees, branded fare options, loyalty benefits, and other structured details early in search; failing to provide accurate, useful information can make them less attractive to travelers and AI assistants.

Quotes:

> Travelers now look for much more information when they search: refund and change fees, branded fare options, loyalty benefits, and more.

### 💰 Flight-level profitability becomes more important

If cross-sell and upsell opportunities weaken and replacement monetization has not yet emerged, the economics of the flight itself become more consequential. More granular profitability forecasting can help set offers that convert while remaining profitable.

Quotes:

> Until new monetization opportunities emerge the economics of the flight itself become much more important.

### 📊 Servicing data can improve automation

Wenrix says real servicing data— including airline policies and agents' actions—can help agencies understand how rules are applied and improve automated servicing, particularly across complex workflows.

Quotes:

> Our approach is to rely on servicing data, real servicing data, to understand better the airline policies and agents' actions.

## Idea Cloud

- Automated Servicing Must Handle Edge Cases (score: 364)
- AI Travel Channels Require Broader Readiness (score: 342)
- Servicing As The Brand Touchpoint (score: 333)
- Predicting Fare Details At Search (score: 328)
- Richer Information In AI Search (score: 326)
- AI Disruption To OTA Economics (score: 314)
- Granular Flight Profitability Forecasting (score: 301)
- Differentiating Offers Beyond Price (score: 255)
- Metasearch And Direct Remain Important (score: 75)

## Summary

The session opened with an informal audience poll about whether an AI tool had helped attendees plan or book travel to the conference, with a show of colored cards indicating roughly half had used AI assistance. This led into anecdotes about the presenters' own journeys: one had used an AI tool to find a route from Singapore via Dubai to Miami but ultimately booked with a human agent out of a wish to support people in the industry, and recounted that Dubai airport at 1 AM was still packed with travelers buying dates, chocolate, and gold, suggesting that despite rapid change in travel technology, some human behaviors remain constant.

The discussion turned to how the travel startup landscape has shifted since the stage was known as the Travel Innovation Summit 18 years ago, noting that some of the sharpest new companies now come from growth markets in Asia, Latin America, the Middle East and Africa, where small teams can build and globally launch products quickly. It was emphasized that access to Silicon Valley funding is no longer the key competitive advantage; speed and customer insight now matter more.

This context framed the announcement of the Global Startup Pitch, a program run jointly by WIT, Phocuswright and Phocuswire and supported by American Express GBT, now in its fifth year with close to 1,000 entrepreneurs having applied globally. The program's first winner, Wheel the World, was cited as a success story, having since raised $19 million and demonstrated the scale of opportunity in accessible travel. The call for entries for the sixth edition, the Global Startup Pitch 2027, was formally opened, with attendees encouraged to share the program's QR code with founders who might belong on the stage.

Historical performance data for the Launch stage was shared to establish its credibility: since 2008, 682 companies have pitched, 527 of them (more than three in four) have gone on to raise funding totaling $14.7 billion, and 150 have been acquired, representing more than one in five presenters. It was noted that some of the acquirers of past presenters were in the room, and that the newly released Hot 25 Travel Startups list, sponsored by American Express GBT, already shows Odesia tagged as acquired following OpenAI's announced acquisition last week. Two companies from that Hot 25 list, Definitiv and Pintours, were flagged as presenting on the Launch stage that day, inviting the audience to judge whether the editors' picks proved accurate.

The format for Launch, presented by Spotnana, was then explained: companies of any size get six minutes to show a new product and must answer the single question of why now, after which a focus group of sales, strategy, investment and technology executives questions each presenter and rates how likely the product is to succeed. The audience was instructed to vote using smiley or frowny cards after each pitch and to cast a people's choice vote through the conference app, with three awards to be given — a focus group choice winner and runner-up, and a people's choice winner. The focus group panel was introduced, including representatives from Madrona Ventures and Spotnana, Perk, and American Express GBT, before the first presenter, from Fareportal, was welcomed to the stage.

## Key Takeaways

### 🌍 Growth markets are producing globally competitive travel startups

Some of the strongest travel startup work is coming from growth markets across Asia, Latin America, the Middle East, and Africa—not only from established innovation centers.

Quotes:

> Some of the sharpest work we see comes from the growth markets across Asia and Latin America, the Middle East, and Africa.

### 🚀 Applications are open for the 2027 Global Startup Pitch

The call for entries to the sixth Global Startup Pitch is open, inviting founders and people connected to promising founders to apply.

Quotes:

> We have opened our call for entries to the 6th Global Startup Pitch 2027.

### 🏆 The Hot 25 list is surfacing startups with major strategic potential

The organizers’ latest Hot 25 Travel Startups list includes Odesia, whose strategic significance was underscored by OpenAI’s announced acquisition, and two listed companies are presenting in Launch.

Quotes:

> Odesia is already tagged acquired as OpenAI announced their acquisition of Odesia last week.

### ♿ Accessible travel is a substantial, validated startup opportunity

Wheel the World, the program’s first winner, has demonstrated the scale of the accessible-travel opportunity and reportedly raised $19 million since participating.

Quotes:

> They've shown this industry really how big the opportunity is in accessible travel.

### ⚡ Small teams can now reach global markets at unprecedented speed

A startup based in a city such as Bangkok, Buenos Aires, Riyadh, or Lagos can build a product in weeks and sell it worldwide almost immediately, dramatically shortening the path from idea to market.

Quotes:

> The small team in Bangkok, or in Buenos Aires, or Riyadh, or Lagos, can build a product in weeks, sell it to the world in a day, and it's never been faster to go to market.

### 📈 Pitch-stage startups often go on to raise funding or get acquired

The organizers report that 527 of 682 companies that pitched on their stages have raised money, for a combined $14.7 billion, while 150 were later acquired—evidence that pitching can surface companies with substantial market potential.

Quotes:

> Since 2008, 682 companies have pitched on our stages. 527 of them have raised money... for a combined 14.7 billion dollars... 150... have been acquired.

### 🗳️ Audience voting adds a direct customer signal to startup evaluation

Alongside the expert panel’s assessment, attendees can indicate whether they would buy each product and vote for a People's Choice winner.

Quotes:

> After each pitch, hold up a smiley face if you would buy it, or a frowny face if you won't.

### 🧭 Industry buyers and investors pressure-test each startup’s proposition

A panel of sales, strategy, investment, technology and hospitality leaders evaluates each product and asks the questions a potential buyer would ask, giving presenters a practical market test.

Quotes:

> They'll tell each presenter how likely the product is to win, and ask the questions a buyer would ask.

### 🎯 Customer knowledge and speed are replacing funding access as moats

Access to Silicon Valley funding is no longer viewed as a lasting competitive advantage; execution speed and a deep understanding of customers matter more.

Quotes:

> Access to Silicon Valley funding is no longer a moat. Speed and knowing your customers are.

### ⏱️ Launch pitches must answer “why now?” in six minutes

Each Launch presenter has six minutes to introduce one new product and make a concise case for why the market needs it now.

Quotes:

> Companies of any size show one new product in 6 minutes. Each one has to answer a single question: why now?

# Letsfly

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Chelsea Salamone, Principal, Thayer Investment Partners; Ellen Keszler, CEO, Clear Sky Associates; Erik Blachford, Founder, Pine5 Ventures LLC; Kristina Carter, VP, Global Partnerships, Perk; Kurien Jacob, Partner, Highgate Technology Ventures; Michael Berk, Director of Strategic Innovation and Partnerships, American Express Global Business Travel; Pete Comeau, Managing Director, Phocuswright; Steve Singh, Executive Chairman and CEO, Spotnana; Traci Mercer, Chief Commercial Officer, Priceline; Roger Sharp, Chairman, WebBeds Group and North Ridge Partners; Reno Wang, SVP, Global Commercial, Letsfly Limited
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 1:39 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 1:48 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 2642
**Session ID:** `4ecd425b-7aa1-4878-a1db-a4482a57701f`

## Summary

The session opened with a pitch from Fareportal, the company behind CheapOair and OneTravel, introducing Fareportal Travel OS, a white-label operating system for brands to sell and distribute travel. The pitch framed every purchase as tied to an emotion and a destination, noting that most brands lack their own travel portal even though a handful of banks like Chase already generate billions in travel bookings, leaving a large underserved opportunity for other brands to capture loyalty and revenue by offering branded travel booking directly to their customers.

The platform was described as a multi-sided system with a Partner OS for launching branded travel businesses via point-and-click configuration, a Traveler OS delivering a branded customer experience including automated rebooking when flights are cancelled, and a Distribution OS letting suppliers push targeted offers (such as discounts or bonus points) directly through partner brands. A live demo showed a customer site, goislandbound.com, being configured and launched, as well as a bank-branded travel portal being stood up through a short AI-driven setup process, allowing customers to redeem points for trips. The commercial model was presented as bespoke rather than boxed, with revenue share and commissions tailored to each partner's goals, paired with a 24/7 branded concierge service built on Fareportal's 25 years of servicing experience.

Audience and panel reaction was largely positive, though questions pressed on differentiation and go-to-market focus. In response to questions about funding needs and initial priorities, the first focus was identified as the Partner OS enabling partners to go live quickly. Asked what makes the offering truly innovative versus a souped-up affiliate program, the answer emphasized that unlike affiliate links sending customers off-site, this keeps customers inside the brand's own branded ecosystem, building loyalty and capturing margin on high-value, emotionally charged purchases. On differentiation from other companies building similar solutions, the response again stressed the bespoke, partner-specific approach combined with end-to-end servicing at scale, backed by a dedicated customer success manager, with one large new customer confirmed to be launching by the end of November.

The second pitch came from Letsfly, presenting Postra, a post-booking automation solution aimed at OTAs. The pitch argued that while AI is automating more travel operations, a confirmed booking is not the end of the customer journey, since travelers still need help with changes, cancellations, refunds and ancillaries, and that delivering this servicing well is hard because airlines have different products, rules and fragmented booking channels requiring expert judgment. Postra combines automation with an expert team that prioritizes urgent cases (such as imminent departures), feeding resolved-case data back into the AI engine to continuously improve automation coverage. It supports over 100 airlines across Asia, Europe and the Americas, connects through GDS, NDC, airline direct-connect APIs and aggregation channels, offers a white-label option for travel sellers, and combines servicing with ancillary sales such as seats, bags and meals. A pilot client, a European OTA, reportedly saw operating costs cut by around 70% and servicing success rates rise from 60% to 99.5%, alongside new ancillary revenue.

Audience reaction was broadly positive, but the panel was more skeptical, questioning whether this problem would ultimately be solved at the infrastructure layer rather than through a standalone servicing product, and probing the business model given Postra's relationship to Letsfly itself. In clarifying the model, it was explained that the company operates as a B2B service for client agencies such as OTAs, who pass along booking information like PNR or ticketing details so Postra can handle post-booking servicing on their behalf. When pressed on who the ideal customer would be, given that large OTAs often build this capability in-house, the response maintained that the solution can technically support all types of agencies, including the large OTAs that generate the highest volume of post-booking requests.

## Key Takeaways

### ❓ Letsfly’s customer focus and differentiation remain under scrutiny

Panelists questioned whether large OTAs would build Posture in-house, whether the problem will be solved at the infrastructure layer and what clearly differentiates the product. Letsfly described OTAs and agencies as its customers and said it has one live partner in Europe, but did not identify a more specific ideal customer segment.

Quotes:

> “The large OTAs are doing this themselves… Who would your ideal customers be?”

### 🧳 Fareportal offers bespoke, white-label travel operations

Fareportal positions Travel OS as a supplier-agnostic way for brands to sell travel under their own identity while relying on Fareportal’s platform, operations, servicing and branded concierge. Its commercial model is tailored to each partner’s goals rather than standardized.

Quotes:

> “It’s your brand, our platform. It’s your customers, our operations… It’s bespoke, it’s not boxed, it’s supplier agnostic.”

### 🔄 Travel OS connects partners, travelers and suppliers

Fair Portal describes Travel OS as a multi-sided platform with three connected components: Partner OS for launching businesses, Traveler OS for the branded customer experience and Distribution OS for supplier access to brands.

Quotes:

> “It’s made up of a partner OS for launch. It’s traveler OS for your customers. Getting that branded experience. And it’s distribution OS for suppliers.”

### 🛎️ Traveler OS combines disruption support with branded concierge service

When a flight is canceled, Traveler OS can present pre-prepared replacement options at no additional cost, allowing customers to rebook with one tap. A branded 24/7 human and AI-supported concierge provides additional servicing under the partner’s name.

Quotes:

> “The flight gets canceled, and that’s where traveler OS gives customers flight options at no additional cost, pre-prepared for them… And we have that 24/7 concierge available for them.”

### 📣 Suppliers can target offers through partner channels

Distribution OS allows airlines, hotels and other suppliers to promote directly through selected brand channels, such as a lifestyle brand or bank portal, using discounts, bonus points and other incentives.

Quotes:

> “An airline, or hotel, can now promote directly to specific channels… Direct offers… whether that’s 20% discount on beach getaways or 20% more bonus points on their bank portal redemption.”

### ✅ Posture takes post-booking requests through to execution

Posture is designed to handle changes, cancellations, refunds and ancillary requests—from retrieving available options and rules through confirmation and execution. It supports both servicing and sales of seats, bags, meals, check-in and other extras.

Quotes:

> “Posture is a completion-oriented post-booking automation solution that covers both post-booking servicing and ancillary service.”

### 🧑‍💻 Posture combines automation with expert escalation

Requests that automation cannot complete are routed to an expert team, with urgency-based prioritization—for example, giving precedence to travelers departing soon. Resolution and cost data are then fed back into the automation engine to improve future handling.

Quotes:

> “Not every request can be handled entirely through automation. So what happens when the automation cannot complete a request on its own? We have expert team sitting behind to handle such requests.”

### 📈 Post-booking support can cut costs and improve resolution

In a European OTA pilot, Letsfly says Posture reduced operating expenses by around 70% and increased servicing success from 60% to 99.5%. The platform is positioned to reduce support costs while protecting loyalty and creating revenue from existing bookings.

Quotes:

> “OPEX was reduced by around 70% with Posture… an increase in servicing success rate from 60% to 99.5%.”

### 🖱️ Tenant Hub lets partners configure a travel business without building everything from scratch

Tenant Hub is presented as the partner-facing environment where brands can use point-and-click tools to configure branding, features, customer experience and operational capabilities for a dedicated travel business.

Quotes:

> “With point-and-click, run and launch a completely dedicated branded travel business.”

### 🌐 Posture supports fragmented airline connectivity

Let’s Fly says Posture supports more than 100 airlines across Asia, Europe and the Americas, and can service bookings across GDS, NDC, direct airline APIs and aggregation channels.

Quotes:

> “Posture currently supports more than 100 airlines across Asia, Europe, and Americas… across almost all the channels, such as GDS, NDC, airline direct connect API, and aggregation channels.”

# Globaleur

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Chelsea Salamone, Principal, Thayer Investment Partners; Ellen Keszler, CEO, Clear Sky Associates; Erik Blachford, Founder, Pine5 Ventures LLC; Kristina Carter, VP, Global Partnerships, Perk; Kurien Jacob, Partner, Highgate Technology Ventures; Michael Berk, Director of Strategic Innovation and Partnerships, American Express Global Business Travel; Pete Comeau, Managing Director, Phocuswright; Roger Sharp, Chairman, WebBeds Group and North Ridge Partners; Steve Singh, Executive Chairman and CEO, Spotnana; Traci Mercer, Chief Commercial Officer, Priceline; David Lee, CEO, Globaleur
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 1:48 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 1:57 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 704
**Session ID:** `b45c52b5-c30d-4419-9193-aa449e909270`

## Summary

The session opened with the tail end of a prior panel discussion about airline distribution, where panelists debated why a travel technology was being positioned toward OTAs rather than travel management companies (TMCs), noting that TMCs typically access airline content through GDS and questioning whether a live partner outside of one specific carrier existed; the answer confirmed one live partner in Europe before the panel wrapped up.

The next speaker was introduced as representing Globaleur, which presented an agentic AI system designed to personalize, plan, sell, and settle an entire trip within a brand that travelers already use, rather than sending them to third-party platforms.

The core problem framed for the industry was that travel brands generate customer intent but third parties capture most of the resulting margin and transaction value. This was illustrated with market figures: airlines sold $157 billion in ancillaries in 2025, yet far larger adjacent categories—hotel stays, tours and activities, rental cars, and other ancillaries—are mostly captured outside of the originating brand. The tours and activities market alone was cited at $271 billion, with 67% of those bookings still happening offline, underscoring the scale of value leaking away from brands.

The system was described as being deployed directly inside airline platforms, hotel platforms, airports, and other partner brands. It first interprets traveler intent from natural-language requests (for example, a family trip to Tokyo for three days), then personalizes a plan using preference and demographic data, upselling stays, tours, activities, dining, and other experiences. Rather than requiring travelers to jump across five different platforms, the system settles all bookings within the host brand's own platform, and performance data is fed back to partner brands to continuously improve the experience.

Concrete use cases were walked through for different partner types. With airlines, the tool is embedded in websites, mobile apps, and airport lounges, leveraging existing booking data (traveling party, trip length, destination) so travelers can immediately chat with the agent about preferences such as dietary needs. With hotels, a QR code placed in guest rooms lets properties push offers and facility information while guests ask questions about amenities, hours, or airport transport, with the concierge handling responses. For destination marketing organizations, a partnership with one of Asia's largest DMOs, Busan, was highlighted: a previously static, blog-style 2D content platform was rebuilt into a fully agentic system that lets visitors specify interests—such as K-pop, beauty, or seafood—along with travel-party details to receive a complete, tailored Busan itinerary covering tours and activities. The presentation then moved toward explaining why major travel brands choose to partner with the company, comparing its offering to alternatives, before the transcript cuts off.

## Key Takeaways

### 🧳 Globaleur positions agentic AI as an end-to-end commerce layer

The platform is presented as an agentic AI system that can personalize a trip, plan the itinerary, sell related products, complete the booking, and settle the transaction within a travel brand's environment.

Quotes:

> It's an agentic AI system that personalizes, plans, sells, and settles your entire trip in a brand that everyone already uses.

### 🛍️ The platform cross-sells the full trip around the flight

After a flight is booked, Globaleur can recommend and sell stays, tours, activities, dining, and other trip components based on the traveler's profile and requirements.

Quotes:

> We upsell your stays, tours, and activities, places to eat, places to do, based on your traveler profile.

### 💰 Travel brands risk losing ancillary revenue to third parties

Globaleur's core premise is that brands generate travel intent but third-party platforms capture much of the resulting transaction value, especially beyond the flight booking.

Quotes:

> Travel brands create the intent, but the third party captures all the margins and transactions.

### 🗺️ Traveler intent becomes the starting point for personalization

A natural-language request—such as a three-day Tokyo trip with a child—provides the basis for tailoring the itinerary using preferences, demographics, and other traveler inputs.

Quotes:

> I'm flying to Tokyo for three days, with a child. And then our system starts personalizing based on your preference, demographics, and all the other inputs.

### 🗺️ Tourism boards can turn destination content into trip planners

Globaleur described replacing a static, blog-style destination platform with an agentic system that builds complete itineraries around visitor interests, travel companions, and trip length.

Quotes:

> While it used to be a blog, 2D, like, you know, content-based system, we totally overhauled their entire platform into an agentic system.

### 🏨 Hotels can add an AI concierge through room-based access

Hotels can place QR codes in rooms to let guests ask questions, receive personalized offers, learn about facilities, and arrange services such as airport transportation.

Quotes:

> Once you check in, you scan a QR code that we place in each of the rooms... As a guest, you can ask any questions. What are the pool hours? Do you have any special arrangements for me? How can I get to the airport?

### 📱 Airline touchpoints can extend beyond the website

The proposed deployment model places the agent across airline websites, mobile apps, and airport lounges, using existing booking data to begin post-booking trip planning.

Quotes:

> We usually sit in their website, mobile app, and airport lounges. So once you book the flight, we already have your travel data.

### ✈️ Airline content is expanding beyond traditional GDS channels

The discussion highlighted that TMCs typically connect to airline content through GDSs, while broader carrier support—including low-cost carriers—is an important opportunity.

Quotes:

> Usually the TMCs connect the airline content most through GDS... our pitch point here is we are able to handle all types of carriers. Low-cost carrier is a great example.

### 🏷️ Brands can consolidate fragmented travel bookings

Globaleur aims to let travelers book multiple trip components inside the brand's platform instead of being sent to several external services.

Quotes:

> Instead of going out to five different platforms, now you can book all of this within the brand's platform.

### 🥗 Conversational requirements can shape the itinerary

Travelers can communicate practical needs, such as dietary preferences, through the agent, which then plans the itinerary around those requests.

Quotes:

> You can start chatting with the agent, whether you're a vegetarian, all the other requirements that you possibly have. We'll plan the travel itinerary around those requests.

# Pintours

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Chelsea Salamone, Principal, Thayer Investment Partners; Ellen Keszler, CEO, Clear Sky Associates; Erik Blachford, Founder, Pine5 Ventures LLC; Kristina Carter, VP, Global Partnerships, Perk; Kurien Jacob, Partner, Highgate Technology Ventures; Michael Berk, Director of Strategic Innovation and Partnerships, American Express Global Business Travel; Pete Comeau, Managing Director, Phocuswright; Roger Sharp, Chairman, WebBeds Group and North Ridge Partners; Steve Singh, Executive Chairman and CEO, Spotnana; Traci Mercer, Chief Commercial Officer, Priceline; Lou Chatta, CEO, Pintours
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 1:57 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 2:06 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 1862
**Session ID:** `6121c547-e970-4821-bb3b-c1ad3339fa76`

## Summary

The session opened with a pitch for an airline retail personalization platform that runs inside travel brands' own websites and booking flows, letting partners keep the customer relationship and user behavior data instead of losing it to third-party redirects. The presenter sized the opportunity using the roughly 2.6 billion annual air passengers, a conservative 2% capture rate, and an average $500 transaction value to arrive at a $26 billion market opportunity, and described current traction as 92 named accounts across 22 countries spanning 35 airlines/airports, 42 hotel brands, and 9 government-run destination boards, translating into a $261 million revenue opportunity.

Audience voting on this pitch was uniformly positive, but judges pressed on the plausibility of the 2% attach rate, drawing on the historical difficulty of cross-selling travel ancillaries (the old Expedia analogy of airline tickets as 'milk at the back of the store'). The presenter defended the number by explaining capture happens through two channels—embedding on the brand's website and inside the airline's native booking flow—using data the airline already shares. Further questions covered how hotel and activity content is sourced (partnerships with OTAs, bed banks, and other aggregators for hotels, tours, rental cars, and airport transfers) and how personalization works despite limited first-time user data, relying on airline/hotel-provided demographics, loyalty tier, mileage history, and past bookings, supplemented by simple onboarding questions about trip preferences.

The next pitch introduced an AI 'expert layer' for travel experiences, framed as a way to scale VIP tour-guide expertise that currently costs thousands of dollars per private tour and can't reach most guests. While general AI tools can already plan and book trips, the founder argued they still rely on static content like maps and audio tours and lack the deep, human expert knowledge that distinguishes a true VIP experience; the company's patented interview process is positioned to extract that expertise and embed it on top of leading LLMs. Proof points included powering experiences at Alcatraz and the Empire State Building, and delivering fan experiences across host cities during the World Cup through a partner. The product was shown applied across verticals—tours and activities via an AR demo, cruises as an end-to-end concierge and excursion planner, rail and aviation journeys replacing static maps with personalized AI guidance, and hospitality concierge services—monetized through revenue share or licensing, with the company already supplying an estimated 5–10% of experiences offered by major OTAs.

Audience reaction was mostly positive with one skeptic, and judges raised harder questions in the Q&A. One concern invoked the 'bitter lesson'—that raw computing power tends to overwhelm curated human interfaces over time—questioning whether the human-expertise advantage would hold against fast-improving general AI. Others asked why the company focuses on B2B rather than direct-to-consumer, to which the answer was a desire to power the biggest brands given the scale of opportunity in B2B, and whether it competes with or replaces players like Viator or GetYourGuide; the founder clarified it instead powers those OTAs, supplying a meaningful share of their experience inventory. Pressed on why not simply specialize in being the expert layer for curated venue experiences, the response reaffirmed that this is already the core focus, with interviews revealing tour guides' decades of undocumented knowledge as the company's key differentiator. On scalability, the founder described a patented onboarding process combining existing property materials (maps, PDFs) with a two-to-three-hour expert interview to stand up a new experience in a matter of hours.

## Key Takeaways

### 🌍 Deployment spans 92 travel brands in 22 countries

The company said it has 92 named accounts across 22 countries, covering airlines and airports, hotel brands, and government-operated destination and tourism boards.

Quotes:

> We have 92 named accounts, big brands... spread apart, 22 countries.

### 🌐 Pintors is applying its expert layer across major travel experiences

Pintors said it powers experiences including Alcatraz and Empire and is working with partner Data Curve on World Cup fan experiences across host cities, stadiums, and surrounding journeys.

Quotes:

> We power some of the biggest experiences like Alcatraz and Empire. And now we're focused on the B2B... the World Cup. We were powering the fan experiences throughout every host city.

### 🧑‍🏫 Pintors turns human expertise into an AI layer

Pintors AI says it captures VIP-level knowledge from expert interviews through a patented process, then embeds that expertise on top of leading large language models. The company’s differentiation is that models are increasingly interchangeable, while deeply contextual human expertise remains scarce and can improve experiences across travel brands.

Quotes:

> We take that expertise and embed it, enabling the next generation of experiences... Models are commodity, expertise isn't.

### ⚠️ The 2% attach-rate assumption was challenged

An investor questioned whether a 2% attach rate is genuinely conservative, citing the historical difficulty of cross-selling ancillary products even when airline tickets drive traffic. The company defended the estimate by pointing to booking-system integration and access to airline data.

Quotes:

> To me, that seems very aggressive, just based on what I've seen.

### 🧳 Travel brands can keep the customer and transaction in-house

The company embeds personalization and booking across the entire trip inside travel brands, avoiding redirects to third-party platforms. This is intended to preserve the partner’s customer relationship, behavioral data, and customer intelligence.

Quotes:

> We personalize the entire trip. And we allow the users to book everything within the travel brand. And the partner keeps the customer.

### ⚙️ A patented process aims to scale expert onboarding

Pintours says it can convert an establishment’s maps, PDFs, and other onboarding materials, together with a two- or three-hour expert interview, into an AI experience layer within a few hours. This is its answer to the scalability challenge of curating expertise for individual venues.

Quotes:

> We developed a patented process that can do this in a couple hours... We can do that with a 2 or 3 hour interview, and that's all good to go.

### 💰 Pintors monetizes through licensing and bookings

Pintors is focused on B2B distribution through major travel brands rather than direct-to-consumer sales. It says it earns revenue either through revenue share on experiences booked or through licensing fees for embedding its expert layer into a brand’s systems.

Quotes:

> We do revenue share for experiences booked or we do a licensing model where brands can embed it into their systems and just pay us the license fee.

### 🚢 The platform spans tours, cruises, and rail

The company described deployments in tours and activities through augmented-reality experiences, cruise concierge and excursion planning, and comparable guest experiences for rail.

Quotes:

> In tours and activities, we embed the same AI expert layer into an AR experience... We also power cruises. We are the concierge level on the cruise as well as the on-destination excursion planner and booker... And the same experience works for rail.

### 📈 The company estimates a $26 billion opportunity

Using an estimated 2.6 billion annual air passengers, a conservative 2% capture rate, and a $500 average transaction value, the company presented a potential opportunity of approximately $26 billion.

Quotes:

> 2.6 billion passengers fly... We conservatively can capture about 2%... average transaction value about $500 per transaction. That translates into a 26 billion opportunity for us.

### 🎯 Personalization combines partner data with traveler interaction

Pintours says it initially personalizes using partner-held data such as demographics, loyalty status, mileage, and historical bookings, then refines recommendations through simple preference questions. Its integrations can appear both on a travel brand’s website and inside the airline booking flow.

Quotes:

> Airlines and hotel chains... already have some data about the user, like demographics, loyalty tier, how much mileage you've accrued so far, historical bookings... once the users start engaging with our system, we also ask very simple questions.

# Voyani

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Chelsea Salamone, Principal, Thayer Investment Partners; Ellen Keszler, CEO, Clear Sky Associates; Erik Blachford, Founder, Pine5 Ventures LLC; Kristina Carter, VP, Global Partnerships, Perk; Kurien Jacob, Partner, Highgate Technology Ventures; Michael Berk, Director of Strategic Innovation and Partnerships, American Express Global Business Travel; Pete Comeau, Managing Director, Phocuswright; Roger Sharp, Chairman, WebBeds Group and North Ridge Partners; Steve Singh, Executive Chairman and CEO, Spotnana; Traci Mercer, Chief Commercial Officer, Priceline; Bob Kaufman, Co-Founder & CEO, Voyani
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 2:06 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 2:15 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 1221
**Session ID:** `01100a8a-5157-41ac-b958-56dbf2a9e2db`

## Summary

The pitch opened by framing a problem familiar to travel sellers: after the initial booking, travelers go on to spend significant money with other providers on dining, tours, transportation and events, money the original seller never captures, while customer support calls triggered by in-trip problems (flight delays, lost confirmations) eat into already thin margins. A cited study found 93% of travelers can't fully relax on a trip because managing logistics is their top stressor, and booking margins are further squeezed by massive customer-acquisition spend, with the four largest OTAs spending $20 billion on sales and marketing last year and Expedia putting half its revenue toward it.

Voyani's answer is to target the post-booking, in-trip window, pointing to research showing 47% of trip spend happens in-trip and that tours, activities and attractions alone were a $271 billion category last year. The product is positioned as a personal travel manager app that consolidates all of a traveler's confirmations, vouchers and itinerary details in one place, eliminating the need to dig through emails or scattered apps for things like museum tickets or rail passes, and reducing the support burden on the original seller because travelers already have answers in their pocket. Voyani acts as merchant of record on in-trip purchases made through the app (activities, eSIMs, local transportation, etc.) and shares a portion of that commission back with the partner who referred the traveler, at no cost or fee to the partner or the traveler.

Integration was described as lightweight: sellers keep selling what they already sell, then add a small code snippet to their confirmation page that links to Voyani; when a traveler clicks it, everything just purchased auto-populates their itinerary in the app, and any additional in-trip spend generates a shared commission. The pitch noted that Booking Holdings recently announced a $100 million investment in a similar product (Vail), but argued that the large OTAs will build this for their own customers, leaving an opening for Voyani to serve small and mid-tier travel sellers who lack the resources to build such a platform themselves.

In the Q&A, panelists pressed on differentiation given other players building similar in-trip tools; the response reiterated that the target market is small and mid-sized sellers rather than the large players building in-house. On the mechanics of capturing in-trip spend, it was clarified that Voyani is the merchant of record on purchases made through its own in-trip offerings, so it can track and share revenue on those, but it cannot pay commission on purchases made outside its network, though such outside purchases (confirmations, vouchers) can still be ingested into the app for organization. Asked about competition, the team said it sees no direct competitors today, though card providers were floated as a possible future one. A follow-up noted that being the merchant for only certain categories leaves some spend uncaptured, and it was confirmed that Voyani's merchant role is limited to post-booking, in-trip purchases like additional activities, eSIMs and local transportation, not pre-booking or pre-trip spend. On traction, the product was said to be live in beta with two partners, with the conference being used to recruit more.

## Key Takeaways

### 🔗 A confirmation-page link activates the itinerary

Travel companies can add Voyani with a small code snippet on their confirmation page; when travelers click the link, the products already sold to them automatically populate their Voyani itinerary.

Quotes:

> You offer Voyani with a small snippet of code that adds a link to your confirmation page. When the traveler clicks that link, everything you just sold them automatically populates their itinerary within Voyani.

### 📱 Voyani acts as a traveler’s personal trip manager

The app is designed to accompany travelers throughout the journey, consolidating trip details and reducing the need to search through emails or remember logistics.

Quotes:

> Voyani is your traveler's personal travel manager. Voyani is the app they take with them on their trip to eliminate their stress.

### 🔁 Better trip management is intended to improve retention

Beyond direct revenue, Voyani argues that a smoother trip experience can make travelers happier and more likely to return and book again with the originating travel company.

Quotes:

> But there's something even more important. And that's a happy traveler that comes back and books with you again because they had a great trip experience.

### 📞 Traveler anxiety creates costly support demand

When travelers struggle with logistics, they contact the company that sold the booking, adding support costs to already thin booking margins.

Quotes:

> And what happens when they're stressed? They call you. And every one of those anxious calls eats into what you already made on the booking.

### 📈 Partner revenue comes without additional acquisition cost

The pitch to travel companies is that Voyani can add revenue after the booking without requiring them to incur another customer acquisition expense.

Quotes:

> It's additional revenue without the cost. With your bookings, after acquisition costs and support costs, only a fraction of every dollar reaches your bottom line. From Voyani, every dollar goes straight there.

### 🧭 Post-booking experience is Voyani’s core focus

Voyani has spent the last 18 months building around the post-booking and in-trip experience rather than the initial reservation.

Quotes:

> While most of the industry has been focused on the initial booking, we've spent the last year and a half focused on everything after.

### 🎯 Voyani targets smaller travel sellers

Voyani is aimed at small and mid-sized travel sellers that cannot justify or afford the large investment required to build a comparable post-booking product themselves.

Quotes:

> Our target market is the small and mid-tier travel sellers who don't have the $100 million to invest in a product like this.

### 💳 Voyani monetizes in-trip purchases as the merchant

Voyani acts as the merchant for additional products purchased through its platform and shares resulting revenue with the travel company that originally connected the traveler. Travelers can also import externally purchased confirmations, but those purchases do not generate commissionable revenue for the originating seller.

Quotes:

> Anything that they buy within their trip on Voyani, we're the merchant, we track that, and that's how we share that revenue back with our partners.

### 🧪 Voyani is in beta with two live partners

The product has launched in beta with two partners so far, and the company is using the conference to recruit additional travel sellers.

Quotes:

> We're in beta live. So we have two partners today. And, you know, we're using this conference to help many more after this.

### 🛍️ In-trip spending is a large untapped opportunity

Voyani targets the 47% of trip spending that occurs in-trip, including activities, visas, eSIMs, local transportation and other services.

Quotes:

> A study by the National Travel Center found that 47% of spend on a trip happens in-trip. We capture that spend.

# Dfinitiv

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Chelsea Salamone, Principal, Thayer Investment Partners; Ellen Keszler, CEO, Clear Sky Associates; Erik Blachford, Founder, Pine5 Ventures LLC; Kristina Carter, VP, Global Partnerships, Perk; Kurien Jacob, Partner, Highgate Technology Ventures; Michael Berk, Director of Strategic Innovation and Partnerships, American Express Global Business Travel; Pete Comeau, Managing Director, Phocuswright; Roger Sharp, Chairman, WebBeds Group and North Ridge Partners; Steve Singh, Executive Chairman and CEO, Spotnana; Traci Mercer, Chief Commercial Officer, Priceline; Joseph Fitzgerald, CEO, Dfinitiv
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 2:15 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 2:24 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 1287
**Session ID:** `9e3d22da-bba6-497e-bb61-0a7120f9fbcb`

## Summary

The pitch opened by framing a problem: loyalty benefits such as status, cards, and perks are invisible inside chat-based assistant conversations. Users are pushed out to websites via links or ads, forced to log in and navigate multiple steps, creating a poor experience focused on discovery rather than engagement. On the agent side, tools like Muse and Instinct scrape airline and hotel websites directly, meaning the brands have no way to recognize the user as a loyalty member or even verify they are a legitimate person, which makes suppliers uncomfortable with this kind of interaction.

The proposed solution is a platform that helps brands with large loyalty ecosystems and branded cards build loyalty experiences directly inside chat apps and for AI agents, avoiding the need to redirect users to a separate website. A demo showed booking a flight after logging in with loyalty credentials, with the ability to reserve, upgrade, and use miles within the chat conversation itself. By identifying the user and using a protocol-based connection rather than scraping, agents like Muse could apply a user's Platinum status, wallet card, and miles automatically when booking.

Technically, the platform addresses three needs for brands: a way for business teams (not engineers) to author conversational journeys that work across ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and other chat environments and devices, ready for publishing to plugin marketplaces; a reasoning engine that factors in partner ecosystems, loyalty status, and branded cards to serve the right offers and experiences back to the user in chat; and a management layer that lets brands see which platforms customers are coming through, what agents are doing, and what transactions—flights, rooms, partner programs—are being completed.

On traction and business model, the platform has been powering a major U.S. airline loyalty program for over a year with 100% availability and is SOC 2 certified, positioning it as enterprise-ready. The target market includes any airline, hotel, or cruise line with a large loyalty program and branded cards seeking direct engagement in chat platforms. Revenue comes from a platform fee plus activity-based charges, explicitly not a share of bookings.

In the Q&A, panelists pressed on how the model interacts with existing supplier and card-company loyalty programs and what value it adds beyond them; the response was that airlines and hotels want to engage customers directly in chat to retain margin, upsell related services like hotels or dining, and capture interchange fees by letting users book with a card stored in their wallet rather than entering card details into chat. Another question challenged how the platform could attract multiple loyalty programs without enough scale, and the answer clarified that the company works on behalf of its airline or hotel customers, connecting to their existing supply partners, booking systems, loyalty programs, and branded cards, and presenting that content into ChatGPT or other agents on the brand's behalf. A final question on reliability—whether a platinum member could trust the system to actually secure a better seat—was answered by explaining that logging in pulls the user's status, miles, and upgrade eligibility, which are then applied across all pricing and offers shown. The panel closed by thanking the presenter and applauding the pitch before moving to the next focus group.

## Key Takeaways

### ✍️ Business teams can author conversational journeys

Non-engineering teams can create the conversations customers experience across ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and other chat environments.

Quotes:

> We created the ability for business teams, not engineers, to author conversational journeys that will occur in ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, across the different chat environments.

### 🤝 Direct chat engagement can expand supplier economics

Airlines and hotels may use direct in-chat engagement to transact with customers more directly, potentially reduce intermediary margin leakage and extend relevant offers to hotels, dining and other activities.

Quotes:

> If they book with you directly in chat, they don't have to go through a lot of the other layers, maybe not give up as much margin. Plus, after you book, they may say, well, now that you booked that flight, how about a hotel?

### 🧠 A reasoning engine selects relevant experiences

The platform’s reasoning engine considers partner relationships, loyalty status and branded-card information to return the appropriate experiences and offers in chat.

Quotes:

> It talks to a reasoning engine that looks at the partner ecosystem, the loyalty, the fact you have a branded card, and it serves back the right experience of partners, loyalty and offers back to the user in chat.

### ✅ Enterprise deployment demonstrates platform maturity

The platform has reportedly powered a major U.S. airline loyalty program for more than a year with 100% availability, and its commercial model combines a platform fee with activity-based fees rather than taking a share of bookings.

Quotes:

> We have been powering a major US airline loyalty program for over a year with 100% availability... Our business model is platform fee plus the level of activity we don't take a share of the bookings or things like that.

### 💳 Identity applies loyalty benefits and card context

When a system knows a customer’s loyalty tier and branded card, it can apply relevant benefits, miles and offers while giving the provider greater confidence in the interaction.

Quotes:

> It can know that I am a Platinum member and I have a card in my wallet, and it can go take advantage of all my benefits, use miles, get all the things that my loyalty program brings to me.

### 🌐 Cross-platform journeys support multiple devices and channels

Conversational journeys are designed to work across mobile and desktop and be prepared for publication through different plugin marketplaces and platform channels.

Quotes:

> It works across all different devices, mobile, desktop, things like that. And it will make it ready to publish to the various plugin marketplaces or platforms.

### 💬 Authenticated loyalty booking stays inside chat

Customers can log in within the conversational experience and complete benefits-rich transactions—such as booking flights, reserving upgrades and redeeming miles—without being redirected to a website.

Quotes:

> This is a demo of actually booking a flight after logging in with my credentials, and I can reserve, upgrade, use miles, do all sorts of things that bring my loyalty program into that conversation.

### 🛡️ Structured protocols replace anonymous website scraping

Emerging protocols let brands communicate with chat and reasoning agents in an identified, controlled way rather than relying on agents to scrape their websites.

Quotes:

> The protocols are there and they can be leveraged by companies.

### 🧩 Brands need authoring, integration and management tools

Supporting conversational commerce requires tools to author journeys, connect authentication with existing partner, booking, loyalty and branded-card systems, and monitor customer and agent activity.

Quotes:

> They need a way to build the conversation that's going to occur in chat. They need a platform to integrate with their authentication, with their partners, with their loyalty ecosystems. And they need a way to manage it to see what's going on.

# Gadget Korea

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Chelsea Salamone, Principal, Thayer Investment Partners; Ellen Keszler, CEO, Clear Sky Associates; Erik Blachford, Founder, Pine5 Ventures LLC; Kristina Carter, VP, Global Partnerships, Perk; Kurien Jacob, Partner, Highgate Technology Ventures; Michael Berk, Director of Strategic Innovation and Partnerships, American Express Global Business Travel; Pete Comeau, Managing Director, Phocuswright; Roger Sharp, Chairman, WebBeds Group and North Ridge Partners; Steve Singh, Executive Chairman and CEO, Spotnana; Traci Mercer, Chief Commercial Officer, Priceline; David Kim, Global Strategy & Investor Relations Lead, Gadget Korea Inc.
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 2:26 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 2:35 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 1117
**Session ID:** `e984611d-d7f2-44a3-a827-827c0ef99c64`

## Summary

The session opened with a pitch for a Korea-focused travel service, framed around the gap between booking a trip and actually experiencing a destination once there. The presenter noted that while booking flights, rooms and tables is largely solved, four out of ten visitors to South Korea report frustration with local digital services and payments, often because of self-verification requirements tied to a Korean phone number or apps that are not foreigner-friendly. A concrete example was given of a visitor arriving in Korea and trying to book a restaurant reservation, only to be blocked because the app demands a Korean phone number for verification.

To solve this, the company described two products: Travel Mode and a new service called Koro. The business originated as a South Korean eSIM provider and is repositioning as a travel super app; once a traveler buys the eSIM and connects upon landing, they gain access to a bundle of local mini-apps that are otherwise hard for foreigners to use, plus partner perks such as discounts with Olive Young or duty-free retailers. For longer-term stayers in Korea, who face problems like being unable to get a local number or data, visa confusion, and housing scams, the company is introducing passport-based verification: travelers can scan a passport before arrival, undergo an identity check, and receive a Korean mobile number via eSIM that activates on landing, removing the need for a Korean-number verification step.

The team argued its differentiation comes from owning the underlying technology rather than relying on third parties: its own eSIM platform, its own mobile line, and close partnerships with Korean companies such as Catch Table, Olive Young and Shilla Duty Free. The roadmap includes a global launch of Travel Mode by the end of 2026, passport verification rolling out in early 2027, and a planned collaboration with Visa, alongside future help with housing and job searches for long-term stayers. The founding team's credibility was tied to backgrounds at Wi-Fi Dosirak (described as Korea's top pocket Wi-Fi company), Shinsegae (a leading Korean retailer), and prior experience at the United Nations for the Chief Strategy Officer presenting.

Audience feedback was mixed, with two green and two red reactions. One questioner asked whether a visitor on a postpaid phone, rather than a prepaid eSIM, could still access the app's features, since the eSIM is the delivery mechanism; the presenter confirmed access would still be possible, and the questioner noted interest from a destination marketing organization looking beyond Korea. Another questioner pressed on differentiation, pointing out that many eSIM providers already exist and could pivot to offer the same thing, and observed that the pitch had initially come across as just another eSIM company rather than a broader travel super app. The presenter clarified that the eSIM itself is not the innovation but merely one component of a larger super app aimed at making the foreigner experience in Korea seamless, a framing the questioner said had not been clear from the pitch.

On distribution, the presenter said the company currently relies on B2B partnerships, largely with OTAs and companies interested in building their own travel super apps, and that it is the market leader within South Korea, though most of its partnerships remain domestic rather than international; distribution outside Korea has mainly taken the form of API integrations and white-labeling. The session closed with thanks to the presenter before moving on to the next speaker.

## Key Takeaways

### 🧩 Travel mode bundles connectivity with local services

After connecting in Korea, travelers can access integrated local mini-apps designed to be more foreigner-friendly, rather than navigating services that previously offered limited support to international users.

Quotes:

> Once you connect and land in Korea you basically get a wide variety of local apps that are integrated into our own app that are foreigner friendly.

### 🎁 Partnerships add benefits beyond connectivity

The travel offering is intended to include partner perks such as Olive Young benefits and duty-free discounts, extending the product beyond an eSIM or data connection.

Quotes:

> You also get special perks and benefits from our partners, whether that's maybe the famous Olive Young ... or maybe it could be duty free discounts.

### 🤝 Distribution is driven by B2B partnerships

Gadget currently distributes mainly through partnerships with OTAs and other companies, using APIs and white-label arrangements rather than relying only on direct consumer acquisition.

Quotes:

> For now, we are doing a lot of just B2B partnerships ... most of our distributions have been through API or a lot of white labeling.

### 🧳 Long-term residents face connectivity and settling-in barriers

The speaker highlights a broader set of challenges for foreigners planning to stay in Korea, including lacking a phone number or data, waiting for residence cards, visa confusion and housing scams.

Quotes:

> One of the biggest issues that a lot of foreigners wanting to stay in Korea have is they have no number, no data ... they have a lot of visa confusion or sometimes the most famous housing scams.

### 🛂 Passport verification enables phone plans before arrival

Gadget’s planned new service will let long-term stayers verify their identity with a passport and receive a Korean eSIM phone number before landing, so service can be activated on arrival.

Quotes:

> You can now also get mobile phone plans from South Korea even before you land in Korea. You simply scan your passport ... and then we issue you a number as an eSIM that you can activate as soon as you land.

### 📱 Korean phone verification creates widespread digital friction

The speaker says four in ten visitors report complaints about digital services or payments; a common issue is being unable to book restaurants or use local platforms because they require verification through a Korean phone number.

Quotes:

> Every 4 out of 10 visitors to Korea have some complaints about our digital services or payments. The most famous one being ... self-verification through a Korean number.

### 🗺️ The roadmap expands from travel into settlement support

After launching Travel Mode globally and rolling out passport verification, Gadget says it plans to support longer-term visitors with visa matters, housing, job searches and other settlement needs.

Quotes:

> Afterwards, we'll be working with visa issues, helping with housing, job searches, and much more.

### 🔗 Gadget’s differentiation rests on owned infrastructure and local ties

The company says its competitive advantage comes from technologies it already owns, including its eSIM platform and mobile line, combined with relationships with Korean businesses such as Catch Table, Olive Young and Shilla Duty Free.

Quotes:

> We run on technologies that we already own ... our own eSIM platform ... our own mobile line ... [and] work closely with a lot of local Korean companies.

### 🚀 Gadget is expanding from eSIMs into a travel super app

Gadget, originally a South Korean eSIM company, is transitioning toward a travel super app that brings connectivity and local services together.

Quotes:

> Gadget is originally a eSIM company from South Korea but we are now transitioning into a travel super app.

### 📲 The app is not limited to Gadget eSIM users

Gadget said travelers using a postpaid phone can still access the app and its local-service context; the eSIM is a delivery mechanism, not the only route into the experience.

Quotes:

> If I'm visiting Korea with a postpaid phone, not a prepaid SIM, can I still access the app? ... Yes, you can.

# AltexSoft

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Chelsea Salamone, Principal, Thayer Investment Partners; Ellen Keszler, CEO, Clear Sky Associates; Erik Blachford, Founder, Pine5 Ventures LLC; Kristina Carter, VP, Global Partnerships, Perk; Kurien Jacob, Partner, Highgate Technology Ventures; Michael Berk, Director of Strategic Innovation and Partnerships, American Express Global Business Travel; Pete Comeau, Managing Director, Phocuswright; Roger Sharp, Chairman, WebBeds Group and North Ridge Partners; Steve Singh, Executive Chairman and CEO, Spotnana; Traci Mercer, Chief Commercial Officer, Priceline; Oleksandr Medovoi, Founder & CEO, AltexSoft
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 2:35 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 2:44 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 1293
**Session ID:** `fd0851a7-6a9a-494c-b6f1-b084c88438aa`

## Summary

The session opened with an audience poll about reliance on software, followed by a pitch built around industry statistics: roughly 58% of engineering time is spent understanding existing systems and code, and while AI tools let engineers produce code about 25 times faster, 96% of teams are not confident that AI-generated code is correct, leading to more bugs per developer and review times five times longer. The net result, it was argued, is that AI coding tools alone only yield about 30% productivity gain once these costs are factored in.

Travel was framed as especially exposed to this risk because, after building software for travel clients since 2009, the speaker described typical travel infrastructure as a hybrid of 20-year-old legacy systems and newer, partly AI-generated code, all handling billions of dollars in transactions and customer data — described as a 'ticking bomb with a high blast radius.'

The proposed solution, Interlace, was presented as a product built by travel-tech engineers rather than a generic product team, designed to map an entire software stack into a multidimensional graph of components, user flows, endpoints, and underlying business logic. It acts as a context layer connecting engineers or AI agents to existing coding tools (Cursor, Claude, Codex, etc.), letting users ask in plain language what would happen if a system change were made, with Interlace identifying all impacted systems and logic changes — intended to help even when a senior developer or an entire engineering firm is gone.

Claimed benefits included shipping speed increasing 35% versus using AI coding tools alone, roughly 25% savings on token spend, and a worked example where a $6 million modernization budget could generate $8 million in value plus $60K in token savings. The system was described as deployed within the customer's own cloud with zero code access for AltexSoft and ISO certification, targeting the roughly 700 largest global travel businesses in a $60 billion addressable market, and positioned against proprietary systems like one Uber was said to have built in-house, offering a comparable solution in as little as two weeks.

Audience feedback was mixed, with mostly positive reactions but some skepticism. Questions probed whether the claimed ROI came from token savings or labor savings, with the response pointing to productivity gains from paying clients, including one in attendance, as the primary driver. Another questioner argued that software engineering as a target is becoming obsolete as AI takes over more tasks, and asked whether Interlace was aimed at companies lacking strong prompt engineering skills; the response countered that context behind large, complex systems is the real challenge that good prompting alone cannot solve, and claimed Interlace outperforms roughly ten competing products that have each raised between $10 and $120 million. A further critique raised doubts about how the value was measured and suggested a mismatch between which companies need the product and which are enthusiastic about it, closing the session on a note of unresolved skepticism.

## Key Takeaways

### 📉 AI coding gains may translate into only modest overall productivity

The speaker argues that defects and longer review cycles offset much of the apparent coding-speed improvement: bugs per developer rise, review times become about five times longer, and overall productivity reaches only roughly 30%.

Quotes:

> As a result of that, 54% bugs per developer come up, and the review times are 5 times longer. So basically, as a result of that, what you get is just about 30% in productivity, including everything.

### ⏱️ AltexSoft claims Interlace can be deployed in two weeks

AltexSoft positions Interlace as a quickly deployable alternative to building an equivalent internal system, citing a two-week leasing timeline and Uber’s reported investment in a similar proprietary capability.

Quotes:

> Uber... announced they built the very same proprietary system. We don't know how many millions of dollars and months of software engineering work they put in. But you can get Interlace with us in as much as 2 weeks.

### 📈 AltexSoft claims productivity and token-cost gains beyond AI coding tools

The speaker claims Interlace increases real shipping speed by 35% compared with AI coding tools alone and reduces token spending by at least 25%. A cited example describes a $6 million modernization budget producing $8 million in annual value, with token savings as an additional benefit.

Quotes:

> We see that real shipping speed is actually going up 35% compared to using AI coding tools alone... with Interlace we see at least 25% savings with the token spend.

### 🧩 Interlace maps the software stack as a multidimensional graph

AltexSoft's Interlace is presented as a system that maps an entire software stack into a connected, multidimensional graph of components, user flows, and endpoints.

Quotes:

> Interlace maps out your entire software stack into multi-dimensional graph. You understand the components user flows endpoints.

### ⚖️ Panelists challenged the product’s ROI measurement and target fit

The discussion raised doubts about how productivity gains are measured and whether the organizations that most need system-understanding support will recognize or accept that need. The speaker responded that complexity and lack of context—not prompting skill alone—are the core problem.

Quotes:

> From my perspective, I put it red because I questioned a little bit on the measurement... The companies that might actually need you are not going to accept that they need you, and the companies that are excited about you don't need you.

### 🔍 Faster AI-generated code creates a verification burden

The speaker says engineers are producing code roughly 25 times faster with current AI tools, while 96% remain uncertain whether the output is correct—creating a substantial need for review and validation.

Quotes:

> Nowadays the engineers on average code 25 times faster... however 96% are not sure what has been produced is correct.

### 👥 System knowledge should survive team turnover

The proposed use case addresses the loss of institutional knowledge when senior developers leave or an external development firm is replaced. The system is intended to help users understand and modify the software despite that turnover.

Quotes:

> Imagine a use case when your senior developer is gone or you fire the whole firm which has been building your software.

### 🧠 The product is designed to trace business logic

Beyond cataloging technical components, Interlace is intended to reliably trace the business logic behind them—an important capability when systems outlive the teams that built them.

Quotes:

> What is the most important thing... it reliably traces the business logic behind it.

### 💥 Hybrid travel infrastructure creates a potentially large blast radius

Travel businesses may run decades-old software alongside newer and partially AI-generated components, all handling billions of dollars in transactions and customer data. Poorly understood changes in such systems can therefore have wide consequences.

Quotes:

> The common software infrastructure is kind of like a hybrid. It could be a hybrid of 20-year-old software which you either acquired or built and something very new, very modern, partially generated by AI... This is all at risk because it's a ticking bomb with a high blast radius.

### 🔐 Interlace is deployed in the customer’s cloud without code access

The product is presented as a security-conscious deployment: it runs in the customer’s cloud, AltexSoft claims to have zero access to the customer’s code, and the product is ISO certified.

Quotes:

> We deploy in your cloud, AltexSoft has zero access to your code and it's ISO certified.
