# ZentrumHub

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Chelsea Salamone, Principal, Thayer Investment Partners; Ellen Keszler, CEO, Clear Sky Associates; Erik Blachford, Founder, Pine5 Ventures LLC; Kristina Carter, VP, Global Partnerships, Perk; Kurien Jacob, Partner, Highgate Technology Ventures; Michael Berk, Director of Strategic Innovation and Partnerships, American Express Global Business Travel; Pete Comeau, Managing Director, Phocuswright; Roger Sharp, Chairman, WebBeds Group and North Ridge Partners; Steve Singh, Executive Chairman and CEO, Spotnana; Traci Mercer, Chief Commercial Officer, Priceline; Abhinav Sinha, Co-Founder & CEO, ZentrumHub
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 2:44 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 2:53 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 1210
**Session ID:** `13bf3c1a-e596-40bb-bf39-15463931c6de`

## Summary

The presentation opened by describing a structural problem in travel distribution: sellers are wired into dozens of wholesalers and DMCs, so a single property search in a market like Dubai can trigger more than 500 supplier calls. Despite rates often returning within two seconds, 90% of the time sellers still end up waiting on the slowest suppliers, meaning more supply sources translate into slower answers rather than better rates.

This inefficiency is set to worsen as AI enters the distribution chain: look-to-book ratios have already climbed from historic levels of 100-to-1 or 1,000-to-1 to roughly 300,000-to-1 because of meta platforms, caching and proliferating distributors, and experts expect a further tenfold increase as bed banks and agencies deploy their own AI agents and MCP servers. The talk framed this as a shared cost problem: travel sellers pay to query suppliers and suppliers pay to answer, yet 60% of the suppliers queried for the cheapest rate only win 5–10% of overall bookings, meaning everyone pays but nobody really knows where to ask.

Positioning itself as the entity that sits between sellers and suppliers and processed over 1 million bookings last year, the company announced the launch of ZentrumIQ, an AI layer for the distribution ecosystem with two products: Verdict for travel sellers, which identifies the properties most likely to sell and the suppliers most likely to be competitive so sellers get the best rate at the best speed regardless of how many suppliers they're wired to, and Pulse for suppliers, a first-of-its-kind analytics tool showing where a supplier is winning or losing business and why—illustrated by a supplier strong in Southeast Asia that keeps paying for Miami searches it can never win competitively. Pilot results on Verdict showed a 30% performance improvement and a 45%+ reduction in supplier calls without sacrificing best pricing.

Looking ahead to an agentic era where agencies run their own MCP servers and agents, the pitch argued that someone needs to know what to ask and whom to ask, positioning ZentrumIQ—already integrated into the company's booking engine API—as that layer, with MCP integration planned next since agents will only be as smart as the data feeds they can access. The company reported pilot customers including 10+ sellers on Verdict and two suppliers on Pulse, operating across a market representing €267 billion in sales and 400,000 distributors, summarizing ZentrumIQ as the rails for optimized distribution built for best rate, best speed, and the coming agent-driven landscape.

Audience and panel reaction was mixed. One panelist disclosed that WebBeds, which works with the company, was pleased with the product. Another pressed on differentiation in a crowded hotel API space, prompting a response that the edge comes from global coverage and cross-channel search data (meta platforms, TMCs, B2Bs) that lets the model learn which suppliers perform best for specific hotels rather than just regions. A more skeptical panelist argued the approach addresses the wrong root cause, contending that the real fix is reducing unnecessary searches by surfacing accurate pricing and identifying unlikely-to-win supply at the source, and suggested predictive AI as a better alternative to layering optimization on top of what was called an existing “spaghetti mess.” The presenter acknowledged partial agreement but maintained that sellers currently lack a way to know which supplier to query when, which is the gap the product aims to close, conceding this was only an initial step.

## Key Takeaways

### 💸 Both sides pay for inefficient searching

Travel sellers incur costs while requesting answers, and suppliers incur costs responding to queries—often from markets where they have little chance of winning the booking.

Quotes:

> Travel sellers are paying to ask for answers while suppliers are paying to answer those queries.

### 🌐 Fragmented supply creates massive search complexity

Travel agencies source rates from wholesalers, DMCs and other agencies. A simple Dubai property search can trigger more than 500 supplier calls, illustrating how fragmented distribution has become.

Quotes:

> A simple search for properties in Dubai translates to more than 500 calls across suppliers.

### ⚡ Verdict prioritizes likely winners before querying suppliers

Verdict is designed to identify which properties are most likely to sell and which suppliers are likely to be competitive, allowing the platform to optimize the search rather than query every connected source equally.

Quotes:

> It automatically identifies which are the properties which are most likely to sell and which are the suppliers that are actually going to be competitive.

### 🤖 ZentrumIQ targets both sides of travel distribution

ZentrumHub launched ZentrumIQ as an AI layer for distribution, with Verdict for travel sellers and Pulse for suppliers. It is already integrated into the booking-engine API, with MCP support identified as a next step for the agentic era.

Quotes:

> Today we are launching ZentrumIQ, the AI for the distribution layer that sees both sides... It is already integrated into our booking engine API, and MCP is something next we are looking at.

### 📊 Verdict pilot reports fewer calls and better performance

In its pilot, ZentrumHub reports a 30% performance improvement and a more than 45% reduction in supplier calls, while maintaining the best price.

Quotes:

> We already see a performance improvement of 30% and reduction in call of over 45% across suppliers, all this without losing on the best price.

### 🎯 Suppliers can reduce spending on unlikely opportunities

Pulse is intended to expose mismatches between a supplier’s strengths and the searches it receives. For example, a supplier with strong Southeast Asia inventory may learn that it is spending on Miami searches where it is unlikely to be competitive.

Quotes:

> It is really good in Southeast Asia... and it is still getting the searches for Miami where most likely it is never going to be competitive.

### 🧭 Pulse shows suppliers where they win and lose

Pulse gives suppliers market- and region-level analytics showing where their distribution performs well, where it loses, and why.

Quotes:

> Pulse lets suppliers know how it is performing in different markets, different regions, where it is winning, where it is losing and why it is losing.

### 🧪 Panelists challenge the layer being optimized

Panelists questioned whether optimizing which suppliers receive searches addresses the root cause of distribution inefficiency. They suggested improving pricing at the source and using predictive AI to determine likely outcomes before adding another optimization layer.

Quotes:

> I think by the way you're attacking the problem the wrong way... if you actually go to the root cause and figure out the actual pricing before at the source itself... you will automatically reduce searches.

### ⏱️ More supply can make rate responses slower

Connecting more suppliers does not automatically improve the customer experience: a search may be delayed by its slowest supplier even when most rates arrive within seconds.

Quotes:

> 90% of the time, even though you are getting your rates within 2 seconds, you still end up waiting for your slowest suppliers.

### 📈 AI is driving look-to-book ratios sharply higher

The distribution ecosystem was built for human search behavior, but AI agents and MCP servers are expected to generate many more queries. The look-to-book ratio has reportedly risen from roughly 100–1,000 searches per booking to 300,000–1.

Quotes:

> Not too long ago, we were happy with our look-to-book of 100 to 1, 1,000 to 1. With meta platforms, caching and so many distributors, the look-to-book has already gone to 300,000 to 1.

# Groupize

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Chelsea Salamone, Principal, Thayer Investment Partners; Ellen Keszler, CEO, Clear Sky Associates; Erik Blachford, Founder, Pine5 Ventures LLC; Kristina Carter, VP, Global Partnerships, Perk; Kurien Jacob, Partner, Highgate Technology Ventures; Michael Berk, Director of Strategic Innovation and Partnerships, American Express Global Business Travel; Pete Comeau, Managing Director, Phocuswright; Roger Sharp, Chairman, WebBeds Group and North Ridge Partners; Steve Singh, Executive Chairman and CEO, Spotnana; Traci Mercer, Chief Commercial Officer, Priceline; Charles De Gaspe Beaubien, Chief Commercial Officer, Groupize Inc.
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 2:53 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 3:02 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 1132
**Session ID:** `a35fc3f8-e43a-4246-8c52-7f081e256c26`

## Summary

Groupize pitched itself as a platform that helps corporations manage the spend, logistics, compliance, and travel for in-person meetings and events, with a particular focus on regulated industries such as life sciences and government, as well as guest travel. The pitch framed the core problem as the inefficiency of existing tools, which typically cap bookings at nine sleeping rooms, require 20 to 300 hours to run a meeting end to end, are still handled 80% in Excel, and must integrate with three to eight disparate corporate systems.

The addressable market was sized at $2 trillion, with groups, guests, and meetings representing 35% of the hotel industry; corporations were said to spend 1 to 5% of revenue on this category, ranging from $10 million for a typical corporation to $500 million for a life sciences company and $9 billion for the US government. The company noted it had won a Phocuswright innovation award 13 years earlier and has since built a business with roughly 120 marquee customers, many in regulated sectors, working alongside TMCs.

Eighteen months ago the company rebuilt its platform to be AI-native, centered on an orchestrating agent called Amy that coordinates 14 other agents to run meetings end to end, claiming 80–90% labor savings and 15–30% cost savings for corporations. MCP integrations are already in place, along with a first agent-to-agent integration with SAP. A live demo was planned showing a single prompt generating an event in Dublin for 50 new hires — including sourcing hotels and venues, building multilingual registration sites, booking room blocks and flights directly in Concur, and managing spend — though technical issues meant the accompanying video did not play as intended.

Revenue comes from corporate subscriptions and transaction fees, plus commissions, advertising, and an emerging payments line from a two-sided marketplace. On competition, Cvent was named as the main rival, expected to generate close to $1.5 billion in revenue this year despite only 5% market penetration, with the claim that Amy now outperforms it by being AI-native; newer entrants like BoomPop and Naboo were described as narrowly focused on sourcing, leaving the larger opportunity in shifting the market from Excel to agentic workflows. The team was positioned with deep domain experience — founders with 30 years in groups and meetings, a CTO who was ex-CIO of Boeing, and a head of sales who was ex-SVP at Concur — along with 13 years of integrations into hotel systems, OBTs, GDSs, and compliance systems, a presence across 75,000 corporations, and a newly received FedRAMP certification praised by the US government.

The company is raising a Series A to continue investing in product, integrations, and global go-to-market, and is now opening into the $2 trillion leisure market via white-label offerings for OTAs and event categories like weddings. In the feedback round, panelists called the problem real and the approach of applying deep domain expertise to an agentic model impressive, though one raised concern that incumbents with large existing corporate customer bases could move quickly to build similar AI capabilities, and another flagged that this could be a tough market from an investor standpoint even while agreeing the underlying problem and concept were sound.

## Key Takeaways

### 🛡️ Regulated industries are a core market

Groupize has built a customer base of roughly 120 marquee organizations, focusing on life sciences and the U.S. government. It says its deep compliance and enterprise integrations are reinforced by receiving official FedRAMP certification.

Quotes:

> We have some 120 marquee customers, big focus on regulated industry, the U.S. government... As of yesterday, we received our official FedRAMP certification.

### 📊 Meeting management is still largely manual

Despite the scale of the market, about 80% of meeting management is still handled in Excel, while a single end-to-end meeting can take 20 to 300 hours of work.

Quotes:

> It has to integrate to three to eight disparate systems in a company, and 80% is still done in Excel.

### 💳 Revenue comes from several transaction layers

Groupize’s business model combines corporate subscriptions and transaction fees with marketplace revenue from commissions and advertising, and it is expanding into payments.

Quotes:

> Groupize makes its money from subscriptions from the corporations and transaction fees, and also in our two-sided marketplace.

### 🔗 Enterprise connectivity is central to the platform

Groupize says it has MCPs in place and its first agent-to-agent integration with SAP in market, while the product also connects with systems such as Concur and existing corporate infrastructure.

Quotes:

> We actually have our first A to A with SAP in market.

### 🏨 AI handles sourcing and attendee operations

The proposed workflow includes shopping and negotiating with hotels, venues and restaurants, then creating registration sites, event apps and check-in functionality with support for speakers, sessions and tickets.

Quotes:

> She will shop the venues, the hotels, the restaurants; she will negotiate with them; she will create a complete registration website.

### 🗣️ One prompt can initiate event planning

The demonstrated workflow begins with a natural-language request—for example, setting up a Dublin event for 50 new hires—after which Amy asks clarifying questions and proceeds based on the confirmed assumptions.

Quotes:

> One prompt to Amy will do what would take one week for a human to do.

### 💰 Meetings represent major corporate spend

The session describes meetings as a substantial spending category: corporations may allocate 1% to 5% of revenue, with examples including $10 million for a corporation, $500 million for a life sciences company and $9 billion for the U.S. government.

Quotes:

> Corporations spend 1% to 5% of their revenues on this category.

### 🏢 Corporate meetings remain highly complex

Groupize positions corporate meetings as an operationally complex category spanning logistics, compliance and travel, particularly for customer-facing events and regulated industries.

Quotes:

> There’s nothing simple about a corporate meeting.

### ⚙️ Automation targets labor and savings

Groupize claims Amy can reduce meeting-related labor by 80% to 90% and help corporations save 15% to 30%, though these are presented as company claims rather than independently verified results.

Quotes:

> She saves 80% to 90% of the labor; she helps corporations save 15% to 30%.

### 🤖 Amy orchestrates an AI-native workflow

After rebuilding its platform over 18 months, Groupize now uses Amy as a supervising agent that coordinates 14 other agents to manage the meeting process end to end.

Quotes:

> Amy is a super agent that orchestrates with 14 other agents under the hood to do the end to end.

# TRIPCREATORAI (www.tripcreator.ai)

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Chelsea Salamone, Principal, Thayer Investment Partners; Ellen Keszler, CEO, Clear Sky Associates; Erik Blachford, Founder, Pine5 Ventures LLC; Kristina Carter, VP, Global Partnerships, Perk; Kurien Jacob, Partner, Highgate Technology Ventures; Michael Berk, Director of Strategic Innovation and Partnerships, American Express Global Business Travel; Pete Comeau, Managing Director, Phocuswright; Roger Sharp, Chairman, WebBeds Group and North Ridge Partners; Steve Singh, Executive Chairman and CEO, Spotnana; Traci Mercer, Chief Commercial Officer, Priceline; Marc Harrison, Founder & CEO, TRIPCREATORAI
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 3:02 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 3:11 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 1382
**Session ID:** `359ac7db-ae3d-44f0-8389-cbae9338b293`

## Summary

The session opened with an extended Marco Polo narrative used as a framing device: unlike Columbus, who planned a journey based on secondhand, unverified stories, Marco Polo actually traveled to the places he described. This was mapped onto today's AI landscape, where LLM-generated itineraries are compared to travel writing based on information read online rather than firsthand experience — itineraries that read beautifully but fall apart when a travel specialist tries to execute them, creating a 'nightmare' when a client brings in an unworkable 15-day plan.

The company's product, also named Marco Polo, was presented as a large travel model (LTM) rather than a general-purpose LLM — a B2B tool for tour operators and travel planners built so that operators retain ownership of their data and travel expertise. Instead of predicting the next word, the model determines the best sequence of travel events from a tour operator's own catalog, matched to traveler demographics, producing a cohesive, bookable itinerary. A live demo generated a five-day Tuscany itinerary in 14 seconds, with every point of interest tied to a catalog ID, precise coordinates, and verification against both Marco Polo's own database of over half a million places and the tour operator's catalog, with zero invented locations and lower cost than general AI generation.

The business case rested on a $271 billion tours-and-experiences market, two-thirds of which is still booked offline, with custom trips representing a $25 billion high-ticket segment of which $16.8 billion remains offline and dependent on archaic email-based workflows involving four to five rounds of back-and-forth between client and specialist. The pitch positioned the product as a top-of-funnel collaboration tool where traveler, agent, and operator shape experiences, flights, hotels, and transport together on one screen, offered through three paths: a developer API, an interactive bookable itinerary tool (Vista), and a customization layer matching a brand's voice (Atlas). The long-term vision is an interactive map as the medium for collaborative trip planning, eventually allowing travelers to get feedback from friends and family and to talk to and modify itineraries conversationally, with six brands already live in 2026.

In the Q&A, panelists pushed on whether the company was really building two separate products — a developer-facing model and a crowded application layer. It was clarified that the tool does not itself book trips; booking stays with whatever system an operator already uses, while itineraries can be uploaded and shown interactively, with client communication embedded directly in the itinerary rather than in email. On future focus, the plan is to work with tour operator brands and DMCs and their travel agent networks, concentrating API development on the large travel model as the basis for trip planning and the mapping layer.

Asked what stops general LLMs from catching up and building an equivalent large travel model, the response acknowledged that serving small, medium, and large travel-specific models using operators' own data is something anyone could do, citing a recent comment that 'there is no moat,' and argued that the real defensibility comes from being first to market and from the distribution advantage of the interactive itinerary, with the model itself being a matter of execution and speed. A further question about whether the model was distilled from existing large language models was answered firmly in the negative: the system is described as a transformer model built from scratch using the company's own point-of-interest database and tour operators' data, not a distillation of general-purpose LLMs.

## Key Takeaways

### 🔌 The platform offers three ways to adopt it

Travel companies can build on the API, use interactive bookable itineraries, or create and customize trips using their own itineraries and brand voice. The company says the interactive itinerary and its distribution may provide the stronger moat than the model alone.

Quotes:

> There are three ways then to work with us: developers just build on top of our API; Vista your trips in an interactive itinerary, bookable atlas; trip creation and customization based on your itineraries and your brand's voice... We believe that the distribution, so the side of actually the interactive itinerary, is where our moat comes from.

### 🏢 Operators retain their data and expertise

Marco Polo is positioned as a B2B tool in which tour operators and travel planners retain ownership of their data and travel expertise instead of relying solely on general AI knowledge.

Quotes:

> It's a B2B tool for the itinerary creation and proprietary model for tour operators where you own your data and the travel expertise, not the general AI.

### 🔗 It sequences bookable travel events

Rather than predicting the next best words, the system determines the best sequence of travel events from a tour operator's catalog and then turns that sequence into a cohesive itinerary.

Quotes:

> Marco Polo doesn't predict the next best words; it determines the best sequence of travel events based on a tour operator's catalog.

### 🛡️ Guardrails preserve traveler choice while keeping trips sellable

The system gives clients the impression of choice within constraints defined by the operator, so the resulting trip can still be shaped, quoted, and sold.

Quotes:

> To the client it's the illusion of choice with guardrails of things that the tour operator can only book.

### 🎯 The top of the funnel connects intent to sellable inventory

Marco Polo brings the traveler, agent, and operator together while shaping flights, hotels, transport, and experiences around the operator's real catalog, turning a request into a trip the operator can quote and sell.

Quotes:

> At the top of the funnel on one screen: the traveler, the agent and the operator's product shaping it together—the experience, the flights, the hotels and the transport in between.

### 🧭 Travel AI must be grounded in verified experience

The presentation contrasts general-purpose LLMs, which may rely on assumptions and unverified web data, with a system grounded in verified places and operator inventory so itineraries work in practice.

Quotes:

> AI and LLM wrappers plan like Columbus, based on assumptions and unverified data.

### 🧠 Marco Polo is a travel-specific model built from scratch

Marco Polo is presented as a large travel model built from scratch using the company's POI database and tour operators' data, rather than distilled from general-purpose language models.

Quotes:

> It's a transformer model built from scratch using POIs that we have on our own database and using basically tour operators' data. It's not a distilled model from the large language models.

### 🏷️ Each itinerary point carries structured provenance

The demonstrated itinerary includes a catalog ID, meter-level coordinates, and verification from both Marco Polo and the tour operator catalog.

Quotes:

> First of all it has a catalog ID, second it has coordinates down to the meter and third is verified by both Marco Polo and the tour operator catalog.

### ⚡ Itinerary creation can happen in seconds

The demonstration generated a customized Tuscany itinerary in 14 seconds, showing a major speed advantage over manually assembling comparable trips.

Quotes:

> Five days in Tuscany—two in Florence, Siena and San Gimignano... 14 seconds.

### 📧 Custom-trip planning is still highly manual

The company targets a workflow in which a client request typically arrives by email or form and requires four to five rounds of revisions and 15 to 20 emails between the traveler and specialist.

Quotes:

> A request comes in via an email or a form, then there's four to five iterations back and forth—15 to 20 emails between the client and the travel specialist, if not more.

# Swivul

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Chelsea Salamone, Principal, Thayer Investment Partners; Ellen Keszler, CEO, Clear Sky Associates; Erik Blachford, Founder, Pine5 Ventures LLC; Kristina Carter, VP, Global Partnerships, Perk; Kurien Jacob, Partner, Highgate Technology Ventures; Michael Berk, Director of Strategic Innovation and Partnerships, American Express Global Business Travel; Pete Comeau, Managing Director, Phocuswright; Roger Sharp, Chairman, WebBeds Group and North Ridge Partners; Steve Singh, Executive Chairman and CEO, Spotnana; Traci Mercer, Chief Commercial Officer, Priceline; Sarah Figueroa, Co-Founder/CEO, Swivul Inc
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 3:11 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 3:20 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 1236
**Session ID:** `0918b24e-1ca2-46c7-980e-a1915bdd463d`

## Summary

The pitch opened with a problem drawn from personal experience running a startup: every department wanted to travel for its own reasons, and all spending decisions about whether a trip was worth taking landed on one desk, with no good way to judge value against cost or to know afterward what the trip had actually accomplished.

Swivul was introduced as a travel and entertainment operating system built around an AI concierge called Sky, designed to close the gap between cost and value by connecting internal company data (CRM, calendar, preferences, goals) with external opportunities (conferences, customers, partners). Rather than waiting for someone to request a trip, Sky proactively surfaces travel worth taking, illustrated by a claim that it had identified millions in pipeline and multiple high-value meetings tied to the very conference where the pitch was delivered.

A demo walked through planning an F1 Miami trip, showing Sky identifying an unmentioned multimillion-dollar CRM opportunity and building an entire itinerary around closing it, sourcing passes, rooms, dinners, and transport across the GDS, connected APIs, and local concierges into a single checkout. After the trip, Sky was shown closing the loop by checking CRM and calendar data to see whether meetings happened and opportunities advanced, producing trip attribution without added work, and then rolling this up across the whole travel program to show which trips created pipeline, advanced deals, or retained customers, analyzed like an investment portfolio to guide future spend.

On the business model, Swivul takes a blended roughly 12% take rate on supplier margins and markups plus a company subscription, claims to move companies from intent to checkout five times faster than leading travel platforms, and says its differentiation beyond standard booking, approvals, and receipts lies in its intelligence layer connecting travel decisions to business outcomes. The company cited Mercury as a partner bringing its 300,000+ business customers, pointed to its own use of the product (Sky recommending a conference pitch that won a people's choice award), and reported over 50 active enterprises in its pipeline.

Panel reaction was largely positive but zeroed in on the 12% take rate on gross bookings as rich, prompting questions about who actually pays it; the response pointed to high-margin, hard-to-price-online items like private jets for VIP clients as the driver of margin above a typical OTA. Panelists also pressed on differentiation from a CRM with a connected booking engine and on defensibility against agent-based platforms using connectors, with the response emphasizing that fragmented trip costs (dinners, entertainment outside the travel program) are hard to capture elsewhere, that Swivul is itself agent-based with MCP support, and that its value is centralizing understanding of travel intent and ROI. A final question raised that not every trip delivers a measurable financial outcome, drawing audience laughter about the very conference they were attending, before the session closed with applause.

## Key Takeaways

### 💵 Swivul monetizes through blended travel margins

Swivul combines a company subscription with a 12% blended take rate on supplier margins and markups, with higher-margin services such as private jets and exclusive VIP access helping differentiate its economics from a traditional online travel agency.

Quotes:

> We have a blended take rate markup and commission, but we have things that you can't buy online, so we do things like private jets for the VIPs at Kraken... That's what gets our margin to go up significantly rather than a traditional OTA.

### 🤖 AI can recommend trips before employees ask

Rather than waiting for a traveler to request a destination, Swivul analyzes company context and proactively recommends where someone should go and why.

Quotes:

> Most platforms wait for me to say I need to be in Florida. Swivul says you should be in Florida and tells me why.

### ⚡ Move from travel intent to checkout faster

Swivul claims that its integrated workflow for bookings, approvals, policies, and receipts takes employees from intent to checkout five times faster than leading travel platforms.

Quotes:

> We can take companies and their employees from intent to checkout 5 times faster than leading travel platforms.

### 🔄 Improve policies and recommendations with every trip

Swivul uses accumulated travel data to help companies refine travel policies and make smarter recommendations over time.

Quotes:

> The system learns. We help companies by creating smarter policies and improving recommendations with every single trip.

### 🛒 Unify the full business trip in one workflow

Swivul brings flights, lodging, transportation, dining, events, and other business-critical experiences into one intelligent cart and checkout, helping companies measure the trip’s full cost rather than isolated travel expenses.

Quotes:

> Swivul brings all of that in into one intelligent cart, and that's how we can measure the entire cost of the trip, not just the flight and the hotel.

### 📈 Conferences can be evaluated as pipeline opportunities

The platform can identify the potential commercial value of an event, including pipeline and high-value meetings that were not previously on the user's radar.

Quotes:

> Sky found 3 million dollars worth of pipeline in the room and 15 high value meetings as well as this pitch.

### 💰 Cost does not equal business value

The cheapest option may not be the best investment when missed meetings, transportation, and lost opportunities are included in the calculation.

Quotes:

> A $700 hotel across the street from three critical meetings can create more value than a $400 hotel once you account for missed meetings, rides and the people you never got to meet.

### 🎯 Plan the entire trip around advancing a deal

When given a trip's participants and purpose, Sky can use CRM context to structure the experience around a specific opportunity and its stage in the sales process.

Quotes:

> Sky identifies a $10 million opportunity in my CRM currently in negotiation with the goal of moving to close one and then she builds the entire experience around closing it.

### 🧭 Travel decisions should start with business purpose

Swivul frames business travel as a question of whether a trip should happen and what it should accomplish—not simply how to book it.

Quotes:

> What you just watched looked like a story about booking a trip—it wasn't. It was about deciding whether that trip should happen in the first place.

### ✅ Post-trip workflows can close the measurement loop

After travel, Sky checks whether meetings occurred and whether related opportunities progressed, using CRM and calendar data without requiring additional work from the team.

Quotes:

> After the trip Sky closes the loop: did the meetings happen, did the opportunities move?

# Executive Interview: Building the Next Generation of Travel

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Pete Comeau, Managing Director, Phocuswright; Steve Singh, Executive Chairman and CEO, Spotnana
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 3:20 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 3:40 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 3165
**Session ID:** `5d980396-885a-4752-bd20-b1f2b276399b`

## Summary

The session opened with an executive interview reflecting on the pitches just seen on stage, framed around how to build the next generation of travel. The guest, who co-founded and led Concur before its sale to SAP and now works with Madrona as a travel investor while running another startup, said he was having the time of his life riding the AI wave, praising the founders who had pitched for their courage, but stressed that what matters most is what founders take from the panelists' questions rather than their own answers. What keeps him up at night is the industry's tendency to lose sight of who the real customer is: the individual travel buyer and, equally important but historically underserved, the travel seller, with everyone else occupying a shrinking middle layer that needs to be clear about the value it adds.

On the day's pitches, the feedback was that too many startups are trying to solve too many problems at once rather than nailing one; the advice was to pick a single problem and own it. Beyond front-end innovation, the infrastructure layer of travel is also being rebuilt, and without that rebuild new ventures risk hitting a wall imposed by legacy channel and business models. Three of the companies on stage were singled out as ones worth backing, provided they tighten their focus.

A major thread was the current wave of agentic travel agents, which was described as repeating the industry's historic obsession with the 5% of the journey spent buying rather than the 95% spent traveling, where most dissatisfaction and cost actually occur. United's recent decision to shut off agentic agents was read as evidence that bombarding supplier sites with purchase requests only pushes cost onto human service agents without driving incremental revenue, since the booking already belongs to the supplier. The conclusion was that agentic agents need to become trusted gateways into content and purchasing and deliver value beyond transactions, or they will not survive. The same conference-wide fascination with AI prompted a separate question about what actually distinguishes an AI-native company from one that has simply added AI to its pitch deck; the answer centered on reimagining workflows without being anchored to legacy processes, and on using real user data and behavior to improve the experience, rather than just layering AI onto old systems. Legacy technology was framed as worth understanding but not worth being bound by, drawing a direct parallel to how expense reporting itself was reinvented rather than built on top of what already existed.

On corporate travel and fintech convergence, the discussion covered fintechs like Ramp and Brex (and Capital One's acquisition of Brex) reinventing the expense economics that once underpinned Concur, subsidizing software costs in exchange for card transaction volume, a shift praised as a lesson that no company can rest on its model. The content and distribution model is also moving away from reliance on GDS infrastructure, which is breaking automated, predictive servicing capabilities that need to be rebuilt. Knowing the traveler was framed as requiring a much richer data graph than travel history alone, built in a way that earns durable trust rather than simply monetizing data, since trust is what retains customers long-term.

Looking at the broader vision behind the guest's current venture, the goal remains delivering a 'perfect trip' infrastructure, originally conceived for corporate travel but now extended toward leisure, enabled by cloud-native, integrated data architectures that didn't exist a decade ago but still largely don't exist industry-wide today. The consumer opportunity was judged larger than corporate, with the two experiences converging except for duty of care and policy compliance, which remain corporate travel's distinguishing requirements. The session closed with advice to founders to hold a broad vision and architecture while concentrating immediate execution on solving one problem so well that a customer cannot live without it.

## Key Takeaways

### 🎯 Solve one specific customer problem

Travel startups should define exactly whom they serve and focus tightly on one specific problem rather than pursuing an overly broad scope.

Quotes:

> “This is too much of what I saw today: we're trying to solve too many problems, too broad of a scope. Pick one problem, nail that problem.”

### 🤝 Treat travel sellers as a second customer

Travel sellers are another important customer group, and the industry has not served them well enough.

Quotes:

> “There is a second customer and in my view that second customer has not been well served. That's the travel seller.”

### 🧠 Use a trusted, richer data graph of the traveler

Understanding a traveler requires more than travel history or TMC records. Platforms should combine broader information about the individual to understand decisions across different personas, then use that data to improve the experience and earn long-term trust rather than primarily drive revenue.

Quotes:

> “I want to know more about your life and then really build a data graph on that individual in a way that understands the decisions they make in the different personas in which they operate… If we use that information to go drive a better experience for the customer that we're trying to serve, that trust is everything.”

### 💳 Expect card economics to reshape corporate travel software

Card companies can use travel and expense software to drive more transactions, potentially making software heavily subsidized and overturning the traditional corporate-travel economics associated with standalone platforms.

Quotes:

> “The equation that Concur operated with is no longer valid… it's going to be reinvented in an economic model where the software costs are massively subsidized.”

### 🧳 Put the traveler at the center

Singh argues that the individual consuming travel services—not only the corporate buyer or organizational intermediary—should be recognized as the primary customer.

Quotes:

> “For me in the travel space I think we should identify that the travel buyer, the individual, the person who's actually consuming these travel services, they're the customer.”

### 🛠️ Build an open infrastructure for the perfect trip

Travel innovation will stall without modern infrastructure: an integrated, open data model and systems that communicate across suppliers, channels and service operations. As content moves beyond the GDS, the industry must also reinvent how suppliers differentiate and merchandise content and how travel is serviced digitally, proactively and predictively.

Quotes:

> “The concept of the perfect trip shouldn't exist just for the corporate environment… it requires… an entirely integrated data model, an integrated set of systems that actually talk to one another in a completely open model.”

### 🔄 Make AI-native products rethink the workflow

An AI-native company should not simply add AI to an existing product. It should reimagine the underlying process by questioning legacy workflows and using user data to make the experience fundamentally better.

Quotes:

> “There's also a reimagining of that particular process. Like, how can a particular process be delivered in a world where you don't have to think about the legacy workflows?”

### 🌍 Bring consumer delight into corporate travel

Consumer travel is the larger opportunity, and corporate and leisure experiences are converging. The main distinctions that remain for corporate travel are duty of care, policy, compliance and negotiated requirements—not the basic expectation of a seamless, enjoyable experience.

Quotes:

> “The experiences between corporate and consumer are getting closer and closer together… the difference between corporate and consumer is going to be duty of care, understanding corporate policy and compliance… but the rest of the experience should be just as delightful as what you can get in a consumer experience.”

### 🧑‍💼 Build specialized agents around trusted context

The market will include many category-specific agents alongside broad assistants. Travel agents should use deep, trusted knowledge of a traveler’s preferences and circumstances—like an exceptional executive assistant—to deliver specialized value at far lower cost.

Quotes:

> “There'll be lots of them… you'll have specialized agents… pick a category, be exceptional in that, but you're a subcategory to the broader-based agents.”

### 🧳 Focus innovation on the trip, not the booking

Most travel innovation targets the small purchase window, even though the larger source of customer frustration occurs throughout the journey—from leaving home until returning.

Quotes:

> “The vast majority of innovation is on how you sell… Very little innovation is on how do you actually service that. Most of the problems that you have in a trip are not in the 5% of the time to actually buy something; it's in the 95% of the time from the moment you leave your front door through the trip and all the way back.”

# Phocuswright Research: Who Helps Travelers Decide? AI, Advisors and the New Planning Mix

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Alicia Schmid, Director of Research, Phocuswright
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 4:10 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 4:25 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 2271
**Session ID:** `f522099c-aaf9-4d2a-a5a7-92a1402c4d77`

## Summary

The session opened with a sponsor presentation on Crewfare, a platform built around the idea that the ticket, not the hotel, is now the strongest signal in travel. Crewfare argued that once a fan buys a ticket to an event like Formula 1 Miami or Lollapalooza, they are currently left to plan the rest of the trip alone, and that hotels misprice rooms around event dates while losing revenue and data because nobody tracks where ticket buyers actually come from or what they would have booked. Crewfare's product suite integrates hotels, tickets, transportation and activities at the point of ticket purchase, working directly with leagues, promoters and festivals so travel is part of the experience from the first click rather than something fans figure out afterward, with examples including Lollapalooza, Ultra and NHL events.

The main research presentation, drawn from new Phocuswright Consumer Travel and Travel Advisor survey data, set the scene with three numbers: 52% of US travelers used AI for trip planning in the past year, 21% called or visited an advisor to book at least one trip component, and 73% of advisors now use GenAI themselves, up from 59% a year earlier. The core finding was that AI sits at the top of the planning funnel, sparking slightly more trip ideas than advisors (7% versus 5%) and running neck-and-neck with advisors in research (21% versus 23%), but its role collapses at booking, where AI accounts for only about 2% of actual bookings versus roughly 21% for advisors, even though 95% of AI users said it influenced at least one trip decision. AI was described as functioning more as a copilot than a booking channel.

Four common industry assumptions were tested and each failed to hold. First, AI is not pulling travelers away from advisors; advisor clients actually use AI more and trust it more, but are also more likely to say it falls short for specific or advanced needs, meaning advisor clients are experienced AI users who have hit its limits. Second, advisor clients are not simply older travelers: advisors perceive 45% of clients as 56+, but traveler data shows only 25%, since younger travelers book flights and hotels through advisors in small, easily-overlooked transactions while older cruisers dominate advisor revenue and perception. Third, booking is not moving inside AI any time soon: 43% of AI-familiar travelers say they would book inside an AI platform, but only about 2% (rising to roughly 8% once Google's AI mode is included) actually did, with travelers still preferring OTAs and direct supplier sites because of accountability if something goes wrong. Fourth, trust is not an advisor-only advantage; overall trust in AI and advisors is tied at 38%, but this masks a generational split, with Gen Z and millennials trusting AI more and Gen X and boomers favoring advisors.

On the advisor side, GenAI adoption has climbed sharply, from 41% in 2024 to 73% this year, led by newer advisors with five years or less experience, who are far more eager to expand AI use than those with 20+ years in the business. Advisors overwhelmingly want AI for marketing content and itinerary building rather than client-facing tools; only 17% want to offer clients a chatbot, and two-thirds of agency owners have no plans to do so, preferring to remain the face of the relationship. The main payoff reported is time saved on admin and personalization, though a third of advisors, and half of those with 20+ years, say AI has not yet meaningfully changed their work, and many report spending time correcting client-brought AI misinformation. Both advisors and travelers draw a similar line: comfort with AI monitoring bookings in the background is high, but willingness to let AI book or change a reservation unsupervised is very low (3% of advisors, with travelers split roughly 37% yes to 39% no).

The presentation closed by flagging how fast the landscape is shifting even since the surveys closed, with Google, Meta, Expedia and Booking Holdings all adding AI booking features that nonetheless still route responsibility through a known brand or person, matching what travelers and advisors say they want. The recommended takeaways were that advisors should optimize for AI-driven search, court younger travelers who already book with them, and position themselves to validate and improve AI-built plans rather than compete with them; OTAs and suppliers should aim to be the trusted place travelers complete bookings after AI research; and travel tech should build AI that assists and then hands off to a person or brand. The overarching conclusion was that AI and people both help travelers decide, often on the same trip, with AI shaping the plan and people still making the booking, and that the gap between the 43% who say they would book inside AI and the 2% who actually do is the key metric to watch for when that balance shifts.

## Key Takeaways

### ✅ Human approval remains the boundary for autonomous travel AI

Both advisors and travelers are more comfortable with AI researching, assisting and monitoring trips than independently booking or changing them. Accuracy, personal touch and accountability concerns make approval at the point of purchase a persistent requirement.

Quotes:

> On both sides, AI is welcome to research and assist. People still want to approve the booking.

### 📣 Advisors must show up where AI starts planning

Because AI now sparks more trip ideas than advisors, advisors should optimize for AI-driven discovery and prepare to improve plans clients bring from AI. Validating and personalizing those plans can help advisors earn the booking instead of competing with the initial recommendation.

Quotes:

> The advisors who validate and improve upon that plan instead of competing with it, will earn the booking.

### 🛡️ Responsibility remains a booking barrier for AI

Although 43% of travelers familiar with AI say they would book inside an AI platform, current behavior is much lower because travelers want clarity about who is responsible when something goes wrong. That unresolved trust and accountability gap is central to future distribution shifts.

Quotes:

> AI shapes the decision, and then the travelers leave to book somewhere they already know and trust. Online travel agencies are still the most common way to book flights and hotels, followed directly through suppliers.

### 📊 Disconnected booking obscures valuable traveler data

When organizers and suppliers do not connect ticket and travel behavior, they may not know who attended, where they came from or what additional products they would have purchased.

Quotes:

> Nobody knows who came, from where, and what they would have bought unless somebody asked.

### 🤖 AI is moving to the top of the travel funnel

AI increasingly shapes trip ideas and research before travelers reach supplier or OTA sites: 52% of U.S. travelers used AI for trips in the past year, while 21% used it to research trips—up from 6% two years ago. Businesses need to be visible during this early decision-making stage.

Quotes:

> AI now sits at the very top of the funnel, shaping shortlists before travelers reach your site or app, and it’s moving fast.

### 🏨 Events create major revenue swings for hotels

Hotels that price only from normal seasonal patterns can miss the demand created by major events. Event-linked pricing and distribution can capture substantially higher rates when demand changes the economics of a destination.

Quotes:

> When an event came to the city that warranted the price difference, hotels were not priced at $200 a night. They were priced at $600, $800, $1,000.

### 💳 AI influences decisions more than it closes bookings

Travelers frequently act on AI recommendations, but actual booking behavior remains far behind stated interest: 95% of AI users made at least one trip decision based on AI, while only about 2% said AI was their booking channel—roughly 8% when Google AI Mode is included. Travelers still leave AI to book with OTAs or suppliers they know.

Quotes:

> 95% of AI users made at least one trip decision based on what AI told them... But only 9% said AI was the tool they relied on most. So it's really kind of just a copilot.

### ⚙️ Advisors want AI to improve the work behind the relationship

Advisors are most interested in AI for emails, marketing, website content, itineraries and package curation—not for replacing the advisor in client conversations. The opportunity is to automate routine work while preserving the advisor as the face of the relationship.

Quotes:

> Advisors want AI for marketing, and itineraries, far more than for client-facing chatbots.

### 🧭 Younger travelers are an underdeveloped advisor opportunity

Advisor businesses may overestimate how old their clients are: only 25% of travelers who booked through an advisor are 55 or older, versus advisors’ estimate that 45% of clients are 56-plus. Younger travelers already use advisors for some products, but Gen Z and millennials trust AI more, so advisors must actively build credibility with them.

Quotes:

> Younger travelers are already walking through the door, most advisors are just not building a business around them yet.

### 🤝 Advisors and AI are emerging as collaborators, not replacements

AI has not displaced travel advisors: 21% of travelers used an advisor to book at least one trip component, 23% used one for research, and 73% of advisors now use GenAI, up from 59% a year earlier. AI increasingly shapes the plan while advisors validate, personalize and take responsibility for the booking.

Quotes:

> The answer is both AI and people. And very often it's for the same trip. AI shapes the plan, people still make the booking.

### 🎟️ Build the trip around the event ticket

The ticket is a powerful signal of a traveler’s destination, timing and intent. Event organizers and travel partners can use it to integrate hotels, transportation and activities from the first click, rather than leaving fans to assemble the trip afterward.

Quotes:

> We start where the fans do, at the ticket... Travel is an integrated part of the experience from the very first click, not something that the fan has to figure out afterward.

## Idea Cloud

- AI Shapes Discovery but Rarely Owns Booking (score: 391)
- AI and Advisors Share the Planning Journey (score: 371)
- AI Moves Travel Competition to the Top of the Funnel (score: 361)
- The Ticket Is Travel's Strongest Signal (score: 339)
- Event Travel Needs One Integrated Journey (score: 334)
- Events Reshape Hotel Economics (score: 295)
- The Fan Experience Determines Event Loyalty (score: 290)
- Event Platforms Coordinate the Entire Ecosystem (score: 236)

# Executive Panel: The New Travel Advisor - Designing the Human + AI Model

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Alicia Schmid, Director of Research, Phocuswright; Evan Konwiser, Chief Product & Strategy Officer, American Express Global Business Travel; Henley Vazquez, Co-Founder, Fora Travel; Matthew Upchurch, Chairman and CEO, Virtuoso
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 4:25 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 4:50 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 4435
**Session ID:** `1aa71d65-51ae-400b-a8b0-93b050823595`

## Summary


## Key Takeaways

### 🤝 Combine AI speed with human trust

The panel sees the strongest travel model as a partnership: AI provides speed, information and discovery, while humans provide trust, judgment and care.

Quotes:

> The meeting in the middle of having the trust of the human with the speed of AI is kind of the right spot to be in.

### 🛫 Human support matters most when plans break

AI can provide useful travel information, but disruptions such as ground stops require a human who can coordinate rebooking, hotel extensions and next steps.

Quotes:

> Who’s going to extend your hotel? Who’s going to check when you can get on another flight? Who’s handling all of those things while we’re all here in a meeting?

### 🧑‍💻 Routine AI support frees humans for complexity

AI can resolve large volumes of straightforward advisor-support requests without reducing satisfaction, allowing human teams to concentrate on exceptional, ambiguous or high-stakes problems that require judgment.

Quotes:

> Our internal support ticket system runs on AI. We are answering over 40,000 inquiries every month with an over 90% satisfaction rating... that other set that really is complicated allows our support team to get involved.

### 🔄 Post-trip debriefs turn service into learning

An advisor’s value extends beyond booking and in-trip support: the quality of the post-trip debrief can distinguish a trusted advisor from a transactional agent by turning each journey into a deeper learning relationship.

Quotes:

> The single biggest differentiator that the customers themselves told us was the difference between a transactional travel agent and a trusted travel advisor was the quality of the post-trip debrief. And it becoming a learning experience.

### ⚠️ Verify AI against current expert data

AI can produce confidently incorrect travel information when it relies on generic or outdated web content, so expert-curated, current industry data and human judgment are essential for trustworthy answers.

Quotes:

> It told me A and the answer was B... It didn’t understand that they moved terminals six months ago... I think we have to raise the trust bar.

### 💳 Corporate trust is the next adoption barrier

For agentic AI to book and spend at scale, corporate buyers must be comfortable giving it fiduciary authority over company funds; the remaining barrier is institutional trust and control, not simply technology.

Quotes:

> That’s a CFO saying I’m allowing agentic to spend my money... I don’t know that CFOs are at the point where they’re ready to let AI at scale spend their money on their behalf.

### 📈 Embedded AI can multiply advisor productivity

When AI is integrated into an advisor platform, it can compress tasks such as creating client cards, drafting itineraries, producing quotes, analyzing business performance and answering routine support questions from hours to seconds. The panel linked active AI use with substantially higher advisor volume.

Quotes:

> Something that would have taken me an hour will take me 60 seconds... 96% of our top advisors are using Via on a daily basis... their volume is five times the ones that aren’t so active with AI.

### 🧠 AI cannot replace judgment and taste

AI can accelerate research and execution, but advisors still provide the judgment, taste, personal style and relationship-based understanding that shape a luxury client’s experience.

Quotes:

> I think there’s judgment. There’s taste... people deal with a particular advisor not because they’re technically great but because of their style.

### 💬 Human voice is becoming a premium

As AI increasingly handles service interactions, a genuinely human voice—and clarity about whether a caller is speaking with AI—will become part of the premium travel experience.

Quotes:

> Human voice the other line is a luxury good... I think travelers can and should tell the difference.

### 🔐 Advisors want automation with control

Advisors are open to AI handling booking, especially when they can monitor the process and retain clear intervention points rather than surrendering control to a traditional booking engine.

Quotes:

> The number one thing our advisor said they would actually like to use AI for is booking. But they have an assumption going in a booking that I can monitor a booking that I have access to.

### 💎 AI raises the value of expertise

AI makes information and basic knowledge easier to access, but high-quality expertise remains costly because it depends on curated data, judgment and experience rather than generic speed.

Quotes:

> Asking ChatGPT what’s the best hotel in London very quickly every single one will be in the heart of London... Good actual intelligence is quite expensive.

## Idea Cloud

- Human support matters during disruption (score: 362)
- Advisors sell relationships, not bookings (score: 347)
- Human judgment makes AI useful (score: 340)

- Agentic travel needs corporate trust (score: 317)
- AI expands advisor productivity (score: 314)
- AI makes expertise more valuable (score: 289)
- AI improves discovery and preparation (score: 280)
- AI must reveal its human imitation (score: 280)

# Changemaker: Allianz Partners (Sponsored by Allianz Partners)

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Bart Welch, President & Chief Commercial Officer, Boundless Labs (Cruisebound); Jeffrey Wright, Managing Director, North America, Allianz Partners; Robert Cole, Senior Research Analyst, Lodging and Leisure Travel, Phocuswright Inc.
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 4:50 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 5:00 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 2056
**Session ID:** `d9e1e1d8-ca2f-4ce9-94c3-e235adde3105`

## Summary

The session opened as an informal bar-side conversation between representatives of Allianz Partners, the global leader in travel insurance running the US and Canadian business, and Boundless Labs, a company operating primarily in the cruise and complex-travel space that is building a fully functioning online cruise booking platform designed to make complicated travel products feel bookable with confidence.

Discussion turned to how to reduce friction in selling something as complex as travel insurance inside a B2B2C booking flow, where consumers are visiting partner sites to book core travel, not insurance. Allianz described its Core Fusion technology, trained on two billion quotes a year, which consumes itinerary and consumer data to surface a relevant, simplified insurance offer rather than overwhelming travelers with choice—for example, not pushing comprehensive cancellation coverage on a refundable air ticket, or highlighting electronic device protection for travelers with kids. The goal is to support conversion rather than slow it down, since partners need shoppers to move quickly into the booking cart.

On the cruise side, the conversation highlighted how uncommoditized and complex cruise products are—differing ship by ship, with varied deposit schedules and countless add-on decisions around dining, excursions, and entertainment—and how travel technology has largely failed to address this complexity. Boundless Labs noted that this gap created the opportunity to build its platform, which now sees 91% of bookings completed without any human touch, and that the company has recently pivoted toward a B2B model to bring this frictionless experience to established brands building their own e-commerce journeys.

The panel then explored how AI agents are shifting from search and comparison tools toward recommendation and transactional roles, and what that means for an industry built around human-consumable booking paths. Allianz raised the risk that agents trained primarily on speed and price might never surface travel insurance at all, illustrated by an anecdote about a personal agent who had never proactively mentioned travel insurance until prompted, and who offered to stop mentioning it altogether. At the same time, there was seen to be real opportunity: unlike humans, AI agents can digest large volumes of coverage details, exclusions, and comparisons instantly, potentially enabling an assisted-sale model where quality and reliable delivery on promises become the deciding factor as more choice becomes feasible to present.

On the cruise-specific AI outlook, it was noted that full AI booking agents for cruise remain further off than in other travel verticals given the product's complexity, but AI is already proving valuable for discovery and cross-sell—feeding content to AI tools that shape opinions before consumers reach the site, and using AI-informed testing and merchandising to grow joint travel-protection attach rates by 70% year over year, which also eases customer service when cruise disruptions occur, such as ships departing without delayed passengers. Closing remarks affirmed that AI tools benefit both human and AI agents, with human agents backed by AI assistance since no person can recall the details of 30,000 individual sailings, underscoring a symbiotic relationship between the two companies' technologies.

## Key Takeaways

### 🤖 Agentic commerce creates training risks and new opportunities

As AI evolves from search and comparison toward recommendation and transactions, agents may omit travel insurance if they are trained mainly for speed and price. But they can also instantly process coverage, exclusions and comparisons, creating opportunities for assisted recommendations and making product quality more important.

Quotes:

> The risk is that agents that have been trained maybe in speed and price maybe will never offer travel insurance. The opportunity is that agents can digest all of the coverages, exclusions and comparison shop kind of instantly.

### 🎯 Relevance can prevent unnecessary coverage

Insurance recommendations should reflect what the traveler has already purchased; for example, a refundable air ticket may make comprehensive trip-cancellation coverage unnecessary.

Quotes:

> If you’re buying a refundable air ticket, you don’t need a comprehensive trip-cancellation product.

### 📱 Simplified protection must fit the core booking journey

Travelers visit partner websites and apps to book trips, not to shop for insurance. Protection therefore needs to be presented in a small amount of digital space, with complex coverage simplified enough to support rather than disrupt the primary conversion.

Quotes:

> People are going to those websites and mobile apps not to buy travel insurance. They’re there for core booking. Our challenge is to take something as complex as travel insurance—we have dozens of covered reasons—and really simplify it.

### 🧠 AI agents may need a different booking experience

Travel companies built around human UI and UX may need separate booking paths for AI agents, with information structured for machine decision-making rather than only for human browsing.

Quotes:

> You may have two separate booking paths. One for an agent. One for a human. You’ve got to be able to give data to the agent.

### 📈 AI can boost protection attach rates and service outcomes

AI-assisted discovery and cross-sell experimentation are helping Allianz and Boundless grow travel-protection attachment, with the speakers reporting 70% year-over-year growth in attach rate. Protection also makes customer service easier when cruise disruptions occur.

Quotes:

> Through those tests and through really helping feed those models, our attach rate on our joint products has grown 70% year over year. If something goes wrong with the cruise and some of these products, it makes our customer services job a lot easier.

### 🚢 Cruise booking is high-value and highly complex

Cruises are not commoditized products: travelers compare different ships and manage deposits, dining, excursions, entertainment and other add-ons, often making a high-ticket purchase with substantial decision complexity.

Quotes:

> Cruise tends to be one of the highest ticket items in travel today and probably the most complex. Cruises are not commoditized.

### 🧑‍💻 AI can augment human cruise agents

AI tools can help human agents manage the scale and complexity of cruise inventory, including thousands of ships and sailings, rather than replacing human assistance altogether.

Quotes:

> We do provide them with AI tools as well because a human cannot remember the details of a single ship, let alone 30,000 sailings that we have on our platform.

### 🤝 Complex-travel technology is moving into B2B partnerships

Boundless Labs has pivoted toward partnering with established brands so they can incorporate a more seamless cruise-booking experience into their own e-commerce journeys.

Quotes:

> We recently pivoted pretty strongly to the B2B space so that we can partner with established brands to bring that experience to them.

### 🤖 Core Fusion personalizes insurance offers

Allianz’s Core Fusion technology uses itinerary data, consumer data and learned preferences to recommend a travel-protection product that is more relevant to the customer and booking segment.

Quotes:

> It consumes itinerary data, consumer data, learns about preferences and then really is giving an offer for a product that the consumer will value.

### ⚡ Frictionless booking can automate most cruise sales

Boundless Labs reports that 91% of its bookings do not require human assistance, demonstrating the potential for a seamless digital flow even in complex travel categories.

Quotes:

> 91% of our bookings do not touch a human.

# Presentation + Executive Panel: From Inspiration to Impact

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Erik Blachford, Founder, Pine5 Ventures LLC; Jeremy Jauncey, Founder, CEO, Beautiful Destinations; Lynn Carpenter, Senior Vice President of Marketing, Visit California; Tsega Worku, Vertical Director of Auto & Travel, TikTok
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 5:05 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 5:35 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 4517
**Session ID:** `2734e67f-e093-40be-81a7-a1821215888c`

## Summary

The session opened with framing for why inspiring travelers is especially hard right now: geopolitical turmoil, a record El Niño causing weather disruptions to events like marathons and ice-skating, extreme-wealth effects reshaping luxury expectations, and the approach of AGI flooding the internet with even more content. Despite this uncertainty, people are not expected to stop traveling, so the challenge for destination marketers and creators is to break through the noise and help people find places that make them feel most like themselves.

Panelists from TikTok, Beautiful Destinations and Visit California shared examples of what has recently broken through. TikTok highlighted Singapore Tourism Board's full-funnel "Journey" campaign, which used gamification, livestreams and creators rather than just pretty imagery to turn viewers into participants. Beautiful Destinations pointed to a broader shift away from seasonal campaigns toward always-on, constant content, noting the industry is finally adapting the always-connected approach it has advocated since its own founding. Visit California described moving away from traditional campaigns toward content built around a single promise—quiet and reassurance—given how anxious travelers currently feel.

Discussion turned to overtourism and the risk that a single viral recommendation can overwhelm a quiet spot. Panelists argued that content and creators are actually an opportunity for dispersion, letting smaller destinations without big ad budgets gain visibility by using the right content on the right platforms, while large, saturated hotspots can redirect some attention to mom-and-pop or lesser-known places. Visit California tied this to sustainability directly, describing its Traveler Responsibility Code adopted after pandemic-era overcrowding in rural areas, creator briefs that build in sustainable-travel messaging, and the ongoing challenge of communicating closures like Highway 1 or chain requirements on Highway 80 without discouraging visitors from California's hundreds of other destinations. The upcoming Olympics was raised as a live test case: Paris's experience showed a net tourism loss from visitors staying away, and Visit California is already working to message early that California and LA remain open and safe, aiming to disperse visitors and offset a projected 20% business impact in the LA region.

On whether destinations can actually steer demand, panelists agreed social platforms now shape far more of the journey than top-of-funnel inspiration alone. Over 60% of consumers reportedly begin travel planning on social media, often discovering a destination before the brand communicates anything itself, and TikTok data shows 60% of US users say they visited a place because of content seen on the platform—figures Visit California echoed by noting 76% of travelers lock in a destination before planning begins, meaning safety and logistical education (like road closures) needs to happen at the inspiration stage, not after a decision is made. Panelists noted social is now used much further down the funnel too—for comparison and decision-making, such as sharing reels of hotel room types—compressing campaign timelines from weeks to days and requiring brands to react almost in real time, including countering inaccurate media narratives with fast, factual alerts.

Closing on AI-generated video, panelists agreed authentic, creator-led content still performs best and builds trust, but AI is valuable for solving content-volume problems—filling gaps for brands that can't produce enough real footage of every hotel room or route—and for quickly generating variations of proven real footage for paid media testing rather than requiring new shoots each time. Visit California confirmed it similarly uses AI to repurpose the same content clips with different messages rather than replacing original real-world production.

## Key Takeaways

### 🧭 Set practical expectations before destination lock-in

Because travelers may choose a destination before researching access or conditions, destinations should deliver practical guidance during the inspiration phase—covering closures, responsible behavior, timing and alternatives—rather than waiting until trip planning is underway.

Quotes:

> 76% of people start their travel planning after they do destination lock ... The education has to happen early at the inspiration phase.

### 🔎 Social discovery can drive actual visits and planning

Social platforms increasingly influence the full travel journey: travelers discover destinations there, decide where to go and use short video to research and compare options before visiting.

Quotes:

> Over 60% of consumers will start their travel journey in social media ... We're seeing much further down the funnel decisions being made researching planning comparison.

### 🤫 Travel content can sell relief, not just destinations

In an anxious environment, destination marketing can break through by addressing what people need emotionally—such as quiet, recovery and personal space—rather than focusing only on destination attributes.

Quotes:

> The biggest campaign that has broken through for us right now is the one that sort of promises silence. A little less about us and a little more about you. What do you want?

### 🤖 Use AI to scale content without losing authenticity

AI can help travel brands increase content volume, create catalog variations and adapt paid media after identifying what works. The panel stressed that the underlying destination or product footage should remain accurate, creator-led content is especially effective, and outputs should follow brand guidelines.

Quotes:

> Creator-led content will always do much better than any other content on the platform. But if you're going down to lower the funnel and you need your catalogs and you need volume of content, I think AI has done a lot.

### 📱 Relevant content can outweigh media budgets

For destinations and travel businesses with limited funds, matching useful, platform-native content to the right audiences can generate disproportionate reach through recommendation algorithms, reducing reliance on traditional advertising.

Quotes:

> If you know how to make the right kind of content for the right kind of platforms you're going to have a far outsized impact than having big advertising budgets. You don't need to rely on that.

### 🏟️ Use major events to disperse visitors regionally

For major events such as the Olympics, destinations should start communicating early, promote experiences beyond the host area and set expectations about access and alternatives. Regional dispersal can help avoid concentrated losses and pressure in the event area.

Quotes:

> For the Olympics, dispersal is critical. ... LA has California. We are working in concert right now to start the messaging to assure people that traveling to California is going to be safe and open.

### ⚡ Build an always-on, signal-led content operation

Travel marketers can move beyond seasonal campaign windows by continuously publishing content and using feeds, trends and audience behavior as live research. This can shorten the distance between an insight, an idea and execution from weeks to days.

Quotes:

> The amount of data and insight that you get from looking at your feeds, looking at the content that's trending and then using that to inform your strategy means that you can move from these campaign windows ... to a matter of days.

### 🎮 Full-funnel campaigns turn inspiration into participation

Singapore Tourism Board’s Journey campaign combined gamification, live programming and creators to move beyond attractive imagery and actively bring audiences into the travel experience.

Quotes:

> They didn’t just use pretty pictures and videos and say, ‘Come visit Singapore.’ They utilized gamification. They went on live. They used a lot of creators to bring the noise to the platform and actually bring the people to the experience.

### 📰 Use factual alerts to counter broad travel narratives

Destinations can respond to confusing or outdated media coverage with timely, factual updates about what is open, closed or changing, helping travelers make decisions based on current conditions.

Quotes:

> We have a travel alert that we work on ... it's very factual. This highway is open. This highway is closed. And a lot of times it's reversing media narrative.

### 🌱 Destination marketing must protect travel assets

Travel marketers have a responsibility to safeguard natural and cultural assets by setting expectations for responsible behavior, incorporating sustainability into creator briefs and directing visitors toward viable alternatives when climate-sensitive sites or routes are disrupted.

Quotes:

> We have a responsibility in the travel industry to make sure that we are safeguarding our travel assets for the future.

## Idea Cloud

- Travel Inspiration Amid Uncertainty (score: 372)
- Responsible Tourism Protects Fragile Destinations (score: 349)
- Social Media Can Disperse Tourism (score: 323)
- AI Raises Content Volume and Authenticity Questions (score: 298)
- Always-On, Responsive Travel Content (score: 290)
- Olympics Require Regional Travel Dispersal (score: 208)
