# Executive Interview: Booking's B2B Ambition

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Omri Morgenshtern, CEO, Agoda; Linda Fox, Executive Editor, PhocusWire
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-18 5:35 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-18 5:55 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 3698
**Session ID:** `909c42a7-eea1-4b76-8d83-188c723b1355`

## Summary

The discussion opened with the rationale for unifying Booking Holdings' B2B operations into a single division despite the company's longstanding practice of keeping its brands independent. The reasoning centered on a large and growing B2B market where partners consistently voice two needs: help navigating tech disruption and demand for heavy customization rather than one-size-fits-all solutions. Concentrating energy on go-to-market and, especially, on product and technology was framed as the way to deliver explainable yet highly customizable tech to partners.

On scope, advertising on Booking's platforms is generally treated as part of the B2B remit, while payments is considered more complicated and was described as a separate, longer conversation, partly because some brands such as Agoda already run their own platform outside the Booking Partner Division's remit. Priceline's B2B business was explicitly excluded from the new division because of an ongoing FTC investigation and the operational complexities that entails; for now Priceline Partner Services remains separate with its own challenges, with no commitment to future integration.

Superior tech in B2B was defined as a combination of shipping speed, the ability to customize quickly at scale (a discipline learned from operating across diverse Asian markets), and staying state of the art with technologies like AI, aiming for a “best in the world” bar on each feature. Rather than favoring one platform, the approach draws on strengths from both Agoda and Booking.com technology depending on the product: APIs are leaning more toward what is now called the Booking Partner Division API (the former Agoda API), while other products, such as redirect-type integrations, lean on Booking.com, with most current production still running on Booking.com technology. Ahead of the January go-live, the team already largely operates as one unit with a playbook for cross-selling products, though a long tail of work remains around legal entities, financial systems and backend integration; the playbook itself was credited to a wider team effort and described as intentionally short and simple.

On agility within a large organization, B2C traffic was described as the proving ground: AI assistants and other products are tested and refined on consumer brands before being extended to B2B partners, so B2C and B2B are seen as mutually reinforcing rather than in tension. Demand for B2B travel services was characterized as broad across geographies and products, with financial institutions, airlines, and a growing stream of loyalty partners cited as attractive, and AI platforms positioned as partners to supply with Booking's underlying AI “brain” rather than competitors to fight, since consumers may reach that brain through various front ends. Despite the B2B push, B2C was described as an equally loved “kid,” with AI expected to expand B2C opportunity too, so the moves were framed as additive rather than a shift of priority away from consumers.

On competitive positioning, acquisitions remain a tool but are not the default, given confidence in building tech internally and an entrepreneurial culture inside Booking Holdings, with acquisitions more likely to fill capability gaps or open new verticals; AI was also credited with making tech cheaper and faster to build. Asked about industry comparisons on room-night production, the response acknowledged a feature-by-feature gap in places but expressed confidence that within six to twelve months each major product line could reach “best in the world” status. On a personal note, multiple AI agents are already used for everyday transactions like e-commerce and bookings, though not yet for high-stakes purchases, with experimentation framed as essential to learning. Success for the division in a year's time was defined as growth for partners and for Booking, and not losing partners to competitors on product capability; failure would be losing on tech rather than on price. Asked about a hypothetical life beyond Booking, interest in healthcare innovation and its potential to extend human lifespan was raised as the one area that might draw attention away from travel.

## Key Takeaways

### 🚧 The B2B operation is largely ready, with backend work remaining

The team already operates largely as one and has identified its product investments and commercial playbook. Remaining work is mainly a long tail of legal entities, financial-system integration and other backend services before full operation.

Quotes:

> By and large we are ready to operate from January. There is a long tail of creating more entities. There's a long tail of better merging financial systems.

### 🎯 Booking is concentrating B2B resources around customization

Booking believes B2B is a large opportunity, but partners increasingly need help navigating technological disruption and demand heavily customized solutions. It is therefore concentrating go-to-market, technology and product resources in a dedicated B2B effort.

Quotes:

> We felt that now is a good time in order to achieve that, to just focus our energy both on the go-to-market, but even more importantly on the tech and product, to really put technology that is explainable, simple, but highly customizable.

### 🌐 Demand spans markets, products and new partner types

Booking sees B2B growth opportunities broadly rather than in one geography or product category. Alongside established partners such as airlines and financial institutions, it is targeting newer travel distributors, particularly loyalty programs that want to add travel services.

Quotes:

> The market is so big pretty much everywhere ... Obviously you have the usual suspects, the airlines, the financial institutions ... but really everywhere we look we see opportunities to grow with our partners. Also a bunch of new partners that we can work with, especially loyalty.

### 🏢 Priceline B2B remains outside Booking Partners for now

Priceline B2B will stay separate because its operational challenges differ from those of the Booking Partners division, including complexity associated with an FTC investigation.

Quotes:

> We felt that the right thing to do is to keep those things separated. So for the time being Priceline B2B stays.

### 🧪 B2C brands will build and validate Booking’s AI before B2B rollout

Booking intends to build trusted, verticalized travel assistance in its consumer brands first, using real traffic to refine capabilities such as itinerary planning, booking and proactive support. It will then extend the proven experience to B2B partners rather than treating B2B as a replacement for B2C.

Quotes:

> We’re strong believers in verticalized travel assistance ... We’re definitely going to build it in B2C ... and because it’s going to be so great, we’re going to extend it to the B2B side.

### 🧬 Booking and Agoda technology will be combined selectively

The unified B2B operation will not standardize on one technology stack. API products will lean more heavily on Agoda’s former API, while other products—such as redirect experiences on Booking.com—will use Booking technology.

Quotes:

> It's really a mixture. There are a bunch of products when it comes to API there will be more will be leaning more into the booking partner division API that is the former Agoda API. But there are other products where we are leaning for instance ... redirect type of products ... that will lean heavily on booking.

### 🔄 B2B expands Booking’s reach rather than replacing B2C

Booking does not view B2B as a superior alternative to B2C. Its strategy is to serve consumers through whichever brands, partners or interfaces they choose, accepting some overlap because the opportunity to reach consumers elsewhere is larger.

Quotes:

> We go to the consumer where the consumer is ... The potential to serve the consumers elsewhere is much bigger than trying to worry too much about the overlap.

### ⚡ Superior B2B tech means speed, customization and simplicity

Booking defines superior B2B technology through rapid shipping, scalable architecture and the ability to customize quickly for different markets and partners. It also wants solutions to remain simple and explainable while incorporating state-of-the-art capabilities such as AI.

Quotes:

> Superior tech means agility. Being able to customize. And then being state of the art with the newest technologies like AI.

### 🤖 AI platforms can access Booking’s travel intelligence

Booking sees AI platforms as another interface to the same consumer-facing travel capability, not merely as channels for distributing inventory. Its ambition is to provide itinerary planning, booking, recommendations and proactive support through partners as well as its own brands.

Quotes:

> Eventually we want them to talk to our AI brain. The same AI brain that can travel in itinerary for you, help you book, make recommendations, be proactive when things change.

### 🏆 Booking aims to close B2B feature gaps within 12 months

Booking sees B2B as a feature-driven competition. While acknowledging that some capabilities still need improvement, it believes focused investment and its technology platform can put each major product line in a best-in-world category within roughly a year.

Quotes:

> B2B is a feature game ... with the right focus, certainly with the type of tech that you have today, there’s no reason why 12 months from now we cannot look at each one of the main product lines ... and tell yourself we are in this ‘best in the world’ category.

### 💸 AI efficiency comes from matching models to tasks

Booking is testing multiple AI models and selecting the right one for each use case rather than relying on a single premium model. It sees this task-specific approach as a way to control token costs while retaining capability.

Quotes:

> We optimize that cost by picking the right model for the right task. On average you don't need to pay the premium cost.

### 📈 B2B success means partner growth without product-driven losses

Morgenshtern defines success primarily through growth for B2B partners and Booking. He also wants the division’s technology to become best-in-world enough that partners do not leave because competitors offer capabilities Booking cannot match.

Quotes:

> BPD is about growth. It’s about growth for our partners. Growth for partners is growth for us ... I’m not willing to lose on product. In a year from now, that shouldn’t happen.

## Idea Cloud

- AI Becomes Booking’s Travel Brain (score: 380)
- Agility Defines Superior B2B Tech (score: 377)
- Why Booking Is Combining B2B (score: 370)
- Partners Want Customization, Not Blanket Solutions (score: 345)
- Booking’s B2B Operating Model (score: 343)
- B2C Remains The Proving Ground For AI (score: 330)
- Best-In-World Means Feature Completeness (score: 318)
- Demand Spans Partners And Markets (score: 310)
- Building Can Outpace Buying (score: 286)
- AI Agents Move Into Everyday Transactions (score: 273)
- Priceline B2B Stays Separate (score: 167)

# Welcome Back & Hall of Fame Recognition

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Pete Comeau, Managing Director, Phocuswright
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-19 9:30 AM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-19 9:35 AM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 583
**Session ID:** `a7826258-5408-43c0-bf93-aedc652eae90`

## Summary

The discussion opened with a question about how the rise of AI agents making booking and offer decisions might ripple through the complex partnership networks that underpin loyalty programs, and what this means for how such partnerships should be structured going forward.

One view was that these partnerships would actually become stronger, particularly around servicing disruptions, because agents could operate across brands to solve problems that currently create friction. This was illustrated with a personal example of using an AI assistant (Muse) during a flight disruption: the tool rebooked a flight on a non-partner airline and adjusted hotel arrangements seamlessly, prompting the observation that if airline partnerships had been made visible and surfable to the agent, the disruption could have been handled entirely within the existing partner ecosystem, which would have been the ideal outcome.

Another perspective framed loyalty programs as marketplace builders: by combining demand with a virtual currency, these programs effectively create an ecosystem, making the choice of partners a critical strategic decision that shapes the entire marketplace going forward.

The conversation then turned to owned distribution channels, asking whether the growth of agentic bookings means travel brands are effectively giving up on their own websites and direct customer access points. It was noted that suppliers have already begun asking whether they even need a website anymore. The response was that owned channels are not being abandoned in the near term, but their role needs to be rethought: brands may need to make their own assets richer in content, reconsider booking flows, and rethink how customers experience those channels, since owned assets remain important for the foreseeable future even as agents take on more of the booking decision-making.

The session closed with a sponsor segment promoting a free research study, invited via a scannable code, examining how brands are showing up and what is being said about them, before thanks were extended to the panelists. The program then transitioned into the Hall of Fame recognition, introduced by recalling that the Phocuswright Hall of Fame was established in 2018 to honor individuals who played an instrumental role in shaping the travel industry by refusing to accept the status quo and driving new developments.

## Key Takeaways

### 🤝 Partnerships should enable complete agent-led servicing

The value of a partnership increasingly depends on whether its inventory and capabilities are accessible to an agent that can handle an entire customer problem, not just make an isolated booking.

Quotes:

> If those partnerships had made surfable to my agent that the disruption could be completely handled on them, that would have been ideal.

### 🌐 Owned websites remain important in the near term

The rise of agentic bookings does not mean suppliers should abandon their websites or other controlled customer access points; those assets are expected to remain important for the foreseeable future.

Quotes:

> We're certainly not there right now. In fact, you do need to think about your assets that you control differently.

### 🧭 Partnership strategy defines the customer ecosystem

Choosing partners is a strategic decision because it determines the boundaries and capabilities of the marketplace that a loyalty program can offer through agents.

Quotes:

> The notion of who you partner with is extremely important. Because you're defining really that ecosystem, that marketplace.

### 🏪 Loyalty programs can become partner marketplaces

Loyalty programs have the ingredients to organize a marketplace around their partners: existing customer demand, an ecosystem of participating brands, and a virtual currency.

Quotes:

> Loyalty programs have the ability to create a marketplace with their partners. They've got the demand. They've got a virtual currency.

### 🤖 Agents can resolve disruptions across brand boundaries

Travel agents may be able to solve servicing problems that span airlines, hotels, and other brands—for example, rebooking a disrupted flight on a non-partner airline and updating the hotel reservation as part of one recovery process.

Quotes:

> Muse went in, put me on a flight on a non-partnership airline, rebooked and redid everything on my hotel. And it was absolutely solved.

### 📝 Websites need richer content for agentic discovery

Suppliers may need to enrich the content on their owned digital assets so that customers—and potentially the agents acting for them—can understand and evaluate the offering more effectively.

Quotes:

> You may need to make them much richer in terms of the content that's within them.

### 🔄 Booking flows must evolve for new customer experiences

Beyond adding content, suppliers need to rethink how customers experience their sites and how booking flows work as agentic interactions become more common.

Quotes:

> You may need to think about the way a customer is going to experience it and the booking flows. So there's a lot of implications that may require rethinking.

## Idea Cloud

- Agents Transform Loyalty Partnerships (score: 344)
- Cross-Brand Agentic Servicing (score: 329)
- The Future Of Owned Websites (score: 278)
- Richer Content For Agent Discovery (score: 258)
- Rethinking Booking Experiences (score: 238)
- Brand Visibility In Agentic Journeys (score: 73)

# Executive Interview: Powering the New Travel Seller

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Alfonso Paredes, President, B2B & Chief Commercial Officer, Expedia Group
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-19 9:35 AM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-19 9:55 AM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 4466
**Session ID:** `c4271ff0-c8da-4aae-9d96-5656d27ddec8`

## Summary

The session opened with the induction of this year's Phocuswright Hall of Fame honoree, who began his travel career opening more than 250 restaurants for PepsiCo before joining a young Expedia, where he managed hotel partnerships worth over $2 billion a year in gross bookings. He later spent 16 years as Managing Director of Travel at Google working with major airlines, hotel companies, OTAs and destinations, then returned to Expedia Group to run and more than double the revenue of its advertising business into a billion-dollar-plus media network. His industry leadership also included chairing HSMAI, serving as Vice Chairman of the World Travel and Tourism Council, and sitting on the US Travel and Tourism Advisory Board and the board of Global Glimpse.

The main interview opened on a personal note, with the Expedia B2B leader recounting traveling to the US with his son to watch seven World Cup matches in seven cities, continuing a tradition his own father had started with him in 1982, and deliberately skipping the final to prioritize sharing the tournament experience with his family. Asked how the Spanish national team's success reflected his approach to building Expedia's B2B business, he described it as a team effort rather than a collection of star players, crediting colleagues across M&A, supply, and product teams—including those behind recent acquisitions such as CarTrawler—for enabling the business to integrate new capabilities and achieve velocity. He also emphasized that having teams distributed across markets such as Madrid, Dublin, London, Seattle and Singapore helps the company understand local partner and traveler needs, since every segment and every country differs.

On the pressures facing B2B travel, the discussion turned to why banks, airlines and retailers are entering travel: it was framed as a natural expansion into a category that generates significant consumer spend, with these companies relying on technology and servicing partners like Expedia while they focus on their core customer relationships. Asked how Expedia's B2B unit compares to priorities Booking Holdings has articulated—speed, customization and simplicity—he argued that speed and execution have long been givens for his team, and that trust in partner relationships will become even more important as AI adoption grows. He noted that recent acquisitions such as CarTrawler and whole-ticket activities deals were pursued because building equivalent capabilities internally would have taken too long, and that once these tools are fully integrated by around the second half of 2027, the company expects to reach full velocity, already launching partners roughly 75% faster than before. A live audience poll on what will matter most for B2B platforms showed customization and simplicity as the top choices, with the executive agreeing that customization is critical since partners want genuine differentiation rather than templated look-alikes.

On the question of power, there was no ambiguity that partners, not platforms, own the traveler relationship. This created little conflict for Expedia because the company deliberately concentrated its own B2C business on the US during COVID while leaving Asia Pacific and Europe growth to partners and B2B, unlike Booking.com which has large direct consumer presence in those regions. On whether AI makes it easy for new entrants to build their own booking products and bypass incumbents like Expedia, the response was that creating a basic booking flow is simple, but travel's complexity—especially servicing issues such as the recent US air traffic control disruptions—requires mastery that is hard to replicate, and that incumbents' operational depth will ultimately be underestimated by newer, noisier entrants.

The conversation closed by looking at a pivot in business models, illustrated through the expanded Qantas partnership. Moving beyond traditional white-label templates that often looked indistinguishable from Expedia or Booking.com, the company built a composable, agentic, AI-powered front end that cut launch times from three to four months down to three to four days. With Qantas specifically, Expedia now also powers the technology behind Qantas Holidays' directly negotiated hotel contracts with chains like Accor, Hilton and Marriott, earning a transaction fee while letting Qantas keep those commercial relationships and focus its marketing on flights—an arrangement described as a long-held ambition that opens new B2B opportunities. A rapid-fire closing round touched on travel sellers' biggest pressure being the generally volatile, fast-changing macro environment rather than one single factor, a preference for Formula 1 over soccer and cash-and-points over pure cash on personal trips, a view that agentic booking is overhyped short-term but underhyped long-term, and interest in extending Expedia's technology to power other consumer brands such as football clubs (noting an existing relationship with Real Madrid) or universities.

## Key Takeaways

### ⚡ Acquisitions accelerate launches only after integration

Expedia is acquiring capabilities in areas such as car rental, activities and experiences instead of building them entirely in-house. The speed benefit depends on integrating the acquired technology and creating synergies; once integrated, Expedia expects to launch partners 75% faster than before.

Quotes:

> We are able now with all this technology to launch partners 75% faster than what we used to do before. So once you integrate in all of that, things get easier. Until then, it's a little bit rough.

### 🔐 Trust remains critical alongside AI

As AI and automation become more prominent in travel, trusted relationships with partners will become even more important rather than less.

Quotes:

> We were talking about AI, we were talking about machines and so on and so on, but trust relationships are the other ones that are going to be key now.

### 🎯 Execution and integration drive B2B growth

Torres credits Expedia B2B’s progress to a strong, complementary team, but emphasizes that execution is what turns acquisitions and strategy into growth. Integrating companies, creating synergies and maintaining the existing business are central to the approach.

Quotes:

> I will put my focus on execution... You need to maintain all of that, you need to execute, you need to bring all the companies together and have synergies.

### 👥 Partners, not platforms, own the traveler relationship

In B2B travel, Expedia’s role is to enable partners that own the customer relationship, while helping them differentiate through customized products and services.

Quotes:

> Who owns the traveler? Our partners. That's for sure. There is no discussion.

### 🤝 Specialist partners let new travel sellers outsource complexity

Organizations entering travel can focus on their customer relationships and core strengths while specialist partners provide the technology, servicing and operational expertise required to deliver travel products.

Quotes:

> They are letting others helping them... while we take care of the technology servicing, et cetera, et cetera. So it's a win-win.

### 🧩 Platforms can power a partner’s own supplier relationships

Expedia’s expanded Qantas model goes beyond supplying inventory: Qantas keeps its direct hotel relationships while Expedia powers the underlying technology and earns a transaction fee. This lets the airline focus its resources on selling flights and marketing its core business.

Quotes:

> They’re going to keep on those relationships with Accor, Hilton, Marriott, et cetera. But the technology, we’re also going to power that technology where we operate getting a transaction fee.

### 🌍 Local presence helps Expedia understand varied travel needs

A team distributed across markets gives Expedia B2B closer insight into partners, customers and travelers, whose needs differ by country and segment.

Quotes:

> You need to be in the market. You need to understand the needs of your partners, your customers, to understand what is it that the travelers want.

### 🎨 Customization makes B2B travel products distinctive

Travel sellers want products that feel distinctive rather than generic white-label storefronts. Expedia’s composable template, with an AI-powered front end, is designed to provide that customization while reducing launch time from three or four months to three or four days.

Quotes:

> We create this composable agentic template... What it used to take us 3 months, 4 months to launch per point of sale, we do it in 3, 4 days now.

### 🤖 Agentic booking has a long-term opportunity despite short-term hype

Torres expects agentic booking to be overhyped in the short term but underhyped in the long term, suggesting travel sellers should distinguish near-term excitement from the technology’s longer-term potential.

Quotes:

> Overhyped in the short term, underhyped in the long term.

### 🛎️ Servicing complexity remains a travel-industry moat

Building a basic hotel-booking interface is relatively easy, but handling disruptions, customer service, complex reservation chains and packages requires specialized operational capabilities that AI and new entrants may struggle to replicate.

Quotes:

> Travel is much more complex... Who is taking care of that? AI? No. Customer service. And you need to have that. You need to master that. That is hard to replicate.

## Idea Cloud

- Teams and acquisitions drive B2B scale (score: 356)
- Customization defines the next B2B advantage (score: 351)
- Service complexity protects specialist travel platforms (score: 342)
- Composable technology reshapes partner economics (score: 334)
- Travel’s new distribution entrants (score: 319)
- Partners, not platforms, own the traveler (score: 290)
- Local presence sharpens B2B advantage (score: 279)

# Executive Interview: The Pressures Reshaping U.S. Travel

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Geoff Freeman, President and CEO, U.S. Travel Association; Josh Weinstein, President & CEO, Carnival Corporation
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-19 9:55 AM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-19 10:15 AM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 3314
**Session ID:** `072f9331-19ae-4aa1-95fb-9acaacff227a`

## Summary

The session opened with a discussion of the cruise sector's post-pandemic strength, framing the conversation around how the U.S. consumer's relationship with travel has fundamentally shifted. The cruise executive explained that prioritization of travel, long a cultural norm in Europe, has finally caught on with Americans since the pandemic, moving from a temporary 'revenge travel' phenomenon five years ago to a lasting change in how people choose to live their lives and allocate spending.

On resilience in the face of economic and shock-driven pressures, it was argued that consumers will cut other spending before giving up travel experiences, and that cruising's relative value versus other vacation options provides some insulation during downturns. Shock events such as groundings, geopolitical conflict, or policy disruptions tend to cause temporary pauses in booking as people normalize new information about their jobs, savings, and the future, but historically travelers have returned to booking en masse once they adjust, as seen even after a prolonged Middle East conflict. This resilience was echoed in broader industry data showing record quarters for hotels and record spring break, Memorial Day, and July 4th travel volumes, despite the mounting number of disruptions.

A significant portion of the conversation focused on how booking behavior is being reshaped by AI. While traditional travel agents will remain relevant for some customers in the medium term, and direct digital booking continues to grow, AI assistants and agentic platforms are seen as disrupting the entire booking landscape. Princess Cruises was cited as the first cruise brand to launch plugins for ChatGPT and Claude, allowing bookings to begin within those platforms, with a stated expectation that within single-digit years consumers may complete entire bookings through AI assistants rather than websites, fundamentally shifting power toward the consumer. This transition is already prompting a reallocation rather than simple increase of marketing and distribution spend, with channels like SEO becoming less valuable while AI-driven efficiencies are being applied across the P&L to boost both cost savings and revenue generation.

On costs, the company does not hedge fuel and instead focuses on efficiency, having cut fuel consumption per unit by about 26% since 2019 and absolute greenhouse gas emissions by 12% despite being larger. Pricing, it was stressed, is not cost-based but driven by what consumers are willing to pay, meaning input cost swings in fuel, food, and labor do not mechanically pass through to fares the way they might in other travel sectors like airlines. Even amid a sustained geopolitical conflict that many feared would cause broader economic disruption, the anticipated worst-case outcomes largely did not materialize, reinforcing a view that the industry adapts to supply chain and geopolitical volatility rather than planning around a stable baseline.

The discussion closed on man-made disruptions and policy, including recent government shutdowns, TSA line backups, air traffic controller shortages, and a steep decline in Canadian travel to the U.S., all described as self-inflicted drags on an otherwise resilient industry that have cost the company a few hundred million dollars in lost revenue and a point or two of yield this year, alongside a warning that aging infrastructure and rising cybersecurity risks will likely produce more such disruptions. Reflecting on a recent meeting with President Trump and other CEOs focused on declining international inbound travel, which is down roughly twelve million visitors versus 2019 despite the World Cup, it was argued that tourism lacks the cabinet-level representation it holds in other major economies, despite contributing about 3% of U.S. GDP and over 15 million jobs, and that better balancing security and openness could improve outcomes for inbound visitors.

## Key Takeaways

### 💰 Prices are set by customer value, not simply by costs

Carnival does not automatically pass higher fuel, labor or food costs on to customers. It prices according to what consumers are willing to pay, with cost changes affecting the gap between revenue and profit rather than directly determining fares.

Quotes:

> We never price based on cost. We don’t. We price based on what the consumer is willing to pay. We don’t pass things on. That’s not our business model.

### 🌍 U.S. consumers now prioritize travel more like European consumers

Since the pandemic, travel has become a much higher priority for U.S. consumers, resembling the long-standing European practice of protecting holiday spending.

Quotes:

> The prioritization that the U.S. consumer gives to travel... has finally caught up, frankly, where Europe has been for decades.

### 🧳 Booking strategy must span advisors and digital channels

Cruise companies need to serve travelers according to their preferred booking method, preserving relationships with trusted travel advisors while supporting growing direct digital demand.

Quotes:

> We need to meet our consumer wherever they’re going to be and however they want to book. There are some people that, for the medium term certainly, are going to want to talk to a person, a trusted advisor... But more and more... people [are] more used to going digital booking directly into our own websites.

### 🌐 Inbound travel needs a stronger place in U.S. policymaking

The U.S. travel industry is urging Washington to treat tourism as a strategic economic sector and balance security measures with a more welcoming approach to international visitors. The speaker links inbound travel to roughly 3% of GDP and more than 15 million jobs, while noting that the country will receive 12 million fewer international visitors than in 2019.

Quotes:

> In every major economy in the world, tourism has a cabinet seat around the table. The only place it doesn't is here... Security is critical and so is tourism.

### ⚠️ Shocks delay travel bookings, but demand can recover strongly

Economic, geopolitical and operational shocks can temporarily delay bookings while consumers assess their jobs, savings and future. Once uncertainty eases, demand can normalize quickly; Carnival reported that recent disruptions cost it several hundred million dollars in potential revenue, but only a few points of yield rather than a pandemic-scale collapse.

Quotes:

> People just take a little time to normalize... But even then, people got past it and they normalized... and started booking en masse again.

### ⚙️ AI is becoming an operating-efficiency tool

Carnival is applying AI beyond customer-facing distribution, using it across functions to improve efficiency, generate demand and strengthen revenue performance. The company says tangible benefits have emerged rapidly after an initially exploratory phase.

Quotes:

> We are seeing and utilizing AI in ways up and down our P&L and across our functions to find efficiency. To generate more demand and be better at the revenue generation. And it’s remarkable how quickly it is coming to fruition.

### 🛫 Aging infrastructure is making travel disruptions more frequent

Travel companies should expect more recurring operational shocks as newer technologies are layered onto aging aviation and travel infrastructure. These disruptions are manageable but can materially reduce revenue, and cybersecurity adds another fast-moving risk as bad actors adopt AI tools.

Quotes:

> It does seem like the type of technology we're trying to introduce into our systems is not compatible with the age and infrastructure of what exists. And so there's going to be these types of disruptions.

### ⛽ Efficiency is Carnival’s main response to volatile input costs

Rather than hedge fuel prices, Carnival focuses on consuming less fuel, food, water and other resources. The company says fuel consumption per unit is down 26% versus 2019, while absolute fuel use is down 13% despite being 11% larger.

Quotes:

> We focused on using less. If you consume less, you save money. And so we are maniacal about consumption. Everything from fuel to food, to the beverages to water production—how do we do things more efficiently and effectively all the time?

### 🚢 Cruising has resilience as a relative-value vacation

Cruise demand may be less sensitive to downturns because cruises compare favorably on price with other vacation alternatives.

Quotes:

> The great thing about cruising is we’re a relative value. So we have some level of resistance to economic downturns because when you comp us versus other vacation alternatives, we’re cheaper.

### 🤖 AI is moving travel distribution toward digital assistants

AI is disrupting advisor-led, direct and website-based booking models. Cruise companies must remain discoverable and useful within AI platforms, as consumers may increasingly rely on digital assistants to plan and purchase travel rather than visiting websites themselves.

Quotes:

> I don’t know what the over-under on number of years is, but it’s single digits, I think, that people won’t be going to websites at all. Because they’re going to pull out their phone... and have their digital assistant literally do everything for them.

## Idea Cloud

- AI is remaking travel booking (score: 369)
- Consumers protect experiences in downturns (score: 363)
- Americans now prioritize travel (score: 347)
- Efficiency offsets travel inflation (score: 312)
- Travel disruptions expose infrastructure risks (score: 303)
- Cruising offers relative value (score: 302)
- Travel distribution is becoming omnichannel (score: 293)
- Tourism needs a seat in government (score: 271)
- Travel prices follow consumer demand (score: 201)

# Hot Spot: Zerolook

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Simone Lini, CEO, Zerolook
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-19 10:15 AM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-19 10:20 AM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 816
**Session ID:** `abedc536-50f1-4742-9aa9-2dd9a5f4022d`

## Summary

The session opened with a pitch built around a real-world example: an AI agent built with Claude Code that ran roughly 881,000 flight searches on Etihad's website, illustrating how AI agents search far more aggressively than humans without tiring, while still booking at normal human rates. A further example showed how a simple multi-city, multi-week flight query (Fort Lauderdale/Miami to Greek islands over a month) could generate 1,400 separate searches, since these are low-intent, high-up-the-funnel queries that convert poorly—best case 1%, implying a look-to-book ratio as high as 144,000 to 1. The core problem stated was that the cost airlines and OTAs pay per search in such cases exceeds the gross margin earned from the eventual flight sale, making the current search infrastructure unsustainable for this kind of volume.

The proposed solution, Zerolook, is described as a bold bet: building a new layer specifically to absorb low-intent queries rather than sending every search to live airline or GDS systems. The pitch clarified that this approach is meant for situations where look-to-book ratios and query volume are problematic, not a replacement for existing infrastructure when that isn't an issue. Zerolook offers a flight shopping API that cuts the cost of search by up to two orders of magnitude, reserving live queries to airlines or GDS systems only for moments of genuinely high purchase intent.

Technically, the system relies on a trained machine learning model—explicitly not an LLM—that learns the pricing patterns airline revenue managers follow, combined with daily sampling of a small set of itineraries to track price movement. This lets the system predict prices accurately without hitting live systems; on their best-performing case, a U.S. carrier, the model reaches 96% pricing accuracy. The stated goal is to push accuracy to 97% across a broad set of carriers by Q1 of next year and launch a full flight search API, with the team acknowledging substantial R&D work remains before the space is fully solved.

On credibility and backing, the pitch highlighted a founding team with deep travel and tech pedigree: a founder who previously sold a company to lastminute.com, led metasearch product and commercial work, and ran partnerships at Google for tours/activities and later flights; a co-founder with an MIT computer science PhD, prior VP experience at Kayak, and engineering leadership roles at NVIDIA and Google; and an advisory board drawing from Sabre Labs, Kayak's machine learning leadership, and other veteran travel-tech operators. The company has raised $1.9 million, is based in Zurich, runs a six-person team (all engineers except the speaker), and is assembling a cohort of test partners ahead of its planned Q1 launch, inviting companies facing look-to-book cost problems to get in touch.

The session then shifted to a broader industry research presentation building on prior talks about what travel companies can learn from AI-native startups and an 'AI advantage' equation for measuring competitive position. Drawing on a new global survey of roughly 150 travel enterprises and startups—nearly all already using AI—fielded in August and September and soon to be published as 'Rebuild or Bolt-On: Travel's Agentic-AI Divide' (sponsored by CNF Travel and Sabre), the talk set up a comparison of how established companies versus startups are faring, noting that even among AI-engaged companies ahead of the industry curve, a significant divide in AI adoption and advantage remains evident.

## Key Takeaways

### 🔢 Broad travel queries can produce thousands of searches

A seemingly simple request—such as finding a one-week June trip from either Fort Lauderdale or Miami to a Greek island—can require roughly 1,400 search combinations across airports, destinations, and dates.

Quotes:

> You have two departure airports. Thirty airports in Greece on island with commercial service. And then you have 24 one-week windows in June. That makes 1,400 searches.

### 📊 Daily sampling helps track changing airline prices

Although the model does not query live systems for every request, Zerolook samples a small number of flights and itineraries each day to observe price movements and update its predictions.

Quotes:

> Every day we also take a sample of a small number of flights, of itineraries, to know how the price is moving.

### ⚖️ AI adoption is opening a divide among travel companies

Early research from a survey of roughly 150 already AI-engaged travel enterprises and startups suggests that established companies and startups are diverging in how effectively they use AI.

Quotes:

> Nearly all of them already use AI. These are companies that are AI engaged, may run ahead of the industry, yet we still see a big divide opening up among them.

### 🎯 Live inventory remains available for high-intent shoppers

Zerolook is not presented as a replacement for live airline or GDS searches. Once a traveler demonstrates sufficient intent, the system can issue a live query to obtain current booking options.

Quotes:

> Whenever someone has high enough intent, they can go and place a live query to the airline, to the GDS.

### ✈️ AI agents create an unsustainable flight-search cost problem

Agents can generate enormous numbers of flight searches because they systematically explore many combinations, but those searches often have low purchase intent. The resulting search costs can exceed the margin earned from selling the flight.

Quotes:

> They don't get tired of searching. The issue is they also book like everyone else. So, the cost becomes an issue for everyone.

### 🧪 The product is still in active research and development

The speaker stresses that Zerolook has not solved the entire flight-pricing problem yet; the reported results are encouraging, but further development is required before the planned API launch.

Quotes:

> We still have a lot of research and development in front of us, and we have by no means solved the entire space.

### 🤖 Zerolook uses traditional machine learning rather than an LLM

The pricing system is based on conventional machine learning trained to learn patterns in airline revenue-management decisions, rather than on a large language model.

Quotes:

> We have trained a machine learning model. It's not an LLM, it's good old style machine learning.

### 📉 Low-intent searches have extremely weak conversion economics

The presentation estimates that broad, exploratory flight searches may convert at best around 1%, creating a look-to-book ratio of approximately 144,000 to one and making conventional live-search economics unworkable.

Quotes:

> Those searches are very high up in the funnel. They don't convert well. Best case, one percent conversion rate. That's a look-to-book ratio of 144,000 to one.

### ✅ Zerolook reports 96% pricing accuracy for one U.S. carrier

Zerolook says its model predicted prices with 96% accuracy for one U.S. carrier without querying a live system, with a goal of reaching 97% across a broader range of carriers by Q1 next year.

Quotes:

> We can be 96% accurate on our prediction on pricing without needing to hit any live system. And our goal is to reach 97% across a wide number of carriers by Q1 next year.

### 💰 The proposed API aims to cut flight-search costs by up to 100×

The company says its flight-shopping API can reduce the cost of searching by up to two orders of magnitude, potentially making high-volume exploratory queries viable.

Quotes:

> We offer a flight shopping API that reduces the cost of search by up to two orders of magnitude.

### 🧩 Zerolook proposes a new layer for low-intent queries

Zerolook’s product is intended to handle broad, low-intent shopping requests separately from existing live airline or GDS systems, while preserving the current setup for searches whose volume is economically manageable.

Quotes:

> We're going to build one new layer that addresses all of the low-intent queries.

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026

## Key Takeaways

### 🏗️ Buy broadly, build where proprietary advantages matter

Choice's framework is to buy AI capabilities such as agent platforms when vendors offer greater scale, governance, and tooling, while building in areas where proprietary data and workflows create differentiation, such as revenue management.

Quotes:

> For the large part, we're mostly going to buy, because there are plenty of organizations out there that have the scale, the governance, the tools. ... There are instances where we have something unique or proprietary that will benefit us.

### 🔧 Workflow redesign separates AI leaders

The biggest reported difference is whether companies have rebuilt core workflows around AI: 92% of those that did report major positive impact, compared with 40% of other companies.

Quotes:

> Among all companies, 92 percent of the ones that rebuilt core workflows report major positive impact from AI. Everyone else, 40 percent. That is a huge difference.

### 🚧 Modernization must happen without disrupting the core business

Travel companies are rebuilding incrementally while keeping critical transactions and customers running. Travelport migrated about 95% of customers to a consolidated platform over two years, while Evan rebuilt its stack during a carve-out and continued serving 40,000 properties.

Quotes:

> It's not just us deploying new technology. It's also our customers that needs to move to a new platform. ... over the last two years, we probably migrated now 95% of our customers to the new platform.

### 🤖 Agentic AI adoption is accelerating

Forty-five percent of surveyed companies are scaling or have scaled agentic AI, up from 28% in 2025. Startups are further ahead than established companies, at 54% versus 33%.

Quotes:

> So 45 percent of the companies that we surveyed are scaling or have scaled agentic AI. Up from 28 percent in 2025. And startups are driving this, being pretty far ahead at 54 percent. Versus established companies at 33 percent.

### 🧑‍💻 Adoption starts with useful workflows, not AI training

Employees are more likely to embrace AI when it removes a difficult, disliked task and delivers immediate value than when organizations mandate generic training. Experienced staff remain important as the judgment layer that can recognize when an AI-generated answer is wrong.

Quotes:

> When people have picked a very complicated process that everybody hates, and have automated it through an agent, that's where it clicks. ... The powerful thing about legacy knowledge and employees is they're really acting as the semantic layer ... providing the critical thinking that knows whether or not the agentic workflows are providing the right answers.

### 🛒 Travel companies lag travelers on agentic booking

Travelers are more prepared than travel companies for agentic commerce: 43% of U.S. travelers familiar with AI say they would book inside an AI platform, while only 28% of surveyed companies are actively working or planning to become bookable there. Technical interoperability is improving, with companies running model-context or agent-to-agent protocols in production rising from 9% to 31% in a year.

Quotes:

> 43 percent of U.S. travelers familiar with AI say they would book travel inside an AI platform. ... But only 28 percent of the companies we surveyed say they are actively working to build or planning to be bookable inside AI.

### 🔄 Self-improving loops connect measurement to revenue

Only 27% of surveyed companies have built self-improving AI loops. These loops depend on measuring outcomes and improving from them, and companies with them are more than twice as likely to quantify AI-driven revenue lift: 39% versus 16% without them.

Quotes:

> The most important part of the loop is step two, that AI can measure the result and figure out how to improve from there. ... Without them, it's 16 percent. With them, a whopping 39 percent that can quantify revenue lift.

### 🧹 Data, migration and change management are the hardest rebuild challenges

Rebuilding an AI-first stack while continuing to serve customers is difficult less because of AI tooling than because of legacy data, data cleaning, platform migration, employee education and process change. These constraints determine whether AI outcomes are reliable and whether teams can adopt new workflows.

Quotes:

> The data is really the biggest challenge, around having outcomes that you can rely upon. Cleaning the data. ... I also [see] the change management aspects. ... that's a lot of education. It's a lot of change in their processes.

### 🧮 AI advantage is a multiplication formula

The speaker frames AI advantage as multiplicative rather than additive: strength in one area cannot compensate for weakness in another, so companies must perform well across every variable.

Quotes:

> This is a multiplication formula, so strength on one variable doesn't make up for weakness on another. You've got to be good at all of them.

### 🎯 AI outcomes need formal KPIs

Although 74% of surveyed companies say leaders are personally accountable for AI outcomes, only 48% set formal KPI targets, limiting their ability to measure and improve what AI delivers.

Quotes:

> 74 percent of the companies we surveyed say leaders are personally accountable for AI outcomes. But only 48 percent set formal KPI targets for it.

# Executive Panel: Rebuilding the Stack While the Business Keeps Running

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Abhijit Patel, Vice President, Commercial Strategy, Choice Hotels International; Kenneth Johansen, Chief Revenue Officer, Travelport; Mike Coletta, Senior Manager, Research and Innovation, Phocuswright; Teresa Mackintosh, Chief Executive Officer, Aven Hospitality
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-19 10:35 AM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-19 11:00 AM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 1803
**Session ID:** `7eef67dc-28a9-4aca-bd85-ca12c8538948`

## Summary

The panel opened on how companies are allocating investment given the pace of change in the industry. One executive described shifting spend toward an efficient API and orchestration layer built on top of a newly migrated platform, with investment now focused on helping agency partners cope with agentic search disrupting their systems and become more competitive against agentic players. Another executive explained that traditional 3-to-5-year strategic planning has given way to 3-to-6-month horizons, since topics considered irrelevant six months ago are now mainstream; the focus is on positioning the company as 'intelligent supply' so customers can participate in evolving demand, whatever the latest model or demand-side partnership turns out to be.

Asked what else the industry needs to upgrade legacy infrastructure for agentic commerce, panelists pointed to three unresolved issues: poorly cataloged, unstructured data that blocks true e-commerce readiness; payments friction, since agents still trip over reCAPTCHA and similar checkpoints even when going through connectors; and governance, specifically how to distinguish helpful agents from scraping bots that companies want blocked. A related tension was raised around whether hoteliers even want agentic bookings at all, given concerns that agents won't upsell the way human-assisted bookings do, suggesting the choice may end up being made hotel-by-hotel rather than dictated industry-wide.

One executive argued that the bigger obstacle isn't legacy technology but organizational structure. The company had already completed a full end-to-end agentic booking including payment, but volume hasn't scaled because basic accountability questions remain unanswered: who is responsible if an agent's performance or payment fails, and how disruptions like airspace closures and rebookings get handled. The conclusion was that the technology to complete agentic bookings already exists, but organizations are not yet ready to maneuver around the operational and accountability questions it raises.

On model selection, the view was that there is no single right choice between open and closed source or local versus cloud deployment; it depends on the specific problem, since different models excel at different tasks, and the value of platform vendors lies in helping pick the right model for a given use case, such as choosing between models for complex pricing decisions. When asked what the industry could learn from a recent FAA incident about rebuilding infrastructure, the quick responses were pointed but light—hire the right contractor, don't use copper wire—acknowledging the comparison to running a company is imperfect but that the stakes and consequences of infrastructure choices are real.

Looking ahead one year, panelists expected incremental but real progress. One expects continued franchisee-focused product investment, helped by data that is already well-structured. Another noted that while industry survey data on AI adoption hasn't moved much in the past year, both more progress and a continually moving finish line will be true simultaneously. A third predicted more authentic support for travel agents as automation frees up their time for client-facing work while lowering servicing costs.

## Key Takeaways

### 🤖 Prepare agencies for agentic search

Travel businesses need to help agencies handle the operational impact of agentic search while improving their efficiency enough to compete with agentic systems.

Quotes:

> We invest quite a lot of time and money in helping our agencies to overcome the problems that they will start to see when agentic search starts punching into their systems. We also need to make them more efficient to be able to compete with agentic systems out there.

### 🤝 Redirect agents toward higher-value client work

As AI automates more travel-agent processes, the expected benefit is not only lower servicing costs but also more time for agents to support clients directly.

Quotes:

> A lot of processes will get automated. It will free up time for the agents to spend more time with clients, and it will at the same time actually lower the cost of servicing clients.

### 🧩 Match the model to the problem

There is no universal choice between open and closed-source or local and cloud models. The appropriate approach depends on the problem, while vendors and platforms can help companies combine models for different requirements such as complex pricing decisions.

Quotes:

> I think it really depends on the situation of the problem that you're looking to solve. Different models are good at different things.

### 🏗️ Build innovation on top of the migrated platform

Kenneth Johansen said the immediate investment focus is no longer migration itself, but using the new platform’s API and process-orchestration layer to bring innovation and greater efficiency to agencies.

Quotes:

> We spent time on migrating to the new platform. We're building a very efficient API and process orchestration layer on top of that platform. So now it's for us about bringing innovation to our agencies.

### 🏨 Weigh agentic bookings against lost upsells

Hotels are divided over whether to enable agentic booking: it may generate additional sales and add-on revenue, but agents may not accept the upsells that many hotels rely on. Participation could become a property-level decision.

Quotes:

> Do they want the additional sales and add-on revenue to be limited as an agent might not be upsold, like so many hoteliers count on?

### 🏢 Prepare the organization for failures, not just transactions

End-to-end agentic booking, including payment, is technically possible, but companies still need clear ownership for agent performance, failed payments, disruptions and rebooking. Organizational readiness is now a larger constraint than basic transaction capability.

Quotes:

> The technology is actually already there to complete an agent booking. But from an organizational standpoint, I'm not really sure we are ready to really maneuver.

### 💳 Remove payment and authentication friction

Agentic commerce depends on payment and authentication flows that agents can complete; mechanisms such as reCAPTCHA still cause agents to fail during transactions.

Quotes:

> We don't talk about [payments] enough... you still have to go through reCAPTCHA. And agents trip up on reCAPTCHA.

### 🔄 Plan in short cycles as the market shifts

With travel technology changing faster than traditional three- or five-year plans can accommodate, companies are focusing on three- to six-month horizons while continuously preparing themselves and customers for what comes next.

Quotes:

> Things that we did not think were relevant or possible 6 months ago are now common topics of conversation. So we're consistently looking at what's next, how is it evolving, and how can we make ourselves and our customers ready for that evolution.

### 🛡️ Govern which agents can transact

Companies need scalable, business-specific rules for deciding which automated traffic to allow. Blanket bot blocking can prevent useful commerce agents from reaching systems, while unwanted scraping and other costly or harmful activity may still need to be stopped. Participation may ultimately vary by hotel or business.

Quotes:

> How do we let in the things that our customers want to participate in, and how do we block a lot of the activity that could drive up our costs or could have adverse outcomes?

### 📚 Structure catalogs before commerce can scale

A major infrastructure gap is that much of the industry’s data is not cataloged in a structured way, limiting the ability of agents and other systems to conduct commerce reliably.

Quotes:

> A lot of the data out there is still not cataloged really well. So for e-commerce or commerce to really take off, you need to have a structured catalog.

# Changemaker: Travel Planners International & Vacation Planners (Sponsored by Travel Planners International & Vacation Planners)

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Charlotte Lamp Davies, Founder, A Bright Approach; Jenn Lee, President, Travel Planners International & Vacation Planners
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-19 11:00 AM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-19 11:10 AM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 781
**Session ID:** `9a4622fb-7286-4668-aca1-8b138f3d3917`

## Summary

The discussion opened around community presence, framing the franchise model as a deliberate way to claim and serve a local area, with Vacation Planners of Salt Lake City cited as an example of a travel agency rooted in its community. This was offered as a response to concerns that people and local businesses have become disconnected from each other.

On technology, the position was clearly not anti-tech: there was no interest in cutting out technology companies, and AI was described as an opportunity for advisors rather than a threat. The conversation framed the real challenge as balance — figuring out where technology helps versus where human interaction remains essential, noting that the industry's core job is simply to get people to leave their homes and have a valuable vacation experience that also satisfies suppliers.

A central proposal was for the industry to stop segmenting travelers into rigid categories — OTA customers versus those who want human advisors — and instead work together. The idea was that companies already holding strong consumer relationships, whether or not they are in the travel business, such as People Magazine or Southern Living, represent untapped opportunities for collaboration with travel advisors and technology platforms alike.

This connects to the importance of advisors being physically present and engaged within their local communities, serving the simple desire of people wanting to go on vacation. It was noted that despite unprecedented access to technology, demand for the human touch has not diminished, and that the advisory role itself is expected to grow substantially — with a figure cited from prior panels suggesting it could quadruple in the coming years.

The session closed on a forward-looking vision: bringing the worlds of technology and human advisory together to reach consumers who have the propensity to travel but have been stalled somewhere in the process, whether by over-reliance on technology without human follow-up or by a human interaction that failed to close the deal. The closing message was that whichever company figures out how to unify these approaches first will come out ahead.

## Key Takeaways

### 🧩 Match the right travelers with the right suppliers

A successful travel ecosystem should create value not only for the traveler but also by helping suppliers reach customers who are a good fit for their offerings.

Quotes:

> We want to make sure the suppliers are being fulfilled with the right customer.

### 📣 Trusted relationships outside travel can unlock new demand

Companies outside traditional travel may already have trusted relationships with people likely to travel. The opportunity is to connect those audiences with travel expertise instead of leaving the relationship fragmented.

Quotes:

> The company that gets this, the company that understands they already have a relationship with the next traveler, whether they're in the travel business or not.

### 🏘️ Local franchises can restore travel businesses in communities

A franchise model is presented as a way to establish recognizable local travel businesses and bring the travel relationship back into the community.

Quotes:

> Our franchise model is designed to claim an area for that reason. Vacation Planners of Salt Lake City. Oh, that's a community. That's my local travel agency.

### 🎯 Partnerships can recover travelers who did not book

A major collaboration opportunity is identifying people with a propensity to travel who did not purchase because a technology-only journey or a human interaction failed to close the sale.

Quotes:

> We start tapping in to consumers that we know are wanting to, that would have a propensity to go on vacation, but they've done something that has prevented them from buying.

### 🧑‍💼 Human advice is becoming more valuable alongside technology

Despite unprecedented access to technology, the speakers see growing demand for human interaction and expect the travel advisory role to expand significantly.

Quotes:

> We have more access to tech than we've ever had before, yet we seek the human touch.

### 🌍 The industry’s job is to deliver valuable vacations

The speakers define the industry’s core job as helping people leave home, take worthwhile vacations, and have experiences that work for both travelers and suppliers.

Quotes:

> We need humans to leave their homes, go on vacation. We need to make sure that that vacation was a great value for them.

### 🔄 Technology and human guidance should work as one journey

The session rejects an either-or choice between technology and people: AI and other tools can help advisors and consumers when digital convenience is combined with human guidance.

Quotes:

> You're not against technology, but, you know, it's kind of balancing, you know, where does technology help us? Surely AI is just an opportunity for the advisor.

### 📍 Advisors need an active community presence

Travel advisors are encouraged to engage directly where customers live, serving people locally while remaining connected to broader technology ecosystems.

Quotes:

> You need to be there as an advisor. You need to be in the community. We want to be in the community. We want to serve the community.

### 📚 Media and lifestyle brands can extend travel distribution

Brands that already engage audiences around lifestyle and culture, such as magazines, could collaborate with travel companies to connect their relationships with vacation planning.

Quotes:

> Think about People Magazine. Yeah. You know, Southern Living. They already have a relationship. Why are we not all talking?

### 🤝 Travel businesses should collaborate across models

OTAs, technology companies, suppliers, and travel advisors should work together instead of treating their customer segments and channels as separate worlds.

Quotes:

> Maybe we stop looking at it as the OTA does this and only serves this kind of client.

# Executive Interview: Where Travel's Power Is Shifting

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Frederic Lalonde, CEO & Co-Founder, Hopper Inc.
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-19 11:40 AM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-19 12:00 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 4452
**Session ID:** `abaa8e51-cbf9-4e33-984d-0ca4f87f5cf6`

## Summary

The conversation opened with Hopper's founder clarifying recent press coverage: he had not left the company and Hopper did not sell itself to Capital One, but rather licensed software and helped Capital One build its own travel team, with Capital One remaining a customer. He also confirmed that Dakota, who co-founded the B2B (HTS) business and worked there for 13 years, left to start his own venture after discussing the move for some time, and that the split was amicable.

On the broader trend of banks building in-house travel capabilities, it was explained that the three largest US banks sell roughly $30 billion in travel annually, growing 22% a year versus 4-6% for the wider category, a dynamic rooted in Amex's legacy loyalty-banking-travel expertise now spreading across Chase, Capital One, and internationally. Hopper's HTS network now connects to about 400 million credit card holders, moves nearly 2 million loyalty points a day, and operates in 23 countries, powering major banks in Australia and Japan, with the business expected to double next year; Capital One taught Hopper credit cards and loyalty while Hopper taught them travel.

On the consumer-facing Hopper app, it was described as far from abandoned: nearly 13 years old, still used by millions, maintaining steady app usage while its website grows 25% year over year, and still skewing toward young users who develop long-term brand loyalty. The app was credited as essential groundwork for building HTS, since operating at scale across many regulatory environments gave Hopper the operating history and fintech infrastructure needed to serve banking partners, even though it will never match Booking.com in scale.

Asked whether banks like Capital One or Chase could become the next big OTA, the response pivoted to a more provocative thesis: it is unclear whether marketplaces of any kind survive the shift to agentic AI. Citing Meta's push of a fully autonomous agent to billions of users, AI was framed as the first self-improving, exponentially advancing technology, unlike prior innovations such as the telephone, implying aggregators that merely compare and display data may become obsolete as agents do the shopping and booking directly. This logic extended to Google itself, said to be deliberately cannibalizing its own highly profitable search business by shifting toward generative answers despite a $600 billion market-cap hit, suggesting the wider industry will follow suit rapidly once 'fast' change kicks in, with civilization unrecognizable within a decade; meanwhile payment rails, fintech, protection products, and inventory were seen as more likely to endure than digital comparison surfaces, including the Hopper app itself, and the scale of recent AI-related IPOs was presented as evidence of how large and fast this shift already is.

On practical implications, it was suggested OTAs should focus on distributing their core inventory, payments, and commerce capabilities outward to whatever agentic platforms emerge, rather than defending the comparison layer, with Booking and Expedia cited as already moving this direction. Blocking AI agents, as in disputes like United and Chase restricting bot access, was compared to suing customers over Napster and seen as untenable, especially as over half of internet traffic is now non-human. Looking ahead, areas seen as resistant to AI disruption included human trust, meaningful relationships, and functions requiring accountability, even as AI is poised to take over high-error-rate domains like medicine; the session closed by endorsing the view that this is an unprecedented moment to build new companies, with more generational businesses expected to emerge in the coming years.

## Key Takeaways

### 🤖 AI agents could disintermediate travel marketplaces

Lalonde believes autonomous agents may handle travel planning, shopping and booking directly, weakening OTAs and other aggregators. He sees payment rails, fintech, protection products and supplier inventory as more durable than consumer-facing digital surfaces and comparison databases.

Quotes:

> In a world where these agents are learning how to do everything for us, it is unclear that you need an aggregator in the middle.

### 📱 Hopper’s consumer business remains active and profitable

Hopper has not abandoned its consumer business: millions still use the app, its website is growing 25% year over year, and the B2C operation remains profitable. The consumer product also serves as a testing and regulatory-learning ground for HTS.

Quotes:

> I don't think we could have built HTS without having—you know, like, literally tens of millions of users going through our Hopper stack. It's profitable.

### 🏦 Bank-led travel is rapidly expanding through Hopper’s loyalty network

Lalonde says bank-led travel, commerce and loyalty is growing about 22% annually—far faster than the broader travel market—and that Hopper’s B2B platform now operates in 23 countries, connects to about 400 million cardholders and moves nearly 2 million loyalty points per day.

Quotes:

> That segment, the bank-led travel, commerce, and loyalty, is growing 22% a year, where the general category is growing about 4 to 6%... Today we're connected to about 400 million credit card holders. We move almost 2 million points a day.

### 🚀 AI is creating a rare window for new companies

Lalonde believes the pace of AI change will create an unusually favorable period for startups and corporate innovators. He argues that the next five years could produce generational companies and that builders should act during this transition rather than wait for the market to stabilize.

Quotes:

> If you're a startup or you're building or you're innovating in your company, statistically, you will not have an opportunity like the next 5 years in your entire lifetime. This is when generational companies will be built.

### 🤝 Human trust and relationships may remain durable

As AI takes over more operational and professional tasks, Lalonde sees meaningful personal and business relationships as one area likely to retain value. He is less certain that creativity will remain exclusively human, but considers relationship-building a positive long-term investment.

Quotes:

> Where we are investing are human time, is in relationship building. And I will at least say, on a positive note, if you are building meaningful relationships with other people, personally or professionally, I think there's a future in that.

### 🔌 Make core travel commerce available to future channels

As distribution changes, Lalonde says travel companies should make their core value—inventory, payments and commerce—available to whatever interfaces or agents emerge, rather than depend on a particular consumer-facing marketplace.

Quotes:

> The one thing I know is the future is taking the core value of your product, the commerce, the payment, the inventory, and making it available for whatever's coming next.

### 📈 Self-improving AI could accelerate travel disruption

Lalonde distinguishes AI from earlier technologies because it improves as it operates, creating nonlinear change that could rapidly reshape travel distribution and other knowledge-based work.

Quotes:

> This is the first time as a species that we have built a technology that is learning... This is the first self-improving innovation that we have created as a species. And it's nonlinear. It's getting better at an exponential rate.

### 🧠 Travel software is shifting from databases to intelligence

Lalonde argues that AI is reducing the strategic importance of manually structured databases, APIs and fixed user interfaces. Companies increasingly need to convert disparate data into intelligence that agents can use.

Quotes:

> We are now all in the business of converting data into intelligence.

### 🔎 Generative AI is forcing Google to cannibalize search

Lalonde says generative answers threaten the blue-link search model that has captured most travel advertising profit. Because AI solves problems within the interface rather than sending users to other sites, Google may be sacrificing near-term search monetization to adapt to the new model.

Quotes:

> AI is not about sending the eyeballs of the user to another surface. It's about solving the problem in the place. And nobody knows how to monetize that, including Google.

### 🚧 Blocking customer agents is unlikely to work

Lalonde argues that travel companies cannot treat autonomous agents as hostile bots if those agents are acting on behalf of their customers. He compares blocking them with earlier attempts to fight changing distribution technologies and says the industry must instead determine how to serve agent-mediated demand securely.

Quotes:

> Blocking agents that are used by a billion people is like when Sony Music sued a housewife in Kentucky for using Napster. If you sue your customers, they're going to go buy your thing somewhere else.
