# Changemaker: Meta (Sponsored by Meta)

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Sanjay Bhatia, Head of Industry, Travel and Auto, Meta; Robert Cole, Senior Research Analyst, Lodging and Leisure Travel, Phocuswright Inc.
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-19 12:00 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-19 12:10 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 1052
**Session ID:** `9b1821d8-41fa-47ac-bbdb-841943281fa5`

## Summary

The session opened with an introduction from Meta's head of industry for travel and auto, framing travel as a $10 trillion market heading toward $15 trillion by 2030, but one still held back by legacy tech silos and fragmented booking channels, call centers, and static apps. The core argument was that Meta is positioning itself to lead a shift from these static channels to AI-enabled conversations, and that most travel brands already invest in chatbots, but legacy bots break down when handling real traveler complexities such as multi-leg changes, payment issues, and disruption triage.

A key statistic cited was that 84% of travelers who have used generative AI for travel-related tasks said it materially improved their experience, which was presented as the opportunity for brands to build a trusted, brand-governed agent. This was tied to a broader $5 trillion opportunity emerging from agentic commerce, as AI collapses the traditional travel funnel. The vision outlined was one where every business can cost-effectively offer customers a 24/7 digital concierge via an AI agent, while customers interface with a single AI agent for all their needs, moving toward autonomous agents managing bookings and service on customers' behalf across platforms where customers already spend their time.

Two specific Meta products were introduced to support this vision: Meta Business Agent, described as an always-on, customer-facing agent in a brand's own voice that turns trip inspiration into a repeatable booking flywheel; and Muse, a consumer-facing conversational agent that helps people find products and experiences across multiple brands. The expectation is that business agents and consumer agents like Muse will increasingly interact seamlessly, turning AI agents from simple deflection bots into true resolution agents.

An illustrative customer journey using a fictional travel provider, Zoomture, and a traveler named Hazel was used to show how these pieces connect: an Instagram ad sparks interest, Muse checks fit against schedule and budget, Zoomture's Business Agent provides pricing and availability, booking can happen directly or via Muse, confirmation and later itinerary changes are handled through WhatsApp, and a later holiday offer sent in the same WhatsApp thread restarts the cycle. The takeaways drawn from this example were that Zoomture stays present throughout the entire path, the relationship lives in one persistent conversation rather than fragmented touchpoints, and each step draws on a different part of Meta's portfolio (ads, utility messages, business agent, marketing messages), with a brand's own Business Agent able to offer personalization no third-party agent can replicate.

WhatsApp was positioned as the critical enabler of this persistent, low-friction relationship, cited as the most opened app on the planet, opened about 23 times a day on average, with 100 million Americans now using it monthly and that number still growing. A product demo video was shown to illustrate the experience in action. The session closed with three takeaways: every conversation is an opportunity to maximize customer lifetime value; AI will only work for enterprises if it drives measurable business outcomes without compromising customer experience, requiring trustworthy quality, safety by design, and consistent brand voice; and WhatsApp, combined with Meta Ads, Muse, and Meta Business Agent, offers a new path to drive impact across the full customer lifecycle while keeping brands in control end-to-end, before handing off to Robert Cole of Phocuswright.

## Key Takeaways

### 📱 WhatsApp is positioned as a scaled relationship channel

Meta argues that WhatsApp’s high engagement and conversational context make it a strong place for travel brands to build persistent one-to-one relationships, especially when combined with ads, utility messages and business agents.

Quotes:

> WhatsApp is one of the strongest places to build those one-on-one relationships at scale.

### 🤖 Traditional chatbots struggle with real travel complexity

Existing bots often fail when travelers need help with multi-leg itinerary changes, payment issues, questions or disruption triage—the situations where resolution matters most.

Quotes:

> Legacy bots break on real traveler complexities... Whether it's managing multi-leg changes, payment issues, questions, and disruption triage.

### 🎯 Enterprise AI must prove business value without compromising experience

For AI to work in large travel enterprises, it must produce measurable business outcomes while maintaining trusted quality, safety by design and a consistent brand voice.

Quotes:

> AI is only going to work for enterprises when it drives measurable business outcomes without compromising the customer experience.

### 🔄 Business and consumer agents may transact with each other

Meta distinguishes between brand-owned business agents and consumer agents such as Mews, envisioning a future where they interact seamlessly to find and purchase travel products and experiences.

Quotes:

> We envision a world in the not-so-distant future where business agents and consumer agents like Mews will be able to seamlessly interact.

### ✅ The goal is resolution, not deflection

The emerging standard for travel AI is expected to move beyond routing customers away from human support toward autonomous agents that can resolve needs on the traveler’s behalf.

Quotes:

> AI agents at that point, they go from being deflection bots to being resolution agents.

### 🧑‍💼 Business agents could provide 24/7 digital concierges

Meta envisions AI enabling every business to offer each customer a cost-effective, always-available digital concierge that supports engagement throughout the customer lifecycle.

Quotes:

> At Meta, we see a world where AI enables every business to cost-effectively give each of their customers a 24/7 digital concierge via an AI agent.

### 🧠 Brand-owned agents can deliver deeper personalization

Meta says a business agent can draw on a travel brand’s own data and expertise to provide personalization that a third-party consumer agent cannot replicate.

Quotes:

> A brand's Meta Business Agent can actually draw on its own data and expertise to offer a level of personalization that no third-party agent can replicate.

### ✨ Generative AI is already improving traveler experiences

The speaker cited research showing that 84% of travelers who have used generative AI for travel tasks say it materially improved their experience, raising expectations for brand messaging channels.

Quotes:

> 84% of travelers who have used generative AI for travel-related tasks say that it has materially improved their experience.

### 🔗 Persistent conversations can power the full travel lifecycle

Meta’s illustrative customer journey shows how one ongoing conversation can connect trip inspiration, pricing, booking, confirmations, changes, service and future marketing—reducing friction while keeping the brand relationship intact.

Quotes:

> The relationship lives in one persistent conversation. This is where we think the real value is derived.

### 🛡️ Brands can use governed agents to preserve trust and identity

Travel companies have an opportunity to build customer-facing agents they govern, ensuring conversations reflect the brand while strengthening traveler relationships.

Quotes:

> This is your opportunity to build a trusted agent that you govern. So every conversation reflects your brand.

# Executive Panel: Who Powers Travel When AI Becomes the Seller?

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Brett Burgess, President & CEO, ATPCO; Linda Fox, Executive Editor, PhocusWire; Niklas Andréen, Chief Commercial Officer of Airline Tech, Sabre Corporation; Steve Domin, CEO, Duffel
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-19 12:10 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-19 12:35 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 2161
**Session ID:** `c60d6725-c565-48d7-9635-09fc010777bc`

## Summary

The discussion opened with concrete brand examples of an AI agent (Muse) already in production use. Air France was cited as deploying it across the full customer lifecycle—boarding passes, flight updates, marketing, one-time password authentication, check-in reminders and loyalty—with marketing specifically driving 4.5x better results than business-as-usual email. Uber was highlighted for using the agent to re-engage drivers and riders, seeing a 27% improvement in rider re-engagement and a 16% improvement in driver re-engagement compared with SMS, illustrating that each brand tailors the tool to its own pain points.

On the consumer side, adoption numbers were shared: 10 million people in the US and Canada were using Muse within weeks of launch, a faster ramp than competitors achieved, with recent expansion into Japan, Mexico, Singapore and Australia. Personal use cases described included destination search, hotel recommendations, and proactive flight-disruption alerts tied to weather. The value was framed as compounding over time through two mechanisms: deep connections to email, loyalty accounts and credit cards that let the agent learn personal preferences (car type, family size, preferred airline and hotel), and persistent memory that lets it proactively surface things users struggle to track themselves, such as children's school communications.

On commercial strategy, the current focus was described as improving user experience and expanding geographically before monetization, guided by a stated principle that the agent should act as a fiduciary to the user rather than to brands. A subscription model with a generous free tier and two paid tiers was launched, but described as still early and being refined. Future commerce and advertising opportunities were acknowledged as under discussion at the parent company level, with travel and retail flagged as two of the leading consumer journeys where incremental value for brands is expected to be proven out over time.

The second part of the session turned to tension between airlines and AI booking agents, prompted by a reference to United's reported resistance. Panelists speculated that airlines' core concerns center on verifying the identity of the actual buyer behind an agent transaction, ensuring technical accuracy of agent responses, and avoiding scenarios where a hallucinating agent books something that doesn't exist—raising the question of who is accountable, the airline or the agent provider, when that happens. It was noted that this is still very early, only weeks into these agents' existence, and that control over the booking experience and shelf visibility remains the central issue for carriers.

Despite the United example, panelists stressed that most airlines are proactively reaching out wanting their content represented well in these new channels, viewing agent-driven discovery as an important emerging sales and advertising channel; one approach described using an MCP layer as a 'front door' to validate and control the relevance of incoming agent traffic. Closing predictions on the share of airline tickets agents will book in two years ranged from roughly 15-20% up to 50%, with one view holding steady due to continued direct sales and another predicting the direct/indirect distinction will blur entirely. On who stands to lose, the consensus was that it will be whoever fails to adapt business models and infrastructure to the new agent-driven volume, rather than any single category of player.

## Key Takeaways

### 🗺️ Consumers are using Muse for destination discovery and travel updates

Consumer use cases include searching for destinations, evaluating hotels and finding activities, as well as receiving weather-related flight updates and understanding their potential impact on a journey.

Quotes:

> I'm using it for destination search when I'm planning on traveling somewhere, understanding what hotels to look at, or understanding what to do in those places. And then I think flight updates.

### ✈️ Air France applies agents across the customer lifecycle

Air France uses its business agent for boarding passes, flight updates, marketing, one-time-password authentication, real-time updates, check-in reminders and loyalty. For marketing specifically, it reports a 4.5x result versus its business-as-usual benchmark, identified as email.

Quotes:

> Air France uses it really across the entire customer lifecycle. So we're talking like boarding passes, flight updates, marketing, one-time password authentication, real-time updates, check-in reminders, loyalty. ... They're seeing, for marketing specifically, that it's driving 4.5x results versus our BAU, which I believe is email in that case.

### 🧾 Travel AI complexity intensifies after booking

Unlike many retail journeys, travel’s hardest agentic tasks often come after the sale: cancellations, rebookings, payments and disruptions must be handled reliably so travelers can complete their journeys.

Quotes:

> The complexity in travel actually quite often starts when you finish the search. ... What we need to solve for ... is everything that happens after the booking. The rebookings, the cancellations, the payments part of it. God forbid disruption.

### 📊 AI agents could reshape airline distribution

Panelists estimated that AI agents could account for roughly 15% to 50% of airline bookings within two years. The shift may blur the line between direct and indirect distribution, while businesses that fail to adapt or provide value risk losing share.

Quotes:

> I'll say maybe 50% of bookings will come through an AI channel in some way through agents. ... Steve, final word? 15 to 20%, maybe. ... Whoever doesn't provide value. I think infrastructure providers will need to be there. ... The map is being redrawn, that's for sure.

### 💳 Muse is prioritizing user value before refining monetization

The team says it is focused first on improving the user experience and proving incremental value for brands, while testing a generous free tier and paid tiers before settling on a longer-term commerce model.

Quotes:

> We're actually really focused on how to improve the user experience right now. ... We launched a subscription model out of the gates. The free tier is very generous.

### ✅ Verified WhatsApp identities can strengthen brand trust

A verified WhatsApp account can help travelers distinguish an official hotel or travel brand agent from an untrusted or impersonated account.

Quotes:

> You guys know that's really the WhatsApp account for that hotel, and that's really the WhatsApp account for travel, which is a very interesting thing that other folks are struggling with.

### 🌍 Muse reached 10 million users and is expanding internationally

Muse reportedly reached 10 million users in the US and Canada within weeks of launch, while expanding to Japan, Mexico, Singapore and Australia.

Quotes:

> We announced in earnings that 10 million people in the US and Canada are using Muse. And that's within weeks after launch. ... In recent weeks, it launched in Japan, Mexico, Singapore, Australia.

### ⚠️ AI frequently misstates flight offer details

Current AI agents struggle to accurately explain what airfares and offers include, especially when rules, contracts and NDC details are involved; one study cited on the panel found only about 6% accuracy across tested origin-destination questions.

Quotes:

> If you ask multiple AI agents the same question about different O&Ds all over the world and then compare the accuracy of the responses to what was actually filed, AIs only get it right about 6% of the time.

### 🚗 Uber uses agents to re-engage both sides of its marketplace

Uber primarily uses the agent to bring riders and drivers back to the platform, with reported improvements in re-engagement versus SMS.

Quotes:

> They've seen 27% improvement in rider re-engagement, but a 16% improvement in driver re-engagement versus SMS.

### 🧠 Connected data and memory enable proactive personalization

Connecting email, loyalty accounts, credit cards and historical trip data helps Muse build a richer understanding of the traveler. Its memory can then support recommendations and proactive reminders based on preferences and personal context.

Quotes:

> I have it connected to my email, my loyalty accounts, credit card. ... It knows what kind of car I drive. It knows I have 3 kids. It knows what airline I fly typically, what hotel I stay at. ... And then the second piece is memory. It knows the things that I care about.

# Presentation + Executive Interview: From Search to Action

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** James Byers, Group Product Manager, Google; Mitra Sorrells, SVP, Content, Phocuswright
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-19 12:35 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-19 12:55 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 3861
**Session ID:** `78a1a9f5-4f5f-4fd8-8d8a-01334a1bea46`

## Summary

The session opened with a retrospective on how travel AI has evolved year by year: in 2023 it was novel but shaky and unreliable, by 2024 it was everywhere but still creaky and prone to hallucinations, in 2025 it became fluent with richer conversations and more accurate data, achieving habitual use at scale, and in 2026 it moved surprisingly quickly into being agentic. Rather than predicting the next label, the key question posed was when travel AI will be trusted the way travel search once was, with users confident about pricing, sourcing, and accuracy.

Google's current travel footprint was outlined as resting on massive-scale products—2.5 billion monthly active users on AI Overviews, billion-user products in AI Mode and the Gemini app, and Ask Maps now live in over 200 countries with new features like road trips. These sit atop the rapidly evolving Gemini model family and a data foundation combining web content, partner feeds for prices and availability, Google's Maps and Local corpora, and personalization signals from Gmail, Wallet, and Calendar. New AI Mode capabilities were highlighted, including end-to-end flight search with date and destination flexibility, bookable itineraries, price graphs and booking-timing guidance, points-and-miles integration across air and hotel, natural-language deal tracking, and a full hotel agentic booking flow from research through room selection to booking.

User behavior is shifting markedly in this AI-driven environment: queries in AI are three times longer on average than in Search, one in five queries is multimodal (voice or image), and users increasingly start in Search before moving into AI to dig deeper. Users now expect personalization as they share more context, but they also need to be convinced the AI got it right, which means trust still has to be earned.

Building that trust was framed around four principles: transparency about whether offers are commercial and how user data is used; privacy as a bedrock commitment even as expectations shift toward users willingly sharing more for better AI experiences; choice, meaning continuing to show every option, price, and booking path rather than steering to convenient answers; and simplicity, so partners only need one integration that works across all of Google's surfaces.

On the data and integration side, web and feed content remains foundational for upper- and mid-funnel discovery and comparison because it efficiently delivers accurate prices and availability. A newly announced Connected Apps platform offers a complementary path for deeper one-to-one relationships, loyalty, personalization, and brand voice, while the Universal Commerce Protocol extends into actions, checkout, and the post-booking relationship. Together with browser agents and account linking, these layers are meant to let Google and partners build trust collaboratively, with the ambition that today's uncertainty will look, in a few years, as distant as 2023 does now, as agentic travel AI capabilities are still only in their early stages.

## Key Takeaways

### 🧩 Use feeds, connected apps and protocols for different jobs

Google’s emerging model assigns different integration methods to different parts of the journey: feeds for discovery, comparison, price and availability; connected apps for loyalty, personalization and post-booking relationships; and connected apps or the Universal Commerce Protocol for checkout and other actions.

Quotes:

> Discovery and comparison is feeds... Loyalty and personalization, also powered by feeds, but also by connected apps... And actions and checkout, both connected apps and the Universal Commerce Protocol to go the last mile.

### 🧠 Travel AI needs structured, connected and attributable data

Incomplete or poorly connected airline data leads to inaccurate AI answers. Fare rules, ticket combinations and other travel datasets are not written in natural language, so they must be structured and linked in ways that let AI interpret them and reliably attribute its answers.

Quotes:

> A lot of the fare rules and all the intricacies of tickets... are not in machine-readable context today. And therefore, whatever conversational AI it is, is going to get stuck because it can't attribute them back together.

### 🔌 Access layers can hide airline-content complexity

Sabre’s strategy is to handle the complexity of NDC, EDIFACT, low-cost-carrier content, fare rules and combinations behind APIs or MCP, allowing travel companies and new entrants to focus on building user experiences rather than integrating every underlying format.

Quotes:

> What we've elected to do at Sabre is build an MCP platform on top of that where we take that complexity away and build the APIs.

### 🗣️ Travel queries are becoming longer, multimodal and personalized

As travelers move from traditional search into AI experiences, they provide more context through longer queries, voice and images, and expect answers tailored to their preferences and situation. Travel products should be prepared for richer inputs rather than relying only on short destination-and-date searches.

Quotes:

> Users are issuing much, much longer queries than they did on Search, 3 times the average query length in AI, and they're issuing them in new ways. 1 in 5 queries is multimodal, issued in voice or with an image.

### 📊 Extreme look-to-book ratios challenge travel economics

Conversational agents can generate dramatically more searches than traditional travel channels—potentially hundreds of thousands of looks for a booking versus roughly 1,000 for a classical travel agent. Every question still has to be answered by airline, hotel or car-rental systems, increasing infrastructure costs and supplier burden.

Quotes:

> We look at look-to-books at classical travel agents, it may be 1,000 looks before they do a booking. You look at some of the conversational AIs, they're in the hundreds of thousands.

### 🔔 Events are preferable to constant polling for changing travel data

Rather than having AI agents repeatedly ask whether prices or disruptions have changed, travel infrastructure can notify them when an update occurs, reducing unnecessary requests and system cost.

Quotes:

> Rather than asking us asking the airline every 15 minutes if the price has changed, how about if we tell you? How about if we tell you your Travelers are disrupted?

### 🤝 Trust requires transparency, privacy and meaningful choice

Travel AI must make commercial relationships, data use and recommendations transparent, protect user privacy and preserve meaningful choice. Users want confidence that the system considered relevant options, used appropriate data and can explain how it reached an answer.

Quotes:

> Users expect AI to do it for them, but they want to be convinced that AI did it right.

### ✈️ MCP and NDC are complementary distribution layers

The speakers do not expect airlines to abandon NDC, whose adoption is continuing. MCP can instead provide an access layer that brings multiple content sources together and may reduce the need for airline-specific direct NDC connectors, particularly for airlines that have not yet adopted NDC.

Quotes:

> I think they're complementary. But what I do think it replaces is this kind of fever that we had from some airlines at the time... bidding customer specific NDC connectors.

### 🛫 AI needs context about the travel product, not just the fare

Travel AI must answer experience questions that fare and schedule data do not capture, such as whether a flight has Starlink, how much legroom or seat width it offers, and whether it will be comfortable for a particular traveler. ATPCO is working to make airlines’ existing product and attribute data usable by AI through an airline context protocol.

Quotes:

> There is no fare that answers the question, is this going to be comfortable for me if I'm tall, right? Or I need extra legroom or I need extra seat width.

### ⚡ Predictive caching can shield suppliers from AI query volume

Conversational systems may generate far more searches than traditional travel channels, especially as agents repeatedly explore options. Travel platforms are therefore using predictive caches to answer language-model queries while reducing repeated calls to airlines and other suppliers; Sabre says its infrastructure handles about 20,000 transactions per second.

Quotes:

> A key part that's been super important at Sabre is getting a predictive cache in place... We do 20,000 transactions a second to be able to shield the suppliers, but providing all of the large language models with the answers they need.

# 'Till Next Year

**Event:** The Phocuswright Conference 2026
**Speakers:** Pete Comeau, Managing Director, Phocuswright
**Room:** Center Stage
**Start:** 2026-11-19 12:55 PM America/New_York
**End:** 2026-11-19 1:00 PM America/New_York
**Status:** completed
**Transcript word count:** 332
**Session ID:** `93d80e69-e428-4816-880b-d045805fc7ca`

## Summary

Reflecting on the hardest lesson of the past year, the Google guest observed that the pace of change in AI may be outstripping the travel ecosystem's ability to absorb it, pushing user behavior in surprising new directions — a disruption that, while unsettling, also creates opportunity. Both speakers agreed that the sense of things moving "too fast" is unlikely to go away anytime soon.

Asked how a hotel or OTA CEO should allocate next year's search and discovery budget, the response emphasized a portfolio approach grounded first in factual accuracy and depth of property content, since large language models now stitch together granular details — pillow quality, hallway noise, bar atmosphere — that traditional search never surfaced. The bulk of investment should go toward accurate pricing, availability, technical feed plumbing, and standout photos and human voice on owned websites, with a secondary allocation toward being "agentic-ready," and roughly 10% reserved for speculative, far-out projects.

On the value exchange between Google and travel partners as more research and decision-making happens inside AI-driven search, it was stressed that the underlying physics of travel — booking flights, staying under a roof, ending the journey with a partner — haven't changed, but partners need more context about why and how a user arrives from Google. Currently, no prior search or conversational context is passed to a hotel when a booking happens inside AI mode, though this was described as an evolving area that must always be built on user consent and control rather than silent data sharing, since real continuity of the journey between Google and partner experiences requires more transparency.

On trust, as ads scale within AI mode, the commitment was to keep labeling clear and consistent with existing search practices and to explain to users why a recommendation appeared — whether from a loyalty brand, a deal, or a sponsored placement — even though this takes up cognitive space and will require ongoing research into how well users actually understand these distinctions. Core search principles were reaffirmed as commercially neutral: ranking and surfacing of content are not influenced by commercial relationships, and that same neutrality must extend to how large language models compose trip plans, even as "ranking" takes a different form in an AI-generated answer.

On agentic booking and supplier control — raised in light of United's decision to block agents from shopping and booking on its site — suppliers were said to deserve choice in how they participate, though the lines get blurry in a browser-based agentic world versus an API-based one, especially when determining whether an agent or the user ultimately completes a purchase at a confirmation step; responsibility for that transaction has to be shared. Pushed on whether Google's shift to AI answers is cannibalizing its own search business, the reply framed the shift as simply following changing user needs and expectations rather than resisting them. Closing out, priorities for 2027 were summarized as getting it right for users, getting it right for partners, and doing so even faster than today. The session closed with organizational remarks thanking sponsors and attendees, noting conference statistics captured via SnapSite, and previewing upcoming events including the Travel Marketing AI Summit in New York and London, Phocuswright Europe in London, WIT events worldwide, a new event in Buenos Aires, and a return to Queenstown in 2028, alongside thanks to the Phocuswright team and parent company NorthStar/JTB.

## Key Takeaways

### 🇦🇷 Buenos Aires joins the event calendar

A new event in Buenos Aires is planned for next year, scheduled for the week before the main Phocuswright Conference.

Quotes:

> A new one next year in Buenos Aires, the week before the Phocuswright Conference.

### 🇪🇺 Phocuswright Europe will introduce a new format

Phocuswright Europe will return to London in May with both a new format and a renewed presence in the city.

Quotes:

> Phocuswright Europe will return to London in May with a new format and a new city in London.

### 👏 The closing recognizes the event team

The session concludes by thanking the Focusrite team and the wider group of people who supported the conference, emphasizing the collaborative effort behind the event.

Quotes:

### 📝 Feedback is part of the improvement process

Attendees are encouraged to share their conference experience through email or the event survey so the organizers can improve future editions.

Quotes:

### 🏔️ Queenstown is planned for 2028

The organization expects to return to Queenstown in 2028, signaling continued commitment to the destination and event format.

Quotes:

### 🏢 The conference is part of a broader global network

Phocuswright and Focusrite operate within the larger Northstar organization under JTB, which supports events around the world.

Quotes:

### 📅 Next year's conference dates are set

The next Phocuswright Conference is scheduled for November 16–18 at JW Marriott LA Live, giving attendees an early opportunity to plan for the following year.

WIT events will continue across multiple regions, including Africa, the Middle East, Europe, Japan, Malaysia and Singapore, in partnership with Phocuswright.

Quotes:

The Travel Marketing AI Summit is scheduled to return to New York City on March 1 and will also take place in London in September.

Quotes:

### 🔬 Research will be made publicly available

The research associated with the conference will be released broadly, allowing people to access it through the organization's website.

Quotes:
